# Rerail rule pack: Python, candidates

The ports in `rules/` here are still being evaluated: scored against the tools' expected findings and reported, never
gated and never shipped. This file is not published; the pack's [README](../README.md) covers the rules it ships.

## Rule details

What each rule matches beyond its one-line description: exact matching, edge cases, upstream settings and known divergences.

### `bandit-b104-hardcoded-bind-all-interfaces`

Matches any Python string constant whose decoded value is exactly `0.0.0.0`, regardless of where it is used.

### `bandit-b108-hardcoded-tmp-directory`

Matches string constants (and implicitly concatenated strings as a whole) whose decoded value starts with `/tmp`, `/var/tmp` or `/dev/shm`; strings that are bare expression statements (docstrings) are skipped.

### `bandit-b202-tarfile-unsafe-members`

Old description claimed: allow `filter="data"`, otherwise classify missing members, members supplied by a call, and other members expressions separately. The current clause does none of that — it flags every `extractall` on a value bound from `tarfile.open`.

### `bandit-b324-hashlib-insecure-functions`

hashlib `md4/md5/sha/sha1` and `hashlib.new("md4"|"md5"|"sha"|"sha1")` unless `usedforsecurity=False`; `crypt.crypt`/`crypt.mksalt` with `METHOD_CRYPT`, `METHOD_MD5`, `METHOD_BLOWFISH`, or with no salt/method at all.

### `bandit-b501-request-with-no-cert-validation`

Covers requests HTTP-verb calls (get, options, head, post, put, patch, delete) and httpx request/stream/Client/AsyncClient calls whose `verify` keyword is the literal False or the string 'False'. The callee root is read as Bandit's call qualname does: through the head's import alias, with relative imports named by their module text and the dots ignored, otherwise by the head's own text.

### `bandit-b506-yaml-load`

Port of Bandit 1.9.4's B506 yaml_load: `yaml.load` without `Loader`, or with a `Loader` (by keyword or second position) other than SafeLoader or CSafeLoader. Port of the kernel test b506_yaml_load_without_a_safe_loader. `# nosec` honoured.

### `bandit-b604-any-other-function-with-shell-equals-true`

A `shell=` keyword counts as truthy per Bandit's has_shell: anything but a falsy constant (False, None, a zero number, an empty string or bytes), an empty list or an empty dict. Calls to subprocess.Popen/call/check_call/check_output/run are excluded (covered by B602).

### `bandit-b613-trojansource`

Closest AST-only approximation of Bandit B613: only bidirectional-control characters inside parsed Python string literals are detected (not comments or other tokens).

### `ruff-async109-async-function-with-timeout`

Port of Ruff's ASYNC109: a positional-only, positional or keyword-only parameter named `timeout` of an `async def` not decorated with `override` from `typing`, `_typeshed` or `typing_extensions`, reported on the parameter. `asyncio.timeout` exists from 3.11, so below 3.11 a parameter is flagged only after an absolute import of `trio` or `anyio` has been seen (Ruff's seen_module, M10a `resolves.seenBefore`; async_function_with_timeout.rs: `module == AsyncModule::AsyncIo && target_version < PY311`).

### `ruff-async110-async-busy-wait`

Port of Ruff's ASYNC110 (async-busy-wait): a `while` loop whose body is a single statement `await <call>` where the callee resolves to `trio.sleep`/`sleep_until`, `anyio.sleep`/`sleep_until` or `asyncio.sleep`, reported on the loop. Names resolve as in Ruff's resolve_qualified_name, through the engine's `resolves` (M7).

### `ruff-async212-blocking-http-call-httpx-in-async-function`

Port of Ruff's ASYNC212 (blocking-http-call-httpx-in-async-function): a blocking method call on a name whose binding holds an `httpx.Client(...)` call or is annotated with `httpx.Client` (directly or in `|`/`Optional`/`Union`: Ruff's check_type through `resolves.binding`) inside an `async def` body whose function is the nearest enclosing function, lambda body or class, reported on the callee. Names resolve as in Ruff's resolve_qualified_name, through the engine's `resolves` (M7). The binding is Ruff's only_binding (`resolves.binding` with `only`: it shadows no earlier binding of the name in its scope).

### `ruff-async220-create-subprocess-in-async-function`

Port of Ruff's ASYNC220 (create-subprocess-in-async-function): a call to `subprocess.Popen`, `os.popen`, or an `os.spawn*` function whose mode (Ruff's find_argument_value("mode", 0): `arguments` in syntactic mode) is given and is not `os.P_WAIT` (directly, or through a name whose value, read as find_assigned_value's lookup_symbol does (`resolves.binding` with `stopAtGlobal`), is `os.P_WAIT`) inside an `async def` body whose function is the nearest enclosing function, lambda body or class, reported on the callee. Names resolve as in Ruff's resolve_qualified_name, through the engine's `resolves` (M7).

### `ruff-async240-blocking-path-method-in-async-function`

Port of Ruff's ASYNC240 (blocking-path-method-in-async-function): a call to an `os.path` function, or a method on a `pathlib` path (a `Path(...)`-family call, or a name whose binding holds such a call or is annotated with a path class, directly or in `|`/`Optional`/`Union`: Ruff's check_type through `resolves.binding`), except Ruff's list of pure methods, inside an `async def` body whose function is the nearest enclosing function, lambda body or class, reported on the callee. Names resolve as in Ruff's resolve_qualified_name, through the engine's `resolves` (M7). A bare name imported from `os.path` resolves through `prefix`. The binding is Ruff's only_binding (`resolves.binding` with `only`: it shadows no earlier binding of the name in its scope).

### `ruff-b006-mutable-argument-default`

Port of Ruff's B006 (mutable-argument-default), stable behaviour: a function parameter default that is a list, dict or set display or comprehension, or a call of `dict`/`list`/`set` or `collections.Counter`/`OrderedDict`/`defaultdict`/`deque` (a subscripted callee judged by its value; parentheses transparent), unless the parameter's annotation is immutable by Ruff's `is_immutable_annotation`: an immutable builtin, `typing` or `collections.abc` type (generic ones also subscripted), `None`, `X | Y`, `Union[...]` of immutables, `Optional[X]`, `Annotated[X, ...]`. Names resolve with `resolves` (imports, their scope and order, and unshadowed builtins, as Ruff's semantic model sees them); `builtins.X` counts only where Ruff's table lists it. Stub files are skipped. Lambdas are not checked (Ruff runs B006 on `def` only). Reported on the default. Ruff's option `flake8-bugbear.extend-immutable-calls` adds immutable annotations.

### `ruff-b008-function-call-in-default-argument`

Port of Ruff's B008 (function-call-in-default-argument): every call inside a parameter default (of a `def` or a `lambda`), not looking inside lambdas, unless the parameter's annotation is immutable (Ruff's `is_immutable_annotation`, as in B006) or the callee is a known mutable constructor (`dict`/`list`/`set`, `collections.Counter`/`OrderedDict`/`defaultdict`/`deque`) or immutable-returning callable (`datetime` types, `decimal.Decimal`, `fractions.Fraction`, `operator` getters, `pathlib.Path`, `types.MappingProxyType`, `re.compile`, immutable builtins; a subscripted callee judged by its value). Names resolve with `resolves` (imports, their scope and order, and unshadowed builtins, as Ruff's semantic model sees them); `builtins.X` counts only where Ruff's table lists it. A bare-name callee whose binding (`resolves.binding`) is `X = NewType(name, <immutable annotation>)` (Ruff's `is_immutable_newtype_call`: a plain assignment, the type as `tp=` or the second positional argument) is exempt where that binding shadows no earlier binding of the name in its scope (Ruff's only_binding: `resolves.binding` with `only`) and is not an unpacking. Reported on each call. Ruff's option `flake8-bugbear.extend-immutable-calls` adds immutable calls and annotations.

### `ruff-b012-jump-statement-in-finally`

Port of Ruff's B012 (jump-statement-in-finally): a `return`, `break` or `continue` statement in a `finally` block, reached through the bodies of nested `if` (not `elif`/`else`), `try` (not handlers, `else` or `finally`), `with` and `match` cases; `return` also through `while`/`for` bodies (not their `else`), where `break`/`continue` stop. Function and class bodies stop the walk. Reported on the statement.

### `ruff-b017-assert-raises-exception`

Port of Ruff's B017 (assert-raises-exception): `<x>.assertRaises(...)` or `pytest.raises(...)` whose exception (first positional argument, or `exception=` / `expected_exception=`) resolves to the builtin `Exception` or `BaseException` (the unshadowed name, or through an import of `builtins`; parentheses transparent). `pytest.raises` is exempt with `match=` or a string/bytes literal second positional argument. Reported on a `with` item that has no `as` target, and on any such call with at least two positional arguments or a `func=` keyword (the call form). `pytest.raises` and the exception names resolve with `resolves` (imports, their scope and order, and shadowing, as Ruff's semantic model sees them).

### `ruff-b018-useless-expression`

Port of Ruff's B018 (useless-expression): an expression statement (parentheses transparent; a bare tuple counts) that is not a comparison, a string or f-string literal, or `...`, and has no effect in Ruff's `contains_effect` sense: no call other than an argument-less `set()`/`list()`/`tuple()`/`dict()`/`frozenset()`, no `await`, `yield`, walrus, comprehension, generator or subscript, and no binary operation with an operand that is not a literal or display, anywhere inside (lambda bodies included). An attribute access with an effect is reported as a useless attribute access. The builtin names must resolve to the unshadowed builtins (`resolves`, Ruff's `has_builtin_binding`). Reported on the expression.

Back to candidates in the stable-rules port (NER-1854): on Ruff's `ISC.py` (line 201) the port reports a parenthesised t-string concatenation that Ruff 0.16.9 leaves alone.

### `ruff-b019-cached-instance-method`

Port of Ruff's B019 (cached-instance-method): a decorator `functools.cache` / `functools.lru_cache` (called or not) on an instance method: a function defined directly in a class body, not decorated with `staticmethod`/`classmethod` (or `abc.abstractstaticmethod`/`abc.abstractclassmethod`), not `__new__`, `__init_subclass__` or `__class_getitem__`, in a class that does not list an `enum` base (`Enum`, `Flag`, `IntEnum`, `IntFlag`, `StrEnum`, `ReprEnum`, `CheckEnum`). Names resolve with `resolves` (imports, their scope and order, and shadowing, as Ruff's semantic model sees them); a base naming a class defined in the file is followed to that class's bases (`resolves.binding` on its definition, cycles fail). Reported on the decorator.

### `ruff-b024-abstract-base-class-without-abstract-method`

Port of Ruff's B024 (abstract-base-class-without-abstract-method): a class with exactly one base or keyword, which is `abc.ABC` as a base or `abc.ABCMeta` as `metaclass=`, whose body has no method decorated with an `abc` abstract decorator and no annotated attribute without a value. Names resolve with `resolves` (imports and shadowing as Ruff's semantic model sees them). Not in stubs. Reported on the class name.

### `ruff-b027-empty-method-without-abstract-decorator`

Port of Ruff's B027 (empty-method-without-abstract-decorator): in a class whose only base or keyword is `abc.ABC` or `metaclass=abc.ABCMeta`, a method directly in the class body whose body is only `pass`, string literals and `...`, with no `abc` abstract decorator and no `typing.overload` / `typing_extensions.overload` decorator. Names resolve with `resolves` (imports and shadowing as Ruff's semantic model sees them). Not in stubs. Reported on the whole definition, decorators included.

### `ruff-b031-reuse-of-groupby-generator`

Partial port of Ruff's B031 (reuse-of-groupby-generator): in the body of `for k, g in itertools.groupby(...)`, a use of `g` inside a nested loop body or list/set/dict comprehension, or after an earlier statement (or the enclosing `if` test or `match` subject) that uses it. Ruff's per-branch use counting (maximum over if/match branches), resets at break/continue/return and reassignment of `g` are not modelled.

### `ruff-b035-static-key-dict-comprehension`

Port of Ruff's B035 (static-key-dict-comprehension): the key of a dict comprehension is constant: a literal, a name the comprehension's generators never store (targets and walrus targets, nested ones included), or an attribute, subscript, operator or tuple built only from such parts. Reported on the key, inside any parentheses.

Back to candidates in the stable-rules port (NER-1854): on Ruff's `C420.py` (line 96) the port reports a dict comprehension split across lines by comments at a position Ruff 0.16.9 does not.

### `ruff-b911-batched-without-explicit-strict`

Port of Ruff's B911 (batched-without-explicit-strict), from target version 3.13 (`when`; `batched_without_explicit_strict.rs:72`): a call to `itertools.batched` with no `strict=` keyword (find_keyword, through `arguments` in syntactic mode) whose first positional argument exists, is not `*` unpacking and is not an infinite iterator. The callee resolves with `resolves` (imports, their scope and order as Ruff's semantic model sees them). Reported on the call.

### `ruff-b912-map-without-explicit-strict`

Port of Ruff's B912 (map-without-explicit-strict), from target version 3.14 (`when`; `expression.rs:797`): a call to builtin `map` (bare `map`, or `map` from `builtins`) with no `strict=` keyword (find_keyword, through `arguments` in syntactic mode), at least three positional arguments (a function and two iterables) or a `*` argument, and no infinite iterator among the arguments after the first. `map` and the itertools names resolve with `resolves`, so a shadowed `map` is not reported. Reported on the call.

### `ruff-ble001-blind-except`

Port of Ruff's BLE001 (blind-except): the first builtin `Exception` or `BaseException` (`resolve_builtin_symbol`: the unshadowed name or `builtins.`) in an `except` clause's type, searching nested tuples, reported on it, unless the handler body (not entering nested `try`, `def` or `class`) re-raises (`raise`, `raise e`, `raise ... from e` with the handler's name) or logs the exception: an expression-statement call of `exception`, or of another logger method with `exc_info` not known falsy, on a logger candidate (a value resolving to `logging` or `flask.current_app.logger`, an inline `logging.getLogger(...)`/`Logger(...)`, or a name whose last part starts with `log`/`LOG` or ends with `logger`/`logging` and that has no qualified name: `resolves.import: true`, Ruff's `resolve_qualified_name(..).is_some()`), or a name resolving to `logging.<method>`.

### `ruff-e402-module-import-not-at-top-of-file`

Port of Ruff's E402 (module-import-not-at-top-of-file): an `import` or `from ... import` statement directly at module level after an import boundary, reported on the statement. A boundary is any earlier module-level statement other than an import, the module docstring (a plain string literal as the first statement), a single-target assignment to a dunder name, and the allowed path/environment setup calls (`sys.path` mutation, `os.environ` edits, `os.putenv`/`unsetenv`, `matplotlib.use`, `pytest.importorskip`, `site.addsitedir`); `if`, `try`, `with` and `match` blocks and everything inside them are never boundaries. Qualified names are resolved through the file's imports as Ruff resolves them at that point of the module.

### `ruff-e712-true-false-comparison`

Port of Ruff's E712 (true-false-comparison) with default settings: `True` or `False` compared with `==` or `!=`, reported on the whole comparison. Operands are checked as for E711; `True == True` (same literal on both sides of `==`) is not reported.

### `ruff-e713-not-in-test`

Port of Ruff's E713 (not-in-test): `not` applied to a comparison whose only operator is `in` (the comparison may be parenthesized), reported on the comparison inside any parentheses; also inside a string Ruff parses as a type definition (M11a `parse` with `annotation`, nested strings included), reported on each such comparison inside the string.

### `ruff-e714-not-is-test`

Port of Ruff's E714 (not-is-test): `not` applied to a comparison whose only operator is `is` (the comparison may be parenthesized), reported on the comparison inside any parentheses; also inside a string Ruff parses as a type definition (M11a `parse` with `annotation`, nested strings included), reported on each such comparison inside the string.

### `ruff-e731-lambda-assignment`

Port of Ruff's E731 (lambda-assignment): a plain or annotated assignment statement with a single name target whose value is a `lambda` (parentheses are transparent), reported on the statement. Chained assignments are skipped, as are annotated assignments whose enclosing scope is a class body.

### `ruff-e741-ambiguous-variable-name`

Port of Ruff's E741 (ambiguous-variable-name): a name `l`, `I` or `O` bound as a variable: assignment, augmented-assignment, annotated, `for`/comprehension, `with ... as`, `except ... as` and walrus targets (through tuple, list and starred unpacking), `global`/`nonlocal` names, function and lambda parameters, and `type` alias names. Not match-pattern captures, imports, type parameters or `del`. Stub files (`.pyi`) are skipped.

### `ruff-f401-unused-import`

Port of Ruff's F401 (unused-import) on the engine's usedness (M10a `binds`): the latest binding of a name made by `import` or `from ... import` (not `__future__`, relative ones included as Ruff ignores their level; not `*`) in a module or function scope that nothing reads, not an explicit re-export (`x as x`, a name a static `__all__` lists), not under `global`, not an import of a module Ruff's option `pyflakes.allowed-unused-imports` lists (or of its submodules or members). Reads through a submodule's alias (`import pyarrow.csv` read as `pa.csv`) and `__all__` entries count. Reported on the unaliased import item's dotted name or on the alias. An `__all__` that may list names only at run time (a call, conditional or comprehension) leaves usedness unknown; names in strings Ruff parses as type definitions are reads (M11a).

### `ruff-f404-late-future-import`

Port of Ruff's F404 (late-future-import): a `from __future__ import` (also relative, by the module's text with `resolves.imported` and `relative: "text"`, as Ruff ignores the level) after the futures boundary, reported on the statement. The boundary is closed by any statement other than a `__future__` import or a string-literal module docstring in first position (ruff_python_parser semantic_errors.rs `visit_stmt`); a compound statement closes it before its body is visited, so a nested `__future__` import is always late.

### `ruff-f631-assert-tuple`

Port of Ruff's F631 (assert-tuple) with default settings: an `assert` whose test (not its message) is a non-empty tuple, parenthesized any number of times. Reported on the whole statement.

### `ruff-f634-if-tuple`

Port of Ruff's F634 (if-tuple) with default settings: the test of an `if` or `elif` branch (not a conditional expression, not `while`) that is a non-empty tuple, reported on the tuple (inside any extra parentheses).

### `ruff-f722-forward-annotation-syntax-error`

Port of Ruff's F722 (forward-annotation-syntax-error) with default settings: a string Ruff parses as a type definition (a string annotation, a `type` value, a `cast`/`TypeVar` argument, a typing generic's `"..."` slice), read with the `annotation` grammar. Three checks: a string Ruff's parser refuses (the parse is one `ERROR` node), reported on the string; each semantic syntax error Ruff's checker reports in a string it parsed (`await` or `yield` outside a function, a walrus rebinding a comprehension variable, duplicate arguments, `__debug__` stored, a name used before its `global`, ...: an `ERROR` node after the expression), reported at the offending node; and Ruff's second error on an `await` evaluated outside any function ("`await` statement outside of a function": the string's host is not in a function body with no class between them, and the `await` is not in a lambda's body or a generator expression's element, conditions or later iterables), at the `await`. Strings nested in one (`List['a b']` inside a string annotation) are read too. Not inside a function decorated with a `no_type_check` that resolves to `typing`, `_typeshed` or `typing_extensions` (Ruff's match_typing_expr): its parameters, defaults, return annotation and body. Ported from the Ruff conformance suite (rerail/conformance/ruff/rules/F722.json at 3f102adcb), with Ruff's `# noqa` honoured as the Ruff CLI honours it. Divergence: a string after the first element of a subscript whose generic resolves to a jaxtyping import (`Float[Array, "b n"]`, `jaxtyping.Float[...]`, `jt.Shaped[...]`) is a jaxtyping shape, which jaxtyping parses with its own grammar, not a forward annotation, so it is not reported. Ruff 0.16.9 reports it. A shape inside a string annotation (`"Float[Array, 'b n']"`) is still reported.

Back to candidates in the stable-rules port (NER-1854): on the probe `rerail/probes/UP037-typeddict-keyword-fields.py` (lines 8 and 9) Ruff 0.16.9 reports the string values of `TypedDict("E", foo="a b")` keyword fields, which the port misses.

### `ruff-f811-redefined-while-unused`

Port of Ruff's F811 (redefined-while-unused) with default settings, on the engine's shadowed-binding pairs (M10a `binds.redefines`): a binding (not a loop variable) whose shadowed binding is unused, on the same fork, where Ruff's Binding::redefines allows the pair (an import redefines an import, a definition or an assignment; a submodule import only the same qualified name, `sameImport`; an assignment only an import or a definition; any other binding an import, a definition or an assignment). Same scope: a dummy name is exempt unless the shadowed binding is an import, and a `def` decorated with an `overload` that resolves to `typing`, `_typeshed` or `typing_extensions` is never redefined. Across scopes: only a shadowed import that is no explicit re-export, and not from a class body. Reported on the redefining name. Ported from the Ruff conformance suite (rerail/conformance/ruff/rules/F811.json at 3f102adcb), with Ruff's `# noqa` honoured as the Ruff CLI honours it.

### `ruff-f821-undefined-name`

Port of Ruff's F821 (undefined-name), on the engine's name resolution (M10a `resolves.unresolved`): a reference with no binding in scope (or one an unconditional `del` unbound), no builtin of the target version or of Ruff's option `builtins` and no visible star import, outside a `try` body whose handler catches the builtin `NameError`; and such a name inside a string Ruff parses as a type definition (M11a `parse` with `annotation`: string annotations, `cast`/`TypeVar` arguments, typing generics' string slices, nested strings), reported on each such name inside the string. Ported from the Ruff conformance suite (rerail/conformance/ruff/rules/F821.json at 3f102adcb), with Ruff's `# noqa` honoured as the Ruff CLI honours it.

### `ruff-f841-unused-variable`

Port of Ruff's F841 (unused-variable), on the engine's usedness (M10a `binds`): the latest binding of a name in a function or lambda scope made by `=` (plain or annotated), `+=`, `:=`, `with ... as name`, a match capture or a `type` alias (Ruff's Assignment, NamedExprAssignment and WithItemVar) that nothing reads, not under `global`/`nonlocal`, not an unpacked assignment (Ruff's is_unpacking_assignment: a name in a tuple-like target whose value is no display, or in a chain with a non-tuple target, a walrus in the value of an all-tuple unpacking, a `with` tuple target), not a dummy (Ruff's option `dummy-variable-rgx`) or traceback name; and any `except ... as name` nothing reads, not a dummy name. A `locals()` call in the function leaves its assignments' usedness unknown (Ruff's USES_LOCALS; not the `except` names'). Names in strings Ruff parses as type definitions are reads (M11a). Ported from the Ruff conformance suite (rerail/conformance/ruff/rules/F841.json at 3f102adcb), with Ruff's `# noqa` honoured as the Ruff CLI honours it.

### `ruff-n999-invalid-module-name`

Port of Ruff's N999 (invalid-module-name): a `.py` or `.pyi` file in a package (the host sent its module path) whose name is not a valid module name (`is_module_name`: `[a-z_][a-z0-9_]*`, not a keyword), reported at the start of the file. The name is the file's stem (`moduleName`), or for an `__init__` file the package root's directory (`package`: Ruff reads `package.path()`, the top package, not the file's own directory), as on disk. In a `versions` or `migrations` directory a leading digit is allowed (`is_migration_name`). Names matching `pep8-naming.ignore-names` (Ruff's option, with its defaults setUp, tearDown, ...) are exempt.

### `ruff-pie790-unnecessary-placeholder`

Port of Ruff's PIE790 (unnecessary-placeholder): a `pass` statement, or an expression statement that is only `...`, in a body (module, class, function or compound-statement block) of two or more statements. An `...` is kept in a `TYPE_CHECKING` block, a `typing.Protocol` class or an `abc.abstractmethod`-decorated function, and there Ruff stops scanning the body, so a later `pass` is kept too.

### `ruff-pie808-unnecessary-range-start`

Port of Ruff's PIE808 (unnecessary-range-start): `range(0, stop)` (an int literal zero, any spelling) with exactly two positional arguments and no keywords, where `range` resolves to the builtin (`match_builtin_expr`). Reported on the `0`.

### `ruff-ret503-implicit-return`

Port of Ruff's RET503 (implicit-return): a function that has a `return` with a value other than `None` and whose last statement can fall through (Ruff's has_implicit_return: recursively the last statement of each `if`/`elif`/`else`, `for`/`while` `else`, `match` case and `with` body; an `if` without `else` and a loop without `else` fall through; `while True`, `assert False`, `return`, `raise`, `try` and calls of known no-return functions do not). No-return functions are `exit`/`quit` (builtin, `sys`, `_thread`, `pytest`), `os`/`posix` `_exit`/`abort`, `_winapi.ExitProcess`, `pytest.fail`/`skip`/`xfail`, `assert_never` from `typing`/`_typeshed`/`typing_extensions`, and functions defined before the analysed function with a `NoReturn`/`Never` return annotation; the callee names are resolved through the file's bindings and imports. Reported on the function, from its first decorator.

### `ruff-ret504-unnecessary-assign`

Port of Ruff's RET504 (unnecessary-assign): `return x` whose preceding statement (comments aside) is `x = value` with a single name target, or a `with` block (not using `contextlib.suppress`) whose last statement is such an assignment, reported on the returned name. Skipped when `x` is declared `global`/`nonlocal` or bare-annotated in the function, referenced in a `finally` enclosing the return (a read or `del` resolving to a binding in the function's scope), or when the function scope's latest binding of `x` has a reference from another scope (`binds` with `latest` and `nestedUse`, which inherits the references of the bindings it shadows); generators and functions whose body is a lone `return` are skipped.

### `ruff-sim105-suppressible-exception`

Port of Ruff's SIM105 (suppressible-exception) at target 3.14: a `try` with one simple statement (expression, assignment, `del`, `assert`, import or `pass`), exactly one `except` handler whose body is only `pass` or `...`, no `else` or `finally`, and whose handled types are all names or attribute chains (or none). Reported on the `try` statement.

### `ruff-sim108-if-else-block-instead-of-if-exp`

Partial port of Ruff's SIM108 (if-else-block-instead-of-if-exp): an `if`/`else` whose two bodies are each one plain assignment `N = value` to the same name (no yield/await value), unless it is a `sys.version_info`/`sys.platform` or `TYPE_CHECKING` block. Missing: Ruff reports only when the generated ternary fits the line length.

### `ruff-sim110-reimplemented-builtin`

Partial port of Ruff's SIM110 (reimplemented-builtin): in a function scope, a `for` loop whose body is one `if` (no `elif`/`else`) returning a boolean literal, followed by a `return` of the opposite literal in the loop's `else` or as the next statement; the loop target and the `if` test contain no `await`/`yield`. Missing: Ruff reports only when the generated `return any(...)`/`all(...)` fits the line length.

### `ruff-sim114-if-with-same-arms`

Port of Ruff's SIM114 (if-with-same-arms): two consecutive `if`/`elif` branches whose bodies are equal as Ruff's ComparableStmt (`text: "structure"`; names without NFKC normalization) and whose body ranges hold the same comment texts in the same order, reported at the first branch. A branch's body range runs from the end of its test's line to the end of its last statement's line (M12a `layout`): a comment on the test's line or after the last statement's line is left out. Up to 3 comments per branch are compared; a branch with more is not reported.

### `ruff-sim115-open-file-with-context-handler`

Port of Ruff's SIM115 (open-file-with-context-handler): a call of a file-opening function (builtin `open`, `io.open`, `codecs.open`, `tempfile.TemporaryFile`, `tarfile.open`, `dbm.*.open`, ... from Ruff's table) or of `open` on a `pathlib.Path`/`zipfile.ZipFile`/`lzma.LZMAFile`/`tarfile.TarFile` instance created in place, outside a `with` or `return` statement, not immediately `.close()`d, not passed to `enter_context` under `with contextlib.ExitStack()` (or the async variant) or to unittest's `enterContext`, and not directly in `__enter__`. Names resolve through the file's imports and the builtin scope (`resolve_qualified_name`). Reported on the callee.

### `ruff-sim116-if-else-block-instead-of-dict-lookup`

Port of Ruff's SIM116 (if-else-block-instead-of-dict-lookup): an `if` whose test and every `elif` test is `N == <literal>` (one `==`, the same name `N`, a string/bytes/number/boolean/None/ellipsis literal), each branch (and any `else`) being one `return` whose value has no side effect (Ruff's `contains_effect`), with at least three distinct literals (Ruff's ComparableLiteral, `text: "structure-parts"`: type and value, `1 == 0x1`, `'a' == "a"`, not `1 == 1.0`; an implicit concatenation part by part), unless `N` resolves to `sys.version_info`/`sys.platform`.

### `ruff-sim201-negate-equal-op`

Port of Ruff's SIM201 (negate-equal-op): `not` applied to a comparison (through any parentheses) whose only operator is `==`, reported on the `not` expression. Exempt, as Ruff: when the enclosing statement is an `if` whose body is a single `raise` (Ruff's `is_exception_check` reads the `if` statement's own body, so an `elif` test counts with it), and when the nearest enclosing scope is a function named `__eq__`, `__ne__`, `__lt__`, `__le__`, `__gt__` or `__ge__` (lambdas, comprehensions and nested functions are their own scopes).

### `ruff-sim202-negate-not-equal-op`

Port of Ruff's SIM202 (negate-not-equal-op): `not` applied to a comparison (through any parentheses) whose only operator is `!=`, reported on the `not` expression. Exempt, as Ruff: when the enclosing statement is an `if` whose body is a single `raise` (Ruff's `is_exception_check` reads the `if` statement's own body, so an `elif` test counts with it), and when the nearest enclosing scope is a function named `__eq__`, `__ne__`, `__lt__`, `__le__`, `__gt__` or `__ge__` (lambdas, comprehensions and nested functions are their own scopes).

### `ruff-sim220-expr-and-not-expr`

Port of Ruff's SIM220 (expr-and-not-expr): an `and` expression (one run of the operator; parentheses start a new one) with a value `not A` and a value `A`, the same plain name (through parentheses), and no side effect anywhere in it (Ruff's `contains_effect`). Reported on the whole `and` expression, once per such pair.

### `ruff-sim221-expr-or-not-expr`

Port of Ruff's SIM221 (expr-or-not-expr): an `or` expression (one run of the operator; parentheses start a new one) with a value `not A` and a value `A`, the same plain name (through parentheses), and no side effect anywhere in it (Ruff's `contains_effect`). Reported on the whole `or` expression, once per such pair.

### `ruff-sim401-if-else-block-instead-of-dict-get`

Port of Ruff's SIM401 (if-else-block-instead-of-dict-get): `if K in D: V = D[K] else: V = DEFAULT` (or the `not in` form with the arms swapped), and the conditional-expression form, where the default has no side effect (`contains_effect`). Operands are compared by source text, where Ruff compares expressions (`ComparableExpr`). The statement form needs `D` to be a name whose binding is a dict (`is_dict` through `resolves.binding` with `only`: Ruff's is_known_to_be_of_type_dict, whose only_binding asks that the binding shadows no earlier one). Partial: Ruff also asks that the suggested `V = D.get(K, DEFAULT)` fits the line length.

### `ruff-sim911-zip-dict-keys-and-values`

Port of Ruff's SIM911 (zip-dict-keys-and-values): `zip(d.keys(), d.values())`, the same plain name on both calls, no arguments to either, no keyword but `strict`, and `zip` the builtin (or `builtins.zip`). Reported on the `zip` call, where `d`'s binding is a dict (Ruff's `is_dict` through `resolves.binding`: a `def`'s `**kwargs`, a dict-typed value or `dict(...)`, a `dict`/`Dict` annotation or return annotation; unpacking carries no value).

### `ruff-w605-invalid-escape-sequence`

Port of Ruff's W605 (invalid-escape-sequence): a backslash in a non-raw string, bytes, f-string or t-string literal part that does not start a valid escape (newline, backslash, quotes, `a b f n r t v`, octal digits `0`-`7`, `x`, `N`, `u`, `U`), one finding per backslash. tree-sitter-python leaves such a backslash as a bare `\` token inside `string_content` (valid escapes are `escape_sequence` nodes); f-string format specifiers keep their text unsplit, so a regex over the specifier walks its escapes and `each` reports every invalid backslash (a specifier with none binds no group and reports nothing).

Back to candidates in the stable-rules port (NER-1854): in the probe `rerail/probes/PYI054-escaped-or-concatenated-string-annotation.pyi` (line 4) the port reports a `\q` inside a string annotation, which Ruff 0.16.9 does not.

### `ruff-a005-stdlib-module-shadowing`

Port of Ruff's A005 (stdlib-module-shadowing): a `.py` file whose module, or a package's `__init__.py` whose package, is named like a standard-library module of the target version (`is_known_standard_library`, one `when`-gated check per range of versions; `_private` names are allowed), reported at the start of the file. Its options are Ruff's, with Ruff's defaults: without `flake8-builtins.strict-checking` the scope takes only top-level modules (files directly under the project root or a `src` root, and `x/__init__.py` there, never a root's own `__init__.py`); `flake8-builtins.allowed-modules` leaves files and packages so named out of scope.

### `ruff-inp001-implicit-namespace-package`

Port of Ruff's INP001 (implicit-namespace-package): a `.py` file outside any package (the host sent no module path), reported at byte 0 (`file.start`) as Ruff reports it. Files directly under the project root or `src/` (Ruff's default `src`), files whose first comment starts at byte 0 with `#!` (a shebang: M12a `layout` `start`) and PEP 723 scripts (a `# /// script` comment block closed by `# ///`) are exempt.

### `ruff-air001-airflow-variable-name-task-id-mismatch`

Port of Ruff's AIR001 (airflow-variable-name-task-id-mismatch): reports a single-target assignment to a plain name whose value is a call of a callee that resolves through imports to `airflow.operators.*` or to `airflow.providers.**.operators.*` (with a segment after `operators`), when the call has a `task_id` keyword whose value is a string literal (implicit concatenation and escapes decoded, no bytes or f-strings) different from the variable name. The finding is anchored on the target name, as in Ruff. Parenthesized callees, values and targets, dotted callee chains split by comments or parentheses, aliased and from-imports, module aliases, `Operator.partial(...)` and .pyi stubs behave as in Ruff; tuple, list, attribute, subscript and starred targets, chained, annotated or augmented assignments, walrus, a non-literal or missing `task_id`, shadowed names and look-alike callees are not reported. No known divergence.

### `ruff-air002-airflow-dag-no-schedule-argument`

Port of Ruff's AIR002 (airflow-dag-no-schedule-argument): reports a call whose callee resolves through imports to `airflow.**.DAG` or `airflow.**.dag` (the class and the decorator, through any module path or alias) and that has no `schedule`, `schedule_interval` or `timetable` keyword; the finding spans the whole call, as in Ruff. A `**kwargs` unpacking does not count as supplying the keyword, as in Ruff. Parenthesized callees, aliases, calls spanning lines, `@dag()` decorator calls, imports under TYPE_CHECKING or in try/except, function- and class-local imports and .pyi stubs behave as in Ruff; a bare `@dag` decorator, names rebound by assignment, star and relative imports, and look-alike or shadowed names are not reported. No known divergence.

### `ruff-air301-airflow3-removal`

Port of Ruff's AIR301 (airflow3-removal): reports uses, resolved through imports, of what Airflow 3 removed or renamed: 76 clauses cover removed or renamed qualified names (including airflow.contrib.* and the airflow.configuration functions), names that moved to the task SDK or to BaseHook, a name appearing inside a string Ruff reads as a type definition (annotations, TypeAlias, cast, TypeVar, NewType, nested quotes, not Literal), removed arguments of DAG, operators (`*Operator` under `airflow.operators` or a provider's `operators` module), task handlers (`*TaskHandler`), auth managers (`*AuthManager`, any constructor argument), task decorators, Variable.get and trigger_rule, removed methods and attributes on instances assigned from DAG, dataset, lineage, provider-manager, secrets-backend (`*Backend`), hook (`*Hook`) and auth-manager constructors (for datasets also through an attribute tail such as `ds.attr.iter_datasets()`, as Ruff's resolve_assignment appends it), deprecated context keys read in a @task function from its `**kwargs` or from airflow.utils.context.get_current_context, removed AirflowPlugin extension attributes, and the apply_defaults decorator (the range stops before a trailing comment as Ruff's does). Dotted callee chains split by comments or parentheses resolve as in Ruff. A renamed name is skipped, as in Ruff, when it sits in an `except ImportError`/`ModuleNotFoundError` (names) or `except AttributeError` (attributes) handler whose try body already imports (also by a relative `from .module import name`, which Ruff matches on the module text) or reads that very replacement; `del name` is skipped. Divergences: a removed constant used as a dotted value pattern in `match`/`case` (for example `case TriggerRule.DUMMY:`) is not reported; an attribute tail after a hook, backend or auth-manager instance whose last segment itself ends in that suffix (`x.OtherHook.get_connections()`) is not followed; and files above about 200 KB exceed the engine's per-file work budget.

### `ruff-air303-airflow3-incompatible-function-signature`

Port of Ruff's AIR303 (airflow3-incompatible-function-signature). Three clauses. (1) `create_asset` called on a `HookLineageCollector` with any positional or starred argument (a lone generator expression counts), reported at the method name. The receiver is either a direct constructor call, possibly parenthesized, or a name read as Ruff's resolve_assignment reads it. That is a name whose assignment holds a constructor call (plain, annotated, chained and tuple targets included), or an attribute chain whose head is assigned a call to `airflow.lineage.hook`, `airflow.lineage` or `airflow` that spells out the rest (`x = L.hook(); x.HookLineageCollector.create_asset(...)`). A name that is only annotated, bound by a walrus, loop or `with`, or assigned a tuple or list is not a receiver. (2) `Dataset` or `Asset` (`airflow.Dataset`, `airflow.datasets.Dataset` or `airflow.sdk.Asset`, through any alias) called through a plain name whose second positional argument is a dict, reported at the callee name. A dict here is a dict literal, a dict comprehension, a call to the builtin `dict`, or a name Ruff's dict inference accepts (dict value, `dict` or `Dict` annotation, `**kwargs`). The first argument must not be starred. Attribute callees such as `airflow.datasets.Dataset("a", {})` are not reported: Ruff sends every attribute callee to the method branch. (3) `get_link` defined directly in a class body, reported at the method name unless its signature is `(self, operator, *, ti_key)` or `(self, operator, ti_key)`. The class's base must be a qualified name under `airflow.models` or `airflow.sdk` ending in `BaseOperatorLink` (an aliased `import airflow.sdk.BaseOperatorLink as X` too), or inherit from one through classes of the same file. One subscript is stripped from a base, as Ruff's map_subscript does. The base lookup skips the class body around a nested class, as Ruff looks it up from the subclass's own scope.

### `ruff-ann001-missing-type-function-argument`

Port of Ruff's ANN001 (missing-type-function-argument): reports each positional-only, regular or keyword-only parameter of a function, method or nested function that has no annotation, anchored on the parameter name (`*args` and `**kwargs` belong to ANN002 and ANN003; lambdas are never checked; type comments do not count as annotations). In a method (a function whose nearest enclosing function or class is a class), the first non-variadic parameter is skipped as the receiver, keyword-only included (`def f(*, x)` in a class skips `x`). The skip does not apply when the method is decorated with `staticmethod` as Ruff resolves it. When the file never imports `builtins`, the bare name counts unless the module binds `staticmethod` anywhere (an annotation without a value or an `except ... as` name does not count: Ruff skips the one and unbinds the other after the handler): Ruff looks it up in the module's final state, so a rebinding after the class also disables it, while function- or class-level rebindings do not. When the file imports `builtins`, the decorator must resolve at its site to the builtin or `builtins.staticmethod`. A module-level `@staticmethod` function is not a method and keeps its first parameter checked. The implementation that directly follows `@overload` definitions of the same name (typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed; through `if`/`else`, `try`, `with`, `match` and loop blocks and the tail of a preceding class body) is skipped, while the `@overload` stubs themselves are checked. Divergences: a nested function that is the first definition of its body, preceded in Ruff's breadth-first definition order by a same-named `@overload` elsewhere, is reported (Ruff skips it); a local `staticmethod` rebinding in the scope of the file's last lambda, loop or comprehension, which Ruff applies file-wide, is ignored; an `@overload` used before its module-level import is not recognised. Default settings only (no suppress-dummy-args, ignore-fully-untyped).

### `ruff-arg001-unused-function-argument`

Port of Ruff's ARG001 (unused-function-argument): reports a parameter of a plain function (a `def` that is not directly in a class body, however deeply it sits in `if`, `try`, loops or other functions) whose binding is never read, on the parameter name only (`*args` and `**kwargs` included, reported on the bare name). A parameter is unused when the scope analysis finds no read of it, including reads from nested functions, classes, lambdas, comprehensions, f-strings, string annotations and annotation-only statements; a parameter that the body rebinds (assignment, `for`, `with`, `except`, import, walrus, `match` capture, `nonlocal` write) is not reported. Names matching `dummy-variable-rgx` (default `^(_+|(_+[a-zA-Z0-9_]*[a-zA-Z0-9]+?))$`, so `_`, `_x`, `__x` but not `_x_`) are skipped, and `flake8-unused-arguments.ignore-variadic-names` makes `*args` and `**kwargs` exempt. Functions are skipped when the body is a stub (only `pass`, string statements, `...` and `raise NotImplementedError`/`NotImplemented`, with or without a call), when it is Ruff's variable stub (an optional docstring, `msg = "..."` or an f-string assignment, then `raise NotImplementedError(msg)` with one positional argument naming the same variable, or neither being a plain name; a lone generator-expression argument counts as one non-name argument), when decorated with `typing.overload`, `typing_extensions.overload` or `_typeshed.overload` (not when written as a call, matching Ruff), and when the function body calls the bare name `locals()` in its own scope (nested functions, class bodies, lambdas and comprehension elements do not count; a comprehension's first iterable does). Files ending in `.pyi` are skipped, `# noqa: ARG001` and `# ruff: noqa` suppress as in Ruff. Divergences: Ruff's preview-only `# ruff: ignore[...]`, `disable[...]` and `file-ignore[...]` range comments are not modelled by the engine; a `locals()` in a class base list or keyword, a nested function's parameter or return annotation, or a `type` alias value is treated as a use by the engine but not by Ruff (Ruff reports); and a lone `[*locals()]` or `{*locals()}` is not recognised as a `locals()` call (Ruff skips the function).

### `ruff-arg002-unused-method-argument`

Port of Ruff's ARG002 (unused-method-argument): reports an unused parameter of an instance method: a `def` directly in a class body (through `if`, `try`, loops or `match` blocks) that is not decorated `staticmethod`/`classmethod` (builtin, `abc.abstractstaticmethod`/`abstractclassmethod`, resolved through imports, aliases and parenthesised or called forms such as `@staticmethod()`) and is not `__new__`, `__init_subclass__` or `__class_getitem__`. The first non-variadic parameter (positional-only, ordinary or keyword-only, whatever its name) is skipped; `*args` and `**kwargs` are checked. A parameter is unused when the scope analysis finds no read of it (nested functions, classes, lambdas, comprehensions, f-strings and string annotations count as reads; rebinding it in the body exempts it). Names matching `dummy-variable-rgx` (default `^(_+|(_+[a-zA-Z0-9_]*[a-zA-Z0-9]+?))$`) are skipped, and `flake8-unused-arguments.ignore-variadic-names` exempts `*args` and `**kwargs`. Exempt: stub bodies (only `pass`, string statements, `...`, `raise NotImplementedError`/`NotImplemented`), Ruff's variable stub (`msg = "..."` then `raise NotImplementedError(msg)`, or a non-name target such as `self.msg = "..."` with a non-name argument, a lone generator expression included), methods decorated with `abc.abstractmethod`, `abstractclassmethod`, `abstractstaticmethod`, `abstractproperty`, `typing.override`, `typing_extensions.override` or `_typeshed.override`, or `typing.overload`, `typing_extensions.overload` or `_typeshed.overload` (a called decorator such as `@abstractmethod()` or `@overload()` is not exempt, matching Ruff), magic names (starting and ending with two underscores) except `__init__` and `__call__` (so `__init_subclass__` and `__class_getitem__` never fire), and methods whose own scope calls the bare name `locals()`. `.pyi` files are skipped; `# noqa: ARG002` and `# ruff: noqa` suppress. Divergences: Ruff's preview `# ruff: ignore[...]`, `disable[...]` and `file-ignore[...]` comments are not modelled; a `locals()` in a class base, class keyword, nested def annotation or return annotation, or `type` alias value counts as a use here but not in Ruff; a lone `[*locals()]` or `{*locals()}` is missed as a `locals()` call.

### `ruff-c400-unnecessary-generator-list`

Port of Ruff's C400 (unnecessary-generator-list): reports a call `list(<generator>)`. The callee must be the bare name `list`, possibly parenthesized, and it must resolve to the unshadowed builtin at the call (Ruff's `has_builtin_binding`). So these stay silent: `builtins.list(...)`, `from builtins import list`, a parameter, a local, a class attribute seen from the class body, an except/for/with/walrus/match binding, `global list` or `nonlocal list` in the calling scope, an unconditional `del list`, and a later module-level rebinding seen from a function body. A bare annotation `list: type` does not shadow, and neither does a star import. The call takes exactly one positional argument and no keywords. That argument is a generator expression: bare, or wrapped in any number of extra parentheses, with an optional trailing comma. The report spans the whole call. Calls inside string annotations are reported too: parameter, return and variable annotations, nested string annotations, the first argument of `cast` and `TypeVar(bound=...)`. Plain strings and `Annotated` metadata are not. The lookup inside a string annotation uses the scope the annotation is written in, so a return annotation sees the module, a local annotation sees the function's parameters, and a class-body annotation sees the class. Two clauses give Ruff's two messages. The short-circuit clause matches a generator with one non-async `for` clause, no `if`, and an element structurally equal to the target (Ruff's `ComparableExpr`: `(a, b) for a, b in y`, `x for (x) in y`, `d['k'] for d["k"] in y` and `d[0x1] for d[1] in y` all count). It advises `list(iterable)`, and says to keep the generator when the target unpacks or assigns to an attribute or subscript. Every other generator, including the Python 3.15 `list(*x for x in y)`, gets the list-comprehension advice. Inside list, set and tuple displays tree-sitter reads `*list(...)` as a call whose function is `*list`, so the clause anchors on a regex capture that starts after the star, as Ruff reports. Divergences: tree-sitter accepts the invalid `list(x for x in y, )`, which this rule reports and Ruff rejects as a syntax error. A starred call inside a string annotation (`n: "[*list(x for x in xs)]"`) is not reported. When the target has a starred element or a parenthesized name nested inside it (`list((a, *b) for a, *b in y)`, `list((a, b) for (a), b in y)`), the call is reported at Ruff's span but with the list-comprehension message instead of the `list(iterable)` one.

### `ruff-c401-unnecessary-generator-set`

Port of Ruff's C401 (unnecessary-generator-set): reports a call `set(<generator>)`. The callee must be the bare name `set`, possibly parenthesized, and it must resolve to the unshadowed builtin at the call (Ruff's `has_builtin_binding`). So these stay silent: `builtins.set(...)`, `from builtins import set`, `frozenset(...)`, parameters, locals, class attributes seen from the class body, except/for/with/walrus/match bindings, `global set` or `nonlocal set` in the calling scope, an unconditional `del set`, and later module-level rebindings seen from function bodies. A bare annotation and a star import do not shadow. The call takes exactly one positional argument and no keywords. That argument is a generator expression: bare, or wrapped in extra parentheses, with an optional trailing comma. The report spans the whole call, including inside f-string and t-string replacement fields. Calls inside string annotations are reported too: parameter, return and variable annotations, nested string annotations, the first argument of `cast` and `TypeVar(bound=...)`. Plain strings and `Annotated` metadata are not. The lookup inside a string annotation uses the scope the annotation is written in. Two clauses give Ruff's two messages. The short-circuit clause matches a generator with one non-async `for` clause, no `if`, and an element structurally equal to the target (Ruff's `ComparableExpr`, so literals compare by value). It advises `set(iterable)`, and says to keep the generator when the target unpacks or assigns to an attribute or subscript. Every other generator, including the Python 3.15 `set(*x for x in y)`, gets the set-comprehension advice. Starred calls in displays (`{*set(...)}`) are anchored from the callee through a regex capture, as Ruff reports them. Divergences: tree-sitter accepts the invalid `set(x for x in y, )`, which this rule reports and Ruff rejects as a syntax error. A starred call inside a string annotation (`n: "[*set(x for x in xs)]"`) is not reported. When the target has a starred element or a parenthesized name nested inside it (`set((a, *b) for a, *b in y)`, `set((a, b) for (a), b in y)`), the call is reported at Ruff's span but with the set-comprehension message.

### `ruff-c402-unnecessary-generator-dict`

Port of Ruff's C402 (unnecessary-generator-dict): reports a call `dict(<generator>)` whose generator element is a tuple of exactly two elements with no starred element, for example `dict((k, v) for k, v in items)`. The element may sit in extra parentheses and may have a trailing comma. The callee is the bare name `dict`, possibly parenthesized. As in Ruff, there is no builtin check, so a shadowed or rebound `dict` (a parameter, a class attribute, a local `def dict`, `from collections import OrderedDict as dict`) is still reported, and the message says to leave those alone. `builtins.dict(...)`, aliases and attribute calls stay silent. The call takes exactly one positional argument and no keywords. That argument is the generator, bare or wrapped in extra parentheses, with an optional trailing comma. One-tuples, three-tuples, starred tuples like `(*v, k)`, lists, conditional expressions and other non-tuple elements are not reported. Filters, several `for` clauses and async generators are. The report spans the whole call. Calls inside string annotations are reported too: parameter, return and variable annotations, nested string annotations, the first argument of `cast` and `TypeVar(bound=...)`. Plain strings and `Annotated` metadata are not. Starred calls in displays (`[*dict(...)]`) are anchored from the callee through a regex capture, as Ruff reports them. Divergence: a starred call inside a string annotation (`n: "[*dict((x, x) for x in xs)]"`) is not reported.

### `ruff-c403-unnecessary-list-comprehension-set`

Port of Ruff's C403 (unnecessary-list-comprehension-set): reports a call `set([<list comprehension>])`. The callee must be the bare name `set`, possibly parenthesized, and it must resolve to the unshadowed builtin at the call (Ruff's `has_builtin_binding`). So these stay silent: `builtins.set(...)`, `from builtins import set`, `frozenset(...)`, parameters, locals, class attributes seen from the class body, except, for, with, walrus and match bindings, `global set` or `nonlocal set` in the calling scope, an unconditional `del set`, and later module-level rebindings seen from function bodies. The call takes exactly one positional argument and no keywords. That argument is a list comprehension, bare or wrapped in any number of extra parentheses, with an optional trailing comma. Async comprehensions and the Python 3.15 `set([*x for x in y])` are reported. List literals, set comprehensions, generators and expressions built from a comprehension (`[...] + [1]`, `[...][0]`, a conditional) are not. The report spans the whole call, including inside f-string and t-string replacement fields. Calls inside string annotations are reported too: parameter, return and variable annotations, nested string annotations, the first argument of `cast` and `TypeVar(bound=...)`. Plain strings and `Annotated` metadata are not. The lookup inside a string annotation uses the scope the annotation is written in. Starred calls in displays (`{*set([...])}`) are anchored from the callee through a regex capture, as Ruff reports them. Divergence: a starred call inside a string annotation (`n: "[*set([x for x in xs])]"`) is not reported.

### `ruff-c404-unnecessary-list-comprehension-dict`

Port of Ruff's C404 (unnecessary-list-comprehension-dict): reports a call to `dict` with exactly one positional argument and no keyword arguments when that argument is a list comprehension (parentheses around it are transparent) whose element is a tuple of exactly two elements. Parenthesized tuples count, and a starred element counts as one element, so `dict([(i, *j) for i in x])` is reported, while `dict([(i,) for ...])`, `dict([(i, j, k) for ...])` and `dict([[i, j] for ...])` are not. The callee must be the bare name `dict`, optionally parenthesized, that resolves to the builtin where it is read, as Ruff's `has_builtin_binding` does: bindings made earlier in module and class bodies, all module bindings seen from function and lambda bodies, parameters, imports (including `from builtins import dict`), class attributes in their own class body (and in that class's default values and first comprehension iterable), and a `global dict` declaration in the reading or an enclosing function or class scope all exempt it. `builtins.dict(...)` is not reported. A generator argument (`dict((k, v) for ...)`) is left to another rule. Calls inside string type definitions are reported at their position inside the string, as Ruff parses them: annotations, subscripts such as `List["..."]` and `cast` arguments, nested strings included. `Literal[...]` strings and plain strings are not reported. The finding spans the whole call, f-string and t-string interpolations included.

### `ruff-c405-unnecessary-literal-set`

Port of Ruff's C405 (unnecessary-literal-set): reports a call to `set` with exactly one positional argument and no keyword arguments when that argument is a list or tuple literal, including empty ones (`set([])`, `set(())`), through any parentheses (`set(((1, 2)))`). A parenthesized scalar such as `set((1))` is not a tuple and is not reported. Neither are `set(*a)`, `set([1], [2])`, `set([1], key=1)`, comprehensions, generators, dict or set literals, or strings. The callee must be the bare name `set`, optionally parenthesized, that resolves to the builtin where it is read, as Ruff's `has_builtin_binding` does: earlier bindings in module and class bodies, any module binding seen from function and lambda bodies, parameters, imports (including `from builtins import set`), class attributes in their own class body, and a `global set` declaration in the reading or an enclosing function or class scope all exempt it. `builtins.set(...)` is not reported. Calls inside string type definitions (annotations, `List["..."]` subscripts, `cast` and `TypeVar` bound arguments, nested strings) are reported at their position inside the string, as Ruff parses them. `Literal[...]` and plain strings are not reported. The finding spans the whole call.

### `ruff-c406-unnecessary-literal-dict`

Port of Ruff's C406 (unnecessary-literal-dict): reports a call to `dict` with exactly one positional argument and no keyword arguments when that argument is a list or tuple literal (through parentheses) and every element of it is a tuple of exactly two elements. An empty list or tuple is reported (`dict([])`, `dict(())`, `dict((()))`). A starred element (`dict([(1, 2), *rest])`), a list element (`dict([(1, 2), [3, 4]])`), a walrus or any other non-tuple element exempts the call. A parenthesized pair counts as a pair, and a starred item inside a pair counts toward its two elements, so `dict([(1, *a)])` is reported. The callee must be the bare name `dict`, optionally parenthesized, that resolves to the builtin where it is read, as Ruff's `has_builtin_binding` does: shadowing bindings in Ruff's evaluation order, `from builtins import dict` and a `global dict` declaration in the reading or an enclosing scope all exempt it. `builtins.dict(...)` is not reported. Calls inside string type definitions, including implicitly concatenated ones, are reported where Ruff reports them. Strings inside `Literal[...]` are not. The finding spans the whole call. The message tells the agent to leave a pair that unpacks with `*` alone, since that has no literal form.

### `ruff-c408-unnecessary-collection-call`

Port of Ruff's C408 (unnecessary-collection-call) with default settings: reports `dict()`, `list()` and `tuple()` called with no positional arguments, and `dict(...)` called with only named keyword arguments (`dict(a=1, b=2)`). Any positional or starred argument exempts the call. `dict(**kw)` and `dict(a=1, **kw)` are exempt, and so are `list(x=1)` and `tuple(x=1)`. The `allow-dict-calls-with-keyword-arguments` option is not modelled; Ruff's default, false, is assumed. The callee is resolved as Ruff's `resolve_builtin_symbol` does, through parentheses. It matches the bare builtin name where nothing file-local shadows it, in Ruff's evaluation order: a module-level `list()` before `def list` is still reported, but one after it, or in any function body, is not. It also matches `builtins.dict`, `builtins.list` and `builtins.tuple` reached through `import builtins`, an alias of it, or `from builtins import ...`, but not through a plain assignment such as `b = builtins`. After Ruff's traversal has visited an absolute import of `builtins`, Ruff switches to qualified-name resolution. That traversal covers the module body in order, then function bodies level by level, then lambda bodies. Under qualified-name resolution, a name the reading scope (or an enclosing one) declares `global` resolves to the builtin unless the module binds it. The document mirrors this with the same traversal order, so `global dict; dict()` is reported only when such an import is visited before the read: not in a class body above the import, and not in a function defined before the function that imports `builtins`. Calls inside string type definitions are reported at their position inside the string. Strings inside `Literal[...]` and `Annotated` metadata are not. The finding spans the whole call.

### `ruff-c410-unnecessary-literal-within-list-call`

Port of Ruff's C410 (unnecessary-literal-within-list-call): reports a `list(...)` call whose only argument is a list or tuple literal and that has no keyword, `**` or extra positional arguments. A trailing comma is allowed, and parentheses around the callee or the argument are unwrapped. The callee must be the bare name `list` and must resolve to the builtin, as Ruff's `has_builtin_binding` decides. A shadowing parameter, assignment, import, comprehension variable, match capture or enclosing-function binding silences it. So does a later module binding seen by a deferred body: function and lambda bodies, and annotations deferred by `from __future__ import annotations` or in stubs. A default value or decorator is read where it stands. An unconditional `del list` unbinds the name for the code after it, so the call is not reported there. `builtins.list(...)` is not reported. The list-literal case (drop the call) and the tuple-literal case (rewrite as a list literal) are separate clauses with different advice. The whole call is reported, including calls inside strings that Ruff reads as type definitions (string annotations, `cast` arguments). Those strings resolve names as forward references, so a later module-level rebinding of `list` silences them, as in Ruff. A name shadowed in the class or function scope where the string appears also silences them, as Ruff's `has_builtin_binding` decides. One divergence comes from the engine: in an escaped or implicitly concatenated string, Ruff gives every nested node the whole string's range. A matching call nested inside another one is then reported twice by Ruff and once by the port.

### `ruff-c411-unnecessary-list-call`

Port of Ruff's C411 (unnecessary-list-call): reports a `list(...)` call whose only argument is a list comprehension (async comprehensions and Python 3.15 starred comprehensions included) and that has no keyword, `**` or extra positional arguments. A trailing comma is allowed, and parentheses around the callee or the comprehension are unwrapped. The callee must be the bare name `list` and must resolve to the builtin, as Ruff's `has_builtin_binding` decides with scope and binding order. A bare annotation (`list: type`) does not shadow. A `global list` declaration shadows only inside its own function: an assignment after it does not rebind `list` for the module or for other functions. An unconditional `del list` unbinds it for the code that follows. `builtins.list(...)`, set and dict comprehensions, and generator arguments are not reported. The whole call is reported, including calls inside strings that Ruff reads as type definitions. One divergence comes from the engine: in an escaped or implicitly concatenated string, Ruff gives every nested node the whole string's range. A `list([...])` nested in another one is then reported twice by Ruff and once by the port.

### `ruff-c413-unnecessary-call-around-sorted`

Port of Ruff's C413 (unnecessary-call-around-sorted): reports a call to builtin `list` or `reversed` whose first positional argument is a call to builtin `sorted`. Other positional or keyword arguments after it do not matter, as in Ruff's `args.first()`. Both callees resolve as Ruff's `resolve_builtin_symbol` and `match_builtin_expr` do. A bare builtin name counts unless scope and binding order shadow it. Comprehension variables, match captures, an unconditional `del`, and later module bindings seen by deferred function bodies all count. A `global` declaration shadows only inside its own function. `builtins.list` and `builtins.sorted` count through `import builtins`, and so do aliases such as `import builtins as b` and `from builtins import reversed as rev`. Names imported from other modules and an unimported `builtins.sorted` do not count. Parentheses around either callee or the inner call are unwrapped. A starred first argument, a generator argument, or `sorted(...)` in a later position is not reported. The `list` case (drop the call) and the `reversed` case are separate clauses. The `reversed` case advises passing or flipping `reverse=` and keeping `reversed` when equal elements must come out in reverse input order. The whole outer call is reported, including calls inside strings that Ruff reads as type definitions. Two divergences come from the engine. First, when a file imports `builtins`, Ruff resolves names in forward references (string type definitions, and annotations deferred by `from __future__ import annotations` or in stubs) against the module scope first. So `list`, `reversed` or `sorted` shadowed only in an enclosing class or function still counts there, and the port does not report it. Second, in an escaped or implicitly concatenated string, the same clause nested twice gets one whole-string span. Ruff then reports twice and the port once.

### `ruff-c414-unnecessary-double-cast-or-process`

Port of Ruff's C414 (unnecessary-double-cast-or-process): reports a call to `list`, `tuple`, `set` or `sorted` whose first positional argument is itself a call to one of `list`, `tuple`, `set`, `sorted` or `reversed`, for the pairs Ruff lists (`set`/`sorted` around `list`, `tuple`, `reversed` or `sorted`; `set` around `set`; `list`/`tuple` around `list`/`tuple`). Both callees must resolve to the builtins as Ruff's `resolve_builtin_symbol` does (a bare name, or `builtins.<name>` and its aliases once `builtins` is imported), so a parameter, assignment, import, enclosing-function binding, `global` rebinding or `del` hides them, in source order for module and class bodies. `sorted` inside `sorted` is reported only when both calls pass the same keyword arguments in any order, compared structurally as Ruff's `ComparableKeyword` does (parentheses, quote style, implicit string concatenation and number spelling do not matter). Parentheses around callees and arguments are unwrapped, a starred or generator first argument is not a call, and the whole outer call is reported. A nested `reversed` inside `sorted` gets its own advice, since it changes the order of items that sort equal. No known divergence from Ruff.

### `ruff-c415-unnecessary-subscript-reversal`

Port of Ruff's C415 (unnecessary-subscript-reversal): reports a call to `set`, `sorted` or `reversed` whose first positional argument is a subscript with a single slice that has no lower or upper bound (not even `None`) and a step that is unary minus applied to the integer literal 1 in any spelling (`-1`, `-0x1`, `-(1)`, `(-1)`); `-1.0`, `-True`, `- -1` and tuple subscripts do not count. The callee must resolve to the builtin, with Ruff's scoping and source-order rules (a bare name, or `builtins.<name>` once `builtins` is imported). Parentheses are unwrapped and the whole call is reported. `reversed` gets different advice from `set` and `sorted`. No known divergence from Ruff.

### `ruff-c416-unnecessary-comprehension`

Port of Ruff's C416 (unnecessary-comprehension): reports a list or set comprehension with one `for` clause, no `if` and no `async`, whose element is the same name as its loop target (parentheses ignored), and a dict comprehension `{k: v for k, v in it}` whose key and value are the two names of its tuple target in order; also `[(k, v) for k, v in d.items()]` (tuple or list target, any parenthesization) and `[x for x in d.items()]` when `d` resolves to a binding Ruff's type inference treats as a dict (a dict display or comprehension or `dict()` call, also through tuple unpacking and walrus; a `dict` or `Dict` annotation on a variable or parameter; a `**kwargs` parameter of a `def`; a `with` item; a function whose return annotation is `dict`). The constructor the fix would call (`list`, `set`, `dict`) must still be the builtin where the comprehension is analyzed, following Ruff's scope lookup: module and class bodies see their own earlier bindings; function and lambda bodies, which Ruff visits after their enclosing scope, see every binding of enclosing function and module scopes and their own earlier ones; class bindings are invisible to methods and nested comprehensions but visible to a comprehension's first iterable; enclosing comprehension targets, lambda parameters, type parameters, `del`, and an `except ... as` name inside its handler hide it, while a bare annotation and a handler name after its handler do not; parameter defaults and decorators belong to the enclosing scope. Strings Ruff reads as type definitions (`x: "[y for y in ys]"`, `List["{y for y in ys}"]`, nested strings) are parsed as annotations and checked the same way, with the shadowing check taken at the string. The whole comprehension is reported. Divergences: a name bound by a `with` item that destructures a tuple of dict displays is not inferred as a dict (see reason); inside a string annotation the `d.items()` forms are not reported, since a name there is not resolved to its dict binding.

### `ruff-c417-unnecessary-map`

Port of Ruff's C417 (unnecessary-map): reports `map(lambda ...)` with no keyword arguments, directly or as the only argument of `list(...)`, `set(...)` or `dict(...)` (themselves without keywords). The lambda must take exactly one plain positional parameter without a default (positional-only, keyword-only and starred parameters do not count, and the iterable count is not checked, so `map(lambda x: x)` is reported, as in Ruff), its body must contain no `yield`, no iterable may be starred or contain a walrus anywhere inside, and the parameter must not be read by a nested lambda (defaults included) that does not rebind it (Ruff's late-binding check). For `dict`, the lambda body must be a 2-element tuple or list. A `map(...)` anywhere among the arguments of a builtin `list`/`set`/`dict` call, keyword values and `**` included, is reported only by the container clause or not at all, matching Ruff's parent-call check; a `*map(...)` is reported on its own. Every callee must resolve to the builtin. The whole outermost call is reported. The generator, list, set and dict forms give different advice. No known divergence from Ruff.

### `ruff-c418-unnecessary-literal-within-dict-call`

Port of Ruff's C418 (unnecessary-literal-within-dict-call): reports a call whose callee is the bare name `dict`, alone or in parentheses, when that name is the builtin where the call runs, as Ruff's has_builtin_binding reads it. Any visible binding silences it: an assignment, parameter, import (`from builtins import dict` included), loop or `with` target, `except ... as` name (also after its handler), `global`/`nonlocal` declaration, comprehension variable, type parameter or class attribute. Module and class bodies see only earlier bindings, function bodies see every module binding, and a later local binding in the same function does not silence an earlier call. The call must take exactly one argument and no keyword or `**` argument. That argument must be a dict literal (`{}`, `{**a}`) or a dict comprehension, possibly in parentheses. Calls inside string annotations and other string type definitions (`x: "dict({})"`, `cast("dict({})", v)`, but not `Literal[...]`) are reported too, with the span Ruff gives them. Elsewhere the span covers the whole call, including a parenthesized callee. Calls with several positionals, keywords or starred arguments are ignored, and so are `builtins.dict(...)` and attribute calls such as `obj.dict({})`. Divergence: Python 3.15 dict-unpacking comprehensions (`dict({**d for d in ds})`, PEP 798) are not reported, because the parser cannot read that syntax.

### `ruff-c901-complex-structure`

Port of Ruff's C901 (complex-structure): reports a function whose complexity is above `mccabe.max-complexity` (an integer option, default 10). Complexity starts at 1 for the function's own body. Each of these in the body adds 1: an `if`, an `elif`, a `for` or `async for`, a `while`, an `except` or `except*` handler, a `try` statement's `else`, a nested `def` or `async def` (at any depth, including methods of nested classes) and a `case`. A last `case` of a `match` with no guard and an irrefutable pattern costs nothing. Irrefutable patterns are `_`, a bare capture name, a parenthesized group, an `as` pattern whose inner pattern is irrefutable (`_ as z`, `y as z`, `(0 | _) as z`), or a `|` alternative containing one. `None`, `True`, `False`, dotted values, `1 as z`, `(1 | 2) as z`, one-element tuples such as `y,` and sequence patterns such as `[*_]` still count. The finding is reported at the function name. These cost nothing: `with`/`async with`, `try`/`finally`, `else` on `if`/`for`/`while`, the class statement itself, conditional expressions, comprehension `if`s, boolean operators and lambdas. Every function is checked, including methods, nested, decorated, generic (PEP 695) and async ones, and functions in `.pyi` stubs. A nested function is reported on its own and also counts toward every function that encloses it. The document counts the decision points together with each function body using a `count` capped by `max` at the option's value, so values such as 0, 1 and 3 behave as Ruff's do, which I checked against Ruff configured alike. Divergences: the message cannot include Ruff's `(N > M)` figures. On a file that Ruff's parser rejects but tree-sitter accepts (a Python 2 `print 'x'` statement), Ruff runs no syntax-tree rule and reports nothing, while the document still reports complex functions.

### `ruff-d100-undocumented-public-module`

Port of Ruff's D100 (undocumented-public-module): reports, at the very start of the file (1:1, zero width, as Ruff does), a Python module whose first statement is not a docstring. A docstring is an expression statement holding a string literal: plain, raw or u-prefixed, triple-quoted, parenthesized, or implicitly concatenated from such parts. Not a docstring, as in Ruff: a bytes, f-string or t-string first statement (also one concatenated with a plain string), a tuple such as """doc""",, a call, subscript, operator or conditional on a string, a walrus, or a string after any other statement (a comment, shebang or encoding line before it is fine; from __future__ imports are not). Skipped as in Ruff: stub files (.pyi), __init__.py (that is D104) and private modules. A module is private when any name in its module path (sent by the host inside a package; otherwise the file stem) starts with an underscore and is not a dunder, so _x and __x are private while __x__, __main__ and __init__ are public. Divergences: the per-rule harness cannot send a module path, so Ruff's pkg/_priv/no_D100_priv.py test file (private only through its package path) is reported; and for a file outside a package whose stem contains a dot followed by an underscore name (a._b.py), file.module cannot tell the stem from a package path, so the file is treated as private where Ruff reports it. Defaults assumed.

### `ruff-d101-undocumented-public-class`

Port of Ruff's D101 (undocumented-public-class): reports the name of a module-level class, including one under module-level if/try/with/for/while/match, whose body does not start with a docstring (forms as D100: plain, raw or u-prefixed string, parenthesized or implicitly concatenated; not bytes, f-string or t-string, a tuple, an expression on a string, or a string after another statement; comments before it are fine). The class is public when its name does not start with an underscore and its module is public (as D100). When the module assigns __all__ at module level (a statement whose target is __all__, also parenthesized, by =, an annotated assignment with or without a value, +=, or the first target of a chained assignment; not x = __all__ = ..., and not inside a class or function), only names listed there are public. Names are read where Ruff reads them: string elements (parenthesized or not) of a list, tuple or bare tuple that is the value, an operand of a left-nested chain of binary operators, a walrus value, or the single list, set or tuple argument of a builtin list() or tuple() call (also list[str](...), no keywords); a value Ruff cannot read (a call such as sorted(), a comprehension, a subscript, a conditional) lists nothing, so every name is private. Nested classes are D106's, classes inside functions are never reported, and .pyi stubs are skipped. The span is the class name. Divergences: an entry that is prefixed, triple-quoted, escaped or implicitly concatenated and sits in a bare tuple, behind parentheses, or under a parenthesized __all__ target is not recognised (engine gap), and the dotted-stem edge of D100. The rule's clauses are source-incomplete under syntax errors, so a file tree-sitter-python misreads but Ruff parses is skipped (pyupgrade/UP045_1.py, whose `Optional[str]is not True` annotation the grammar rejects). Defaults assumed (no lint.pydocstyle.ignore-decorators).

### `ruff-d103-undocumented-public-function`

Port of Ruff's D103 (undocumented-public-function): reports the name of a module-level def or async def, including one under module-level if/try/with/for/while/match, whose body does not start with a docstring (forms as D101). The function is public when its name does not start with an underscore (so dunder functions such as __getattr__ are private), its module is public (as D100) and, when the module assigns __all__, it is listed there (read as for D101). Functions nested in functions are never reported, methods are D102's, and functions decorated with typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed overload (name or attribute resolved through imports, parenthesized or not; @overload() does not count; override does not exempt a function) are skipped. Ruff resolves the decorator where it is read, so a later rebinding of overload does not undo the exemption. Stub files (.pyi) are skipped; the span is the function name.
