Divergences: the __all__ entry engine gap and dotted-stem edge described for D101, and @overload above a later import of overload (which fails at import time) is reported where Ruff exempts it. The rule's clauses are source-incomplete under syntax errors, so a file tree-sitter-python misreads but Ruff parses is skipped (flake8_bugbear/B012.py, whose `match *0, 1, *2:` subject the grammar rejects, and pyupgrade/UP045_1.py, whose `Optional[str]is not True` annotation it rejects). lint.pydocstyle.ignore-decorators is not modelled (default empty).

### `ruff-d200-unnecessary-multiline-docstring`

Port of Ruff's D200 (unnecessary-multiline-docstring): matches a docstring (the first statement of a module, class or function, one plain string, possibly parenthesized; not bytes, f-string, t-string or implicitly concatenated) whose text between the quotes spans more than one line (any line break, \n, \r\n or a bare \r, including a trailing one or a backslash continuation) but holds exactly one line with a non-whitespace character. Reported on the whole string literal, as Ruff does. Agrees with Ruff on its fixtures (D.py, D200.py and the other pydocstyle files) and on 23 review probes (escapes, form feed, NEL and other Unicode whitespace, CR and CRLF files, BOM, nested and generic definitions, non-docstring strings).

### `ruff-d205-missing-blank-line-after-summary`

Port of Ruff's D205 (missing-blank-line-after-summary): matches a triple-quoted docstring whose first non-blank line is followed by something other than exactly one blank line before the next non-blank line (none, or two or more); a docstring with a single line of text is left alone, as is any single-quoted one. Line breaks are \n, \r\n or a bare \r, and a \r\n is never counted as two breaks. Reported on the whole string literal. Agrees with Ruff on its fixtures and on 23 review probes (CR and CRLF files, Unicode whitespace lines, escapes, nested definitions).

### `ruff-d209-new-line-after-last-paragraph`

Port of Ruff's D209 (new-line-after-last-paragraph): matches a triple-quoted docstring with at least two non-blank lines whose last line holds more than the closing quotes (text, an escaped quote or a backslash before them). Reported on the whole string literal. Agrees with Ruff on its fixtures (including D209_D400.py) and on 23 review probes (escaped quotes before the closer, tab-indented closers, CR files, Unicode whitespace).

### `ruff-d212-multi-line-summary-first-line`

Port of Ruff's D212 (multi-line-summary-first-line): matches a multi-line docstring with non-blank text whose first physical line is exactly the opening quotes (an optional r or u prefix and three quotes, nothing after them, not even a space or a backslash continuation). Reported on the whole string literal. Agrees with Ruff on its fixtures and on 23 review probes. Ruff's sibling rule D213 (text on the same line is a violation there) is not part of this document.

### `ruff-d215-overindented-section-underline`

Port of Ruff's D215 (overindented-section-underline): reports the dash run of a section underline (the first non-blank line after a section header, only dashes and surrounding whitespace) that is indented deeper than the docstring's opening line, measured as Ruff does from the line start to the string (indentation plus any opening parentheses). The header is any section name Ruff knows in either style (Args, Parameters, Returns, Notes, See Also, Todo and so on, any case, optional colon, nothing else on the line) on a line after the first, including one directly after the previous section's underline. The opening line's indentation is read from the enclosing def, class or module text, one clause per width (0-6, 8-10, 12, 16 and 20), because the engine has no columns. Diverges from Ruff for other widths, for docstrings that mix the section names of both styles, for headers Ruff rejects through its previous-line and sub-section heuristics, for directives (one hiding a header silences the whole docstring), for docstrings on the def or class line (never reported), and for bare carriage-return files. Agrees with Ruff on its fixtures (D215.py, sections.py) and on the review's exactness probes (parenthesized docstrings, over-indented closing quotes, module docstrings, tabs mixed with spaces, multi-line signatures, consecutive sections).

### `ruff-d300-triple-single-quotes`

Port of Ruff's D300 (triple-single-quotes): reports a docstring (the first statement, ignoring comments, of a module, class or function body: a single plain string literal with an optional `r` or `u` prefix, possibly parenthesized, with at least one non-whitespace character in its text; f-strings, byte strings, a tuple like `'doc',` and implicit concatenations are not docstrings) whose opening quotes are not triple double quotes, unless its text contains triple double quotes; in that case it reports a docstring whose opening quotes are not triple single quotes, unless its text also contains triple single quotes (then nothing is reported). The whole string is reported, as in Ruff, and `.pyi` files are checked too. Empty and whitespace-only docstrings are skipped, matching Ruff's empty-docstring early exit (the check uses the engine's whitespace class, which differs from Rust's only for a few exotic code points). Unlike the other rules of this family it does not depend on visibility, so private definitions and nested functions are checked as well.

### `ruff-d301-escape-sequence-in-docstring`

Port of Ruff's D301 (escape-sequence-in-docstring): reports a docstring (the first statement, ignoring comments, of a module, class or function body: a single string literal with no prefix or only `u`/`U`, possibly parenthesized; raw strings, f-strings, byte strings, a tuple like `"doc",` and implicit concatenations are not docstrings or are skipped) whose text contains a backslash that is not a line continuation (backslash followed by a newline), not the start of a `\u`, `\U` or `\N` escape, and not part of an escaped triple quote (`\"""`, `\'''`, `\"\"\"`, `\'\'\'`) as Ruff's scan treats them; an escaped backslash `\\` is itself reported, as in Ruff, and a backslash at the very end of the text is ignored. The whole string is reported, and `.pyi` files and private or nested definitions are checked too. The scan is expressed as one regular expression over the string's text. The empty-docstring early exit does not matter here since an empty text holds no backslash.

### `ruff-d400-missing-trailing-period`

Port of Ruff's D400 (missing-trailing-period): matches a plain or raw/unicode (not bytes, f-string or t-string) string that is the first statement of a module, class or function body (also inside parentheses; not a tuple, not implicit concatenation) whose summary's logical line (first non-blank, non-underline line, plus following lines up to the first blank or underline line) does not end with a period. Lines break at `\n`, `\r\n` and a lone `\r`, as Ruff's universal newlines do (the content is normalised to `\n` before the check). Skips Sphinx field lines (`:param`, `:type`, `:raises`, `:return`, `:rtype`), a first line that is just a section name (with an optional leading colon), and a summary ending in a backslash. Reports the whole docstring literal. Question marks, exclamation marks, colons and semicolons count as missing a period, as in Ruff (only the autofix differs).

### `ruff-d401-non-imperative-mood`

Port of Ruff's D401 (non-imperative-mood): for the docstring of a function or method (not a class or module) whose name does not start with `test` and is not `runTest`, and which is not decorated with `property`, `builtins.property`, `enum.property`, `functools.cached_property`, `abc.abstractproperty` or `types.DynamicClassAttribute` (resolved through imports, called or not; a shadowed `property` does not count), takes the first whitespace-separated word of the trimmed docstring, deletes every non-alphanumeric character, lower-cases it and reports the docstring when that word is a blacklisted word (a, the, this, handler, subclasses, ...) or has the stem of a known imperative verb without being the verb itself (returns, running, creation, decorator, references, calculations). Leaves other words and empty words alone. The verb and stem list is a regex lexicon matching Ruff on about 270 verbs; rare derived forms (serializable, steppes) and stemmer coincidences (finale, teste) may differ.

### `ruff-d402-signature-in-docstring`

Port of Ruff's D402 (signature-in-docstring): for the docstring of a function or method (not a class or module), reports when the docstring's first non-blank line contains the function's own name immediately followed by `(` and preceded by the start of the line, a space, a tab, a semicolon or a comma (so `xfoo(`, `self.foo(` and `foo (` do not count). The line's call-like words are listed by one replace and the first 24 are compared with the name. Reports the whole docstring literal.

### `ruff-d404-docstring-starts-with-this`

Port of Ruff's D404 (docstring-starts-with-this): for any module, class or function docstring (plain, raw or unicode strings only, not implicit concatenation), splits the trimmed docstring at the first space (not at other whitespace), removes every non-alphanumeric character of that piece, lower-cases it and reports when it equals `this`. So `This`, `(This)`, `THIS`, `Th-is`, `this,` and a `This` followed by a newline and an indented line are reported; `Thisis`, `this-is` (which joins into `thisis`), `This is` and `Those` are not. Reports the whole docstring literal.

### `ruff-d405-non-capitalized-section-name`

Port of Ruff's D405 (non-capitalized-section-name): reports, at the name only, a docstring section-name line whose name is not in its canonical capitalization (`Returns`, `See Also`, `Todo`, ...). A line counts as a section header when it holds only a recognised name, optionally followed by a colon, and it is underlined with - or =, follows a blank line, or follows a line ending in sentence punctuation. After such a line, a lower-case name counts only when no section precedes it. Names are recognised per docstring style. Shared names always count. NumPy-only names (Parameters, Receives, ...) count when the docstring is judged NumPy-style, and Google-only names (Args, Note, Todo, ...) count otherwise. The style follows Ruff's tie-breaks in order: Parameters, then Args, then which style has more sections, then underline versus colon. CRLF files behave like LF files. Divergences, all in unusual docstrings: (1) a lower-case or wrongly cased section name indented deeper than the previous section is approximated by excluding any docstring that has a capitalized line followed later by a deeper lower-case name; (2) a docstring whose RST directive body contains a section-name line is skipped whole, where Ruff skips only the directive body; (3) in the Google/NumPy tie-break (no Parameters or Args, equal counts) the document takes NumPy when any underlined shared header precedes a colon header, while Ruff looks only at the first decisive section; (4) Ruff's exception for a canonical nested name that matches a function parameter is not modelled.

### `ruff-d406-missing-new-line-after-section-name`

Port of Ruff's D406 (missing-new-line-after-section-name): reports, at the name, a NumPy-style docstring section header whose name is followed by a colon or trailing whitespace before the line break. It applies only when the docstring is judged NumPy-style: Parameters is present; or there is no Args and there are more NumPy-only than Google-only sections; or, on a tie, an underlined header comes before any colon header. A carriage return of a CRLF line ending is not a suffix. Divergences, all in unusual docstrings: (1) a lower-case or wrongly cased name indented deeper than the previous section is still treated as a section, though Ruff treats it as one only when it is underlined; (2) a docstring whose RST directive body contains a section-name line is skipped whole, where Ruff skips only the directive body; (3) in the Google/NumPy tie-break (no Parameters or Args, equal counts) the document takes NumPy when any underlined shared header precedes a colon header, while Ruff looks only at the first decisive section; (4) Ruff's exception for a canonical nested name that matches a function parameter is not modelled.

### `ruff-d407-missing-dashed-underline-after-section`

Port of Ruff's D407 (missing-dashed-underline-after-section): in a NumPy-style docstring, reports a section header whose first non-blank following line is not a dash underline. It reports at the name when the section is empty or is followed by text or by a mixed -/= line, and at the underline when the underline is made of = signs. Consecutive empty sections separated by blank lines are each reported. Divergences, all in unusual docstrings: (1) a lower-case or wrongly cased name indented deeper than the previous section is still treated as a section, though Ruff treats it as one only when it is underlined; (2) a docstring whose RST directive body contains a section-name line is skipped whole, where Ruff skips only the directive body; (3) in the Google/NumPy tie-break (no Parameters or Args, equal counts) the document takes NumPy when any underlined shared header precedes a colon header, while Ruff looks only at the first decisive section; (4) Ruff's exception for a canonical nested name that matches a function parameter is not modelled.

### `ruff-d408-missing-section-underline-after-name`

Port of Ruff's D408 (missing-section-underline-after-name): reports, at the dashes, a section underline separated from its section header by one or more blank lines. The header must follow a blank line or a sentence-ending line, as Ruff requires when the next line is not an underline. Divergences, all in unusual docstrings: (1) a lower-case or wrongly cased name indented deeper than the previous section is still treated as a section, though Ruff treats it as one only when it is underlined; (2) a docstring whose RST directive body contains a section-name line is skipped whole, where Ruff skips only the directive body; (3) in the Google/NumPy tie-break (no Parameters or Args, equal counts) the document takes NumPy when any underlined shared header precedes a colon header, while Ruff looks only at the first decisive section; (4) Ruff's exception for a canonical nested name that matches a function parameter is not modelled.

### `ruff-d409-mismatched-section-underline-length`

Port of Ruff's D409 (mismatched-section-underline-length): reports, at the dashes, a section underline whose length differs from the section name's. The underline is the first non-blank line after the header, made only of dashes. A header directly above its underline always counts; a header separated from it by blank lines must follow a blank line or a sentence-ending line. Divergences, all in unusual docstrings: (1) a lower-case first section after a sentence-ending line, with blank lines before its underline, is missed; (2) a lower-case or wrongly cased name indented deeper than the previous section is still treated as a section, though Ruff treats it as one only when it is underlined; (3) a docstring whose RST directive body contains a section-name line is skipped whole, where Ruff skips only the directive body; (4) in the Google/NumPy tie-break (no Parameters or Args, equal counts) the document takes NumPy when any underlined shared header precedes a colon header, while Ruff looks only at the first decisive section; (5) Ruff's exception for a canonical nested name that matches a function parameter is not modelled.

### `ruff-d410-no-blank-line-after-section`

Port of Ruff's D410 (no-blank-line-after-section): reports a section name when another section follows and the line right before that next header is not blank. Header recognition follows Ruff's is_docstring_section: a section name (convention inferred from the names present) with an optional colon alone on its line counts when the next line is a dash or equals underline, or when the line before is blank or ends in , ; . - \ / ] } ); after a previous section a lowercase header needs a blank line before it. Module, class and function docstrings only (no b/f/t prefixes, no implicit concatenation). The reported section may be capitalized after a blank line or punctuation, lowercase after a blank line, or underlined (also after plain text); the next header is one after non-blank text: capitalized after punctuation or underlined. Sections are counted from the end, so at most the last four violating sections of one docstring are reported. Divergences: a capitalized header indented deeper than the previous section counts even when Ruff would treat it as a sub-section; a later lowercase header after a blank line counts only at indentation 0-4; docstrings whose reStructuredText directive block holds a header-like line are skipped; convention inference compares section counts only up to two. A lowercase first section directly after the summary is not reported, and text holding three double quotes between two sections hides the pair.

### `ruff-d411-no-blank-line-before-section`

Port of Ruff's D411 (no-blank-line-before-section): reports a section name whose preceding line is not blank. Header recognition follows Ruff's is_docstring_section: a section name (convention inferred from the names present) with an optional colon alone on its line counts when the next line is a dash or equals underline, or when the line before is blank or ends in , ; . - \ / ] } ); after a previous section a lowercase header needs a blank line before it. Module, class and function docstrings only (no b/f/t prefixes, no implicit concatenation). A lowercase header right after text ending in punctuation is reported when it is the docstring's first section (a lazy prefix finds the first header of any form). Divergences: a capitalized header indented deeper than the previous section counts even when Ruff would treat it as a sub-section; a later lowercase header after a blank line counts only at indentation 0-4; docstrings whose reStructuredText directive block holds a header-like line are skipped; convention inference compares section counts only up to two.

### `ruff-d412-blank-lines-between-header-and-content`

Port of Ruff's D412 (blank-lines-between-header-and-content): reports a section name whose header line, or its dashed underline, is followed by one or more blank lines and then content; with a following section header after the blank lines it is an empty section instead, and before a reStructuredText directive (`.. `) one blank line is allowed. Header recognition follows Ruff's is_docstring_section: a section name (convention inferred from the names present) with an optional colon alone on its line counts when the next line is a dash or equals underline, or when the line before is blank or ends in , ; . - \ / ] } ); after a previous section a lowercase header needs a blank line before it. Module, class and function docstrings only (no b/f/t prefixes, no implicit concatenation). A lowercase first section after punctuation is handled as Ruff does. Divergences: a capitalized header indented deeper than the previous section counts even when Ruff would treat it as a sub-section; a later lowercase header after a blank line counts only at indentation 0-4; docstrings whose reStructuredText directive block holds a header-like line are skipped; convention inference compares section counts only up to two.

### `ruff-d413-missing-blank-line-after-last-section`

Port of Ruff's D413 (missing-blank-line-after-last-section): reports the last section name when the closing quotes follow its last content line without a blank line between. Header recognition follows Ruff's is_docstring_section: a section name (convention inferred from the names present) with an optional colon alone on its line counts when the next line is a dash or equals underline, or when the line before is blank or ends in , ; . - \ / ] } ); after a previous section a lowercase header needs a blank line before it. Module, class and function docstrings only (no b/f/t prefixes, no implicit concatenation). The last header is found greedily from the end of the docstring; a lowercase first section (after punctuation, or after a blank line at any indentation) counts when no other section follows. Divergences: a capitalized header indented deeper than the previous section counts even when Ruff would treat it as a sub-section; a later lowercase header after a blank line counts only at indentation 0-4; docstrings whose reStructuredText directive block holds a header-like line are skipped; convention inference compares section counts only up to two.

### `ruff-d414-empty-docstring-section`

Port of Ruff's D414 (empty-docstring-section): reports a section name followed only by blank lines (and possibly a dashed underline) up to the end of the docstring or the next section header. Header recognition follows Ruff's is_docstring_section: a section name (convention inferred from the names present) with an optional colon alone on its line counts when the next line is a dash or equals underline, or when the line before is blank or ends in , ; . - \ / ] } ); after a previous section a lowercase header needs a blank line before it. Module, class and function docstrings only (no b/f/t prefixes, no implicit concatenation). A lowercase first section (after punctuation, or after a blank line at any indentation) is handled as Ruff does. Divergences: a capitalized header indented deeper than the previous section counts even when Ruff would treat it as a sub-section; a later lowercase header after a blank line counts only at indentation 0-4; docstrings whose reStructuredText directive block holds a header-like line are skipped; convention inference compares section counts only up to two.

### `ruff-d415-missing-terminal-punctuation`

Port of Ruff's D415 (missing-terminal-punctuation): reports a module, class or function docstring whose first logical line does not end in a period, question mark or exclamation point, anchored at the whole string literal (prefix and quotes included, so multi-line docstrings end where the string ends). A docstring is a single plain, `r` or `u` string (single or triple quoted, optionally parenthesized) that is the first statement of the module, class or function body (comments before it are fine); f-strings, t-strings, bytes, implicit concatenations and strings that are not the first statement are not docstrings, as in Ruff. Lines break at `\n`, `\r\n` or a lone `\r`, as Ruff's universal newlines do. The first logical line is the first run of non-blank lines, ignoring lines made only of `-`, `~`, `=` or `#`; as in Ruff, the verdict is on that run's last line, so a wrapped summary is judged where it ends. Exempt as in Ruff: a first line starting with `:param`, `:type`, `:raises`, `:return` or `:rtype` (so `:ref:` or `:class:` summaries are still checked), a first line that is exactly a section name or a colon followed by one, case-sensitive (`Returns`, `:Returns`, `Keyword Args`, but not `Returns:`, `NOTE` or `See  Also`, which Ruff also reports), a logical line ending in a backslash (single-quoted continuation), and empty docstrings (left to the empty-docstring rule). A trailing `:` or `;`, `)`, a closing quote or non-ASCII punctuation such as `。` or `…` still counts as missing punctuation. No known divergence; the not-overfit gate lists the section names as hardcoded words only because its recording of Ruff's names omits crates/ruff_linter/src/docstrings/sections.rs.

### `ruff-d416-missing-section-name-colon`

Port of Ruff's D416 (missing-section-name-colon): under Ruff's default settings (no `convention`), reports the section name of a Google-style docstring section whose header line is the bare name or the name plus a colon followed by trailing whitespace (`Args`, `Returns `, `Args:  `; `Args:` and `Args :` are not reported, the latter is not a section header at all), anchored at exactly the name (`Keyword Args` is one name), one finding per header. Section names are Ruff's Google list matched case-insensitively (Args, Arguments, Attention, Attributes, Caution, Danger, Error, Example, Examples, Hint, Important, Keyword Args/Arguments, Methods, Note, Notes, Other Args/Arguments, Raises, References, Return, Returns, See Also, Tip, Todo, Warning, Warnings, Warns, Yield, Yields). A header counts only as Ruff's section detection allows: it is alone on its line, not on the docstring's first line, and either the next line is an underline of `-` or `=`, or the previous line is blank or ends in one of `, ; . - \\ / ] } )` (the first line counts as previous line for the second one). Single-quoted docstrings continued with backslashes are scanned too. A header starting with a lowercase letter (`returns`, `error`) is a subsection, not a section, as in Ruff, when the docstring has an earlier section header and the lowercase header follows a non-blank line or is indented deeper than that earlier header (so a word such as `error` in a list of values under `Args:` is not reported); this is judged per docstring, so a docstring holding such a subsection reports none of its lowercase headers. The docstring must be a single plain `r`/`u` string that is the first statement of a module, class or function, parenthesized or not. Style is decided as Ruff does: any NumPy `Parameters`/`Other Params`/`Other Parameters` header makes it NumPy (silent); otherwise an Args-like header, or any Google-only name (Example, Note, Todo, Return, ...) without a NumPy-only name, makes it Google; with only names both styles share it is Google when a colon-suffixed header exists and no dash-underlined header does. Underlined headers in a Google docstring (for example `Example` over dashes, as in Ruff's sphinx.py) get a separate message about dropping the underline. Divergences, all rare shapes: RST directive bodies are not skipped; the first-decisive-section order of the tie-break; NumPy-only names winning over any number of Google-only names; uppercase non-canonical headers (`TODO`, `NOTE`) and parameter-named headers indented deeper than the previous section are still reported; Unicode case folding (`ſ` matches `s`); bare CR line endings.

### `ruff-d419-empty-docstring`

Port of Ruff's D419 (empty-docstring): reports a module, class or function docstring whose text between the quotes is empty or only whitespace (`""""""`, `"   "`, `'''\n'''`, `r""`, `u""`, including Unicode whitespace such as no-break space and ideographic space, but not zero-width space or BOM), anchored at the whole string literal including prefix and quotes. A docstring is a single plain, `r` or `u` string (optionally parenthesized, with comments around it) that is the first statement of the module, class or function body; f-strings, t-strings, bytes, implicit concatenations (`"" ""`, which Ruff skips with a warning) and strings that are not the first statement are not docstrings. A string whose body is only an escape or a backslash continuation (`"\n"`, a backslash before a newline) is not empty, as in Ruff, which judges the source text. `.pyi` stubs are checked too. Docstrings of every definition count, including ones nested in `if`, `try`, `with` and `match` blocks, one-line bodies (`class A: ""`) and files with lone carriage-return line endings. No known divergence; 31 probe files agree with live Ruff finding for finding.

### `ruff-dtz001-call-datetime-without-tzinfo`

Port of Ruff's DTZ001 (call-datetime-without-tzinfo): a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves to `datetime.datetime` through the file's imports (Ruff's `resolve_qualified_name`), unless the call (parentheses transparent) is followed by zero or more `.replace(...)` calls and then an `.astimezone` attribute (Ruff's `followed_by_astimezone`; the attribute need not be called), and whose `tzinfo` argument (Ruff's `find_argument_value("tzinfo", 7)`: the `tzinfo=` keyword, else positional argument 7 among those before the first starred one; `**kwargs` passes nothing) is missing or is the literal `None` (parentheses transparent). Reported on the call, with a separate message for each case (no `tzinfo` argument, `tzinfo=None`) written as guidance for a coding agent rather than Ruff's wording.

### `ruff-dtz002-call-datetime-today`

Port of Ruff's DTZ002 (call-datetime-today): a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves to `datetime.datetime.today` through the file's imports (Ruff's `resolve_qualified_name`), unless the call (parentheses transparent) is followed by zero or more `.replace(...)` calls and then an `.astimezone` attribute (Ruff's `followed_by_astimezone`; the attribute need not be called), reported on the call.

### `ruff-dtz003-call-datetime-utcnow`

Port of Ruff's DTZ003 (call-datetime-utcnow): a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves to `datetime.datetime.utcnow` through the file's imports (Ruff's `resolve_qualified_name`), unless the call (parentheses transparent) is followed by zero or more `.replace(...)` calls and then an `.astimezone` attribute (Ruff's `followed_by_astimezone`; the attribute need not be called), reported on the call.

### `ruff-dtz004-call-datetime-utcfromtimestamp`

Port of Ruff's DTZ004 (call-datetime-utcfromtimestamp): a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves to `datetime.datetime.utcfromtimestamp` through the file's imports (Ruff's `resolve_qualified_name`), unless the call (parentheses transparent) is followed by zero or more `.replace(...)` calls and then an `.astimezone` attribute (Ruff's `followed_by_astimezone`; the attribute need not be called), reported on the call.

### `ruff-dtz005-call-datetime-now-without-tzinfo`

Port of Ruff's DTZ005 (call-datetime-now-without-tzinfo): a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves to `datetime.datetime.now` through the file's imports (Ruff's `resolve_qualified_name`), unless the call (parentheses transparent) is followed by zero or more `.replace(...)` calls and then an `.astimezone` attribute (Ruff's `followed_by_astimezone`; the attribute need not be called), and whose `tz` argument (Ruff's `find_argument_value("tz", 0)`: the `tz=` keyword, else positional argument 0 among those before the first starred one; `**kwargs` passes nothing) is missing or is the literal `None` (parentheses transparent). Reported on the call, with a separate message for each case (no `tz` argument, `tz=None`) written as guidance for a coding agent rather than Ruff's wording.

### `ruff-dtz006-call-datetime-fromtimestamp`

Port of Ruff's DTZ006 (call-datetime-fromtimestamp): a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves to `datetime.datetime.fromtimestamp` through the file's imports (Ruff's `resolve_qualified_name`), unless the call (parentheses transparent) is followed by zero or more `.replace(...)` calls and then an `.astimezone` attribute (Ruff's `followed_by_astimezone`; the attribute need not be called), and whose `tz` argument (Ruff's `find_argument_value("tz", 1)`: the `tz=` keyword, else positional argument 1 among those before the first starred one; `**kwargs` passes nothing) is missing or is the literal `None` (parentheses transparent). Reported on the call, with Ruff's message for each case.

### `ruff-dtz007-call-datetime-strptime-without-zone`

Port of Ruff's DTZ007 (call-datetime-strptime-without-zone): a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves to `datetime.datetime.strptime` through the file's imports (Ruff's `resolve_qualified_name`), unless the call (parentheses transparent) is followed by zero or more `.replace(...)` calls and then an `.astimezone` attribute (Ruff's `followed_by_astimezone`), or its second positional argument (comments aside, starred ones counted) is a str literal whose decoded value contains `%z` or an f-string one of whose literal parts contains `%z`. Reported on the call when the call is not the object of a `.replace` attribute whose enclosing expression (Ruff's grandparent: through parentheses and the argument list) is a call passing a `tzinfo=` keyword, and with the `tz=None` message when that keyword is the literal `None`.

### `ruff-dtz011-call-date-today`

Port of Ruff's DTZ011 (call-date-today): a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves to `datetime.date.today` through the file's imports (Ruff's `resolve_qualified_name`), reported on the call.

### `ruff-dtz012-call-date-fromtimestamp`

Port of Ruff's DTZ012 (call-date-fromtimestamp): a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves to `datetime.date.fromtimestamp` through the file's imports (Ruff's `resolve_qualified_name`), reported on the call.

### `ruff-dtz901-datetime-min-max`

Port of Ruff's DTZ901 (datetime-min-max): an attribute expression (any context) resolving to `datetime.datetime.min` or `datetime.datetime.max` through the file's imports (Ruff's `resolve_qualified_name`), reported on it, unless it is (parentheses transparent) the object of a `.time` attribute, or of a `.replace` attribute, whose enclosing expression (Ruff's grandparent: through parentheses, the argument list, a keyword or `**` argument) is a call; for `.replace` that call must also pass a `tzinfo=` keyword that is not the literal `None`.

### `ruff-e401-multiple-imports-on-one-line`

Port of Ruff's E401 (multiple-imports-on-one-line): reports every plain `import` statement that names two or more modules, dotted (`import os.path, sys`) or `as`-aliased (`import re as regex, string`). It never reports `from ... import` statements, however many names they list. Like Ruff's `names.len() > 1` test, it counts the imported names (dotted names and aliased imports), not the commas, so a single name with a stray trailing comma is not reported, and neither is a backslash line continuation before the first name (`import \` followed by `os` on the next line). The whole statement is reported, from the `import` keyword to the end of the last name. A trailing comment is not part of the span. It fires at any nesting depth: inside function, async function, class and method bodies, and in one-line compound statements such as `if x: import a, b` or `try: import a, b`. On `;`-separated lines it reports each multi-name import on its own. It also catches statements split across backslash continuations, and there a `# noqa: E401` on the last physical line suppresses them, as Ruff does. Divergences come from the engine, not the matcher. The parser has no Python 3.15 `lazy import` syntax, so for `lazy import os, sys` the finding starts at `import` rather than at `lazy`. Ruff's range suppressions (`# ruff: disable[E401]` ... `# ruff: enable[E401]`) are not honoured. Jupyter notebooks are not checked. A file that CPython cannot parse but tree-sitter accepts still gets findings where Ruff reports none, because Ruff skips lint rules on a file with a parse error. Two examples are a trailing comma in `import a, b,` and a form feed inside an indentation, which resets the column.

### `ruff-em101-raw-string-in-exception`

Port of Ruff's EM101 (raw-string-in-exception): reports a plain string literal or bytes literal passed as the first positional argument of a call that is the raised exception of a `raise` statement (`raise ValueError("...")`). This includes an implicit concatenation of plain strings and any prefix other than f or t. Only the exception expression counts, never the `from` cause, and it is found even after a backslash continuation following `raise`. Parentheses around the raised call, the callee and the argument are unwrapped, and the span is the literal itself without its parentheses. Only the first positional argument counts: keyword arguments, a later positional argument, a starred first argument and a generator argument are never checked. Calls whose callee resolves to `cast` from `typing`, `typing_extensions` or `_typeshed` are skipped; this works through plain imports, `from` imports, aliases and a parenthesized callee. A shadowed, rebound, deleted or unimported `cast` is still reported, and so is a relative import named `typing`, as in Ruff. f-strings and t-strings are not reported here. Divergences: `lint.flake8-errmsg.max-string-length` is not configurable and stays at Ruff's default of 0, so empty strings are reported (Ruff's `custom` test with length 20 is not ported). `lint.typing-modules` is not honoured, so only the three built-in typing modules' `cast` is skipped.

### `ruff-em102-f-string-in-exception`

Port of Ruff's EM102 (f-string-in-exception): reports an f-string passed as the first positional argument of a call that is the raised exception of a `raise` statement. Any prefix combination counts, such as `F`, `rf`, `Fr` or `fR`, and so does an implicit concatenation with at least one f-string part. Only the exception expression counts, never the `from` cause, and it is found even after a backslash continuation following `raise`. Parentheses around the raised call, the callee and the argument are unwrapped, and the span is the f-string or concatenation itself. Only the first positional argument counts: keyword arguments, later positional arguments, a starred first argument and a lambda or walrus wrapping the f-string are never checked. t-strings are not reported. Calls whose callee resolves to `cast` from `typing`, `typing_extensions` or `_typeshed`, through imports and aliases, are skipped; a shadowed, rebound or unimported `cast` is still reported. Divergence: `lint.typing-modules` is not honoured, so only those three modules' `cast` is skipped.

### `ruff-em103-dot-format-in-exception`

Port of Ruff's EM103 (dot-format-in-exception): reports a `.format(...)` call on a literal receiver passed as the first positional argument of a call that is the raised exception of a `raise` statement. Only the exception expression counts, never the `from` cause, and it is found even after a backslash continuation following `raise`. The `.format` attribute may be parenthesized, as in `("{}".format)(x)`, because Ruff sees through those parentheses. A literal receiver is a plain or bytes string (including an implicit concatenation of plain strings), an integer, float or complex number in any base, `True`, `False`, `None` or `...`, with any parentheses around it unwrapped. An f-string, t-string, name, attribute, subscript, tuple, `__debug__` or signed-number receiver is not reported. The span is the whole `.format(...)` call, starting at the first opening parenthesis of the receiver or callee when it is parenthesized. A chained call after `.format(...)`, a bare `.format` attribute, `format_map`, keyword arguments, later positional arguments and the `from` cause are not reported. Calls whose callee resolves to `cast` from `typing`, `typing_extensions` or `_typeshed`, through imports and aliases, are skipped. Divergence: `lint.typing-modules` is not honoured, so only those three modules' `cast` is skipped.

### `ruff-era001-commented-out-code`

Port of Ruff's ERA001 (commented-out-code): reports an own-line comment (only whitespace before the '#': a comment whose nearest preceding token or comment sits on an earlier line, or that has none) whose text, after stripping leading '#', space, tab and form feed and trailing Unicode whitespace, contains one of ( ) [ ] { } : = % return break continue import; is not an allowlisted directive (Ruff's allowlist, matched as a prefix with no word boundary, so '# type: ignored = 1' is skipped as Ruff skips it); does not start with a task tag (TODO, FIXME, XXX followed by space, colon, parenthesis or end); has no '#' followed by a Unicode digit; has no two consecutive names as Ruff's SimpleTokenizer sees them (keywords, including soft ones and pass/break/continue, are not names; None/True/False are; it stops reading at a number, a string, or any character it does not know); and then either ends in a backslash, matches one of Ruff's four partial-code shapes (elif/else/try/finally/except/case lines, a partial dictionary entry, a multiline assignment opener, a bracket-only line; Unicode word characters as in Rust's regex), or parses as a Python module. The parse is emulated by regex passes: strings by prefix (valid prefixes only, escapes checked for str and bytes, bytes ASCII-only, f/t-string fields delimited with !r/!s/!a and nested format specs), numbers with Python's underscore rules, lambda parameter lists, then five passes that reduce the innermost brackets by kind (call arguments with positional, keyword and unpacking order; parentheses; def parameters with **kwargs last; type-parameter lists; subscripts with slices; lists; dicts and sets; comprehensions), and a final statement grammar (assignments and targets, augmented and annotated assignment, imports including lazy ones, del, return, raise, compound headers with a body on the same line, type aliases, def/class with type parameters). Comments inside a closed PEP 723 '# /// script' block are skipped, only in files that have a '# /// script' line at column 0. Reports the comment's span; '# noqa: ERA001' suppresses. Divergences: bracket nesting deeper than five levels, an indented script-block start in a file that also has a column-0 one, files with syntax errors (nothing reported), and a few invalid lines the emulation accepts (non-target contents of a parenthesised or bracketed assignment target, a non-default parameter after a default, unknown \N{} names, mixed bytes/str or t-string concatenation, unparsed f-string field expressions) or valid ones it misses ('(x): int = 1', a lambda default that is a lambda).

### `ruff-fa100-future-rewritable-type-annotation`

Port of Ruff's FA100 (future-rewritable-type-annotation): in a non-stub module with no `from __future__ import annotations` earlier in the file (an aliased import such as `annotations as ann` and a parenthesized name list count; one nested in a block only covers the rest of that block, as in Ruff), reports typing names inside annotations (parameter, return and annotated-assignment annotations, including names inside quoted annotations, but not strings inside `Literal[...]`, `Annotated` metadata or call arguments) that could be written more succinctly under PEP 563. Two clauses, each gated on the configured Python version as Ruff gates on target-version. The PEP 585 clause, at Python 3.7 and 3.8, reports every reference, bare or subscripted and through any import alias or `typing.` attribute, to the typing aliases Dict, FrozenSet, List, Set, Tuple, Type, Deque and DefaultDict (and typing_extensions Type, Deque and DefaultDict). The PEP 604 clause, at Python 3.7 to 3.9, reports the head of an Optional[...] or Union[...] subscript from typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed, and skips subscripts with a starred element and, unless inside an `if TYPE_CHECKING:` block (the test may be parenthesized, aliased or `x.TYPE_CHECKING`), an annotated assignment in a function body or a quoted annotation, subscripts with a quoted element. Names that are shadowed, not yet imported at that point, or not from the typing modules are not reported. Stub files are excluded by a file-path condition because Ruff treats them as future-annotations files, so scope stays `**/*`. The span is the name or attribute chain, as in Ruff. The document is silent at the engine default of Python 3.10, as Ruff is, and fires only when the host sends pythonVersion 3.9 or lower. Divergences: complex quoted annotations (escapes or implicit concatenation) hit a Ruff quirk where the Optional/Union head is reported only if the last name resolved inside the string is a head, which the document does not model, and findings with identical spans collapse to one; `lazy from __future__ import annotations` and a chained subscript in annotation position (`Optional[List][int]`) do not parse in the engine.

### `ruff-fa102-future-required-type-annotation`

Port of Ruff's FA102 (future-required-type-annotation): in a non-stub module with no `from __future__ import annotations` earlier in the file (aliased or parenthesized imports count; one nested in a block only covers the rest of that block), reports PEP 585 and PEP 604 syntax inside annotations that Python evaluates at runtime. Runtime-evaluated annotations are parameter and return annotations of any def (at any nesting depth) and annotated assignments whose nearest enclosing scope is the module or a class (module-level `if TYPE_CHECKING:` blocks, try, with, for and match blocks do not change that). They do not include annotated assignments inside function bodies, anything inside quoted annotations, or the runtime-required cases Ruff exempts: non-variadic parameters and the return annotation of a `@fun.register` implementation of a functools singledispatch or singledispatchmethod function, and ClassVar, InitVar or KW_ONLY annotations (parenthesized or not) directly in a dataclass-decorated class. PEP 585 clause (Python 3.8 and lower): reports the whole subscript (`list[int]`, `collections.deque[int]`, `(list)[int]`) when its head resolves, through any import alias or attribute chain and respecting shadowing and binding order, to a builtin generic (dict, frozenset, list, set, tuple, type) or one of the collections, collections.abc, asyncio, contextlib, dataclasses, functools, os, queue, re, shelve, types and weakref generics Ruff lists; no typing import is required. PEP 604 clause (Python 3.9 and lower): reports every `|` expression (each nested BinOp, including inside Literal, Annotated, Callable and call arguments). The tree-sitter grammar parses `list[int] | str | None` as the subscript plus one right-hand `str | None` node, so two more clauses recover Ruff's inner spans (`list[int] | str`, `list[int] | str | float`) with a regex over the union's text that skips bracketed and quoted operands. Stub files are excluded by a file-path condition and the scope stays `**/*`. Spans are the whole subscript or `|` node, as in Ruff. The document is silent at the engine default of Python 3.10 and fires only when the host sends pythonVersion 3.9 or lower (3.8 or lower for PEP 585). Divergences: in chains of five or more operands starting with a subscript, inner spans of four or more operands are missed, and so are the inner spans of chains whose operands nest brackets more than two levels deep; `@C.m.register` implementations reached through a class attribute are not exempted; `lazy from __future__ import annotations` and `list[int][int]` in annotation position do not parse in the engine.

### `ruff-fast001-fast-api-redundant-response-model`

Port of Ruff's FAST001 (fast-api-redundant-response-model): reports the `response_model=` keyword argument (the whole `name=value` keyword, first one only) of a FastAPI route decorator when the decorated function's return annotation names the same type. A route decorator is a call of `.get`, `.post`, `.put`, `.delete`, `.patch`, `.options`, `.head` or `.trace` on a name that resolves to a `fastapi.FastAPI` or `fastapi.APIRouter` instance, whether bound by assignment (including chained, unpacked and walrus assignments), `with ... as`, a parameter or variable annotation, or a function's return annotation, through aliases and parentheses; every route decorator of the function is checked. Two types are identical, as in Ruff, when they are names resolving to the same binding (same spelling, not a type parameter of the function itself), subscripts whose value and slice are identical, or tuples of the same length with identical elements, with parentheses, comments, white space and trailing commas after two or more elements ignored (`Dict[(str, Item)]` equals `Dict[str, Item,]`; `List[Item,]` does not equal `List[Item]`); attribute access (`models.Item`), strings, literals, unions written with `|` and names Ruff cannot resolve never match. Stub files report nothing, and neither does a file with a `from __future__ import annotations` (aliased or not), where Ruff has not yet resolved the annotation's names; other future imports do not matter. Divergences from Ruff: types containing a single-element tuple or a tuple nested in another tuple are equated only when spelled with identical tokens, and a return annotation the grammar cannot parse (`-> List[Item][int]`) leaves the file unchecked.

### `ruff-fast002-fast-api-non-annotated-dependency`

Port of Ruff's FAST002 (fast-api-non-annotated-dependency): reports a parameter (the whole parameter, name through default) of a function decorated by a FastAPI route decorator (`.get`, `.post`, `.put`, `.delete`, `.patch`, `.options`, `.head`, `.trace` on a name resolving to `fastapi.FastAPI` or `fastapi.APIRouter`; any of the function's decorators may be the route one) when the parameter has both an annotation and a default that is a bare reference to, or a call of, `fastapi.Query`, `Path`, `Body`, `Cookie`, `Header`, `File`, `Form`, `Depends` or `Security`, resolved through imports and aliases, with parentheses ignored. Positional-only parameters, `*args`/`**kwargs`, parameters without an annotation and defaults that merely contain a marker (`[Query()][0]`, `Query() or None`, `Query()()`) are not reported. Two clauses give different advice: parameter markers (`Query`, `Path`, ...) keep their default argument as the plain default after the move into `Annotated`, while `Depends`/`Security` move whole into `Annotated`. Ruff's availability condition on `typing_extensions` for targets before 3.9 is not modelled (the default target is newer). Markers are resolved at the point of the definition, so a module-level rebinding after a nested route function (which Ruff reads once the module is done) hides the marker, as in Ruff. No divergence from Ruff was found over Ruff's three FAST002 fixtures and 29 live-Ruff probe files.

### `ruff-fast003-fast-api-unused-path-parameter`

Port of Ruff's FAST003 (fast-api-unused-path-parameter): for a function whose first FastAPI route decorator (a `.get`, `.post`, `.put`, `.delete`, `.patch`, `.options`, `.head` or `.trace` call on a name resolving to `fastapi.FastAPI` or `fastapi.APIRouter`) has a plain string literal as its first positional argument, reports every `{name}` or `{name:converter}` path parameter (Starlette's pattern: an ASCII identifier, optionally a converter identifier, no spaces, `!r` or `=`) that the function does not declare. The string is read as a `str.format` template and each matching field is reported over exactly its braces, once per occurrence. A path parameter counts as declared when a regular parameter (not positional-only, not `*args`/`**kwargs`) has that name, or that alias in `Annotated[T, Path(alias="...")]` with `Path` as the second element (the alias replaces the parameter's own name), or when a dependency names it: the default `Depends(...)` when it names a function or class (or calls one), otherwise the single resolvable `Depends` among the `Annotated` metadata (`Depends()` takes the class from the first element). A function contributes its non-positional-only parameters, a class its `__init__` parameters after the first non-positional-only one (or its annotated fields when it derives from `pydantic.BaseModel`), and an instance `Depends(Cls(...))` its class's `__call__` parameters, when the name is bound once in the file; a dependency the file cannot read (an import, a variable, a parameter, a name bound twice, a function called as an instance) or two resolvable `Depends` in one `Annotated` silences the whole route, as in Ruff; `Depends(lambda: ...)`, `Depends(obj.attr)` and `Depends()` with nothing to read are ignored. Comments inside `Annotated[...]` do not shift element positions. One clause covers both a missing parameter and a positional-only one, since both are fixed by declaring a non-positional-only parameter. Divergences from Ruff: escaped (`{{a}}`) or unbalanced braces, the report range of prefixed, triple-quoted or implicitly concatenated path strings, alias strings that are concatenated or prefixed, and parenthesised defaults, annotations or metadata.

### `ruff-fbt001-boolean-type-hint-positional-argument`

Port of Ruff's FBT001 (boolean-type-hint-positional-argument): reports the name of each positional-only or positional-or-keyword parameter of a `def` or `async def` (any parameter before `*` or `*args`) whose annotation is boolean. Boolean annotations are `bool`, the string `"bool"` in any non-f-string form (prefixes, escapes such as `"b\x6fol"` and implicit concatenation included), a `|` union with a boolean member, `Union[...]` with a tuple slice that has a boolean member (parenthesized tuples, starred members and a trailing comma included; `Union[bool]` does not count), and `Optional[...]` of a single boolean annotation. All of these are read through parentheses. `Union` and `Optional` resolve through imports from `typing`, `typing_extensions` or `_typeshed`; a local alias such as `U = Union` does not count. The rule skips `.pyi` stubs, lambdas, keyword-only and variadic parameters, `__post_init__` and the operator dunder methods (`__add__`, `__radd__`, `__iadd__`, `__eq__`, `__getitem__`, `__contains__` and so on). It also skips functions decorated with `@override` from `typing`, `typing_extensions` or `_typeshed`. It skips functions decorated with `@<own name>.setter` too, reading the object through parentheses: `@(foo).setter` counts, but `@x.setter` on a function named `setter`, `@self.foo.setter`, `@(foo.bar).setter` and `@foo.setter()` do not. Annotations whose `bool` is not the builtin do not count (after `from numpy import bool`, `bool = int`, `from builtins import bool` or an enclosing parameter named `bool`). A `del bool` also silences the rule, as it does in Ruff. Functions named `bool` are skipped, because Ruff finds the function's own binding of `bool` in scope. A def nested in a function sees the module's final bindings, as in Ruff, so a later module-level `bool = int` silences both. Divergences: (1) Ruff checks that `bool` is the builtin where the def stands, but the port resolves the annotation's own `bool`. With deferred annotations (string annotations, or `from __future__ import annotations`), a rebinding of `bool` later in the same scope therefore silences the port while Ruff still reports. (2) For `def f[bool](a: bool)`, the port reads the type parameter and stays silent, while Ruff checks the enclosing scope and reports. An annotation that subscripts a generic (`Union[bool, int][0]`) is a tree-sitter-python syntax error, and such a file is not evaluated.

### `ruff-fbt002-boolean-default-value-positional-argument`

Port of Ruff's FBT002 (boolean-default-value-positional-argument): reports the name of each positional-only or positional-or-keyword parameter of a `def` or `async def` (any parameter before `*` or `*args`) whose default is the literal `True` or `False`, read through parentheses, whatever the parameter's annotation. The rule skips `.pyi` stubs, lambdas, keyword-only and variadic parameters, `__post_init__` and the operator dunder methods (`__add__`, `__radd__`, `__iadd__`, `__eq__`, `__getitem__`, `__contains__` and so on). Other dunders such as `__divmod__`, `__abs__` and `__bool__` still report. It also skips functions decorated with `@<own name>.setter`, reading the object through parentheses, so `@(foo).setter` and `@((foo)).setter` count. `@other.setter`, `@self.name.setter`, `@(foo.bar).setter`, `@name.setter()` and `@x.setter` on a function named `setter` do not count as setters. Functions decorated with `@override` from `typing`, `typing_extensions` or `_typeshed` are skipped as well, through any import form, attribute or parentheses. A class attribute named `override` that shadows the import does not count.

### `ruff-fbt003-boolean-positional-value-in-call`

Port of Ruff's FBT003 (boolean-positional-value-in-call): reports each `True` or `False` literal passed as a positional argument to a call, read through parentheses (the span excludes them), in decorators, f-string and t-string fields and stubs too. Keyword values, starred or nested expressions, generator arguments, class bases and match class patterns do not count. Calls inside strings Ruff reads as type definitions are checked as well. That covers parameter, return, variable and attribute annotation strings, `cast`, `TypeVar` and `NewType` string arguments, `type X = "..."` values, and strings nested in a string annotation (Annotated metadata strings included). It does not cover f-strings, strings inside `Literal[...]` or plain strings. Such a finding sits at the literal's exact position when the string decodes one to one. It spans the whole string when the string has an escape or implicit concatenation. A callee named by an identifier or an attribute, parentheses and comments included, is exempt in three cases, inside strings too. (1) Its last name is on Ruff's allowed list (`get`, `setdefault`, `pop`, `getattr`, `setattr`, `int`, `str`, `append`, `index`, `assertEqual`, `param`, `next`, `coalesce` and so on). Only the attribute's own name counts, so `get.x(True)` reports. (2) The name is `set`, or `set` followed by `_` or an upper-case ASCII letter, and the call has exactly one positional argument (starred arguments count, keywords do not). (3) The callee resolves through imports to `multiprocessing.Value` (a rebinding or a parameter of that name does not count). The same import resolution applies to qualified names listed in the `flake8-boolean-trap.extend-allowed-calls` option, which the probes could not exercise because they run Ruff with defaults. Allowed names match by text, as Ruff matches them, so a local function named `get` is exempt too. Callees that are calls, subscripts or lambdas get no exemption.

### `ruff-fix001-line-contains-fixme`

Port of Ruff's FIX001 (line-contains-fixme): reads every `#` comment the way Ruff's `TodoDirective::from_comment` does. It walks the comment's `#`-delimited segments in order. For each one it skips leading `#` characters and then Unicode whitespace, as Rust's `trim_start` does: U+0085 and U+3000 count as whitespace, U+FEFF and U+200B do not. Then it takes the first run of alphanumeric characters (Unicode `Alphabetic` or numeric, the same test as Rust's `char::is_alphanumeric`). The first segment whose word is `fixme`, `todo`, `hack` or `xxx`, in any case, decides the comment. This rule reports only when that first word is `fixme`, and the finding covers just the five characters of the tag. So `# TODO # FIXME` is silent here, while `# noqa:E501 # FIXME`, `# x # y # z # FIXME`, `#FIXME:x` and `# a#fixme` report. The word must end at a non-alphanumeric character: `# FIXMEPLEASE`, `# FIXME2`, `# fixmes` and `# éFIXME` stay silent, while `# FIXME?` and `# FIXME_x` report. An empty first word, as in `#: FIXME` or `# :FIXME`, ends the segment. Strings, docstrings and f-string format specs (`f"{x:#x}"`) are never comments. A comment inside a multi-line f-string replacement field is a comment. These suppress the finding: `# noqa: FIX001`, a blanket `# noqa` anywhere in the comment (so `# noqa # FIXME` stays silent), `# ruff: noqa: FIX001`, and `# flake8: noqa`. Matches Ruff on its own tests and on 37 probe files, with identical start and end positions. The probes cover multiple `#` segments, code and f-string contexts, Unicode words, whitespace and control characters, astral characters before the tag, BOM files, CR-only and CRLF line endings, noqa and file-exemption forms, match and type statements, stubs, and end of file without a newline.

### `ruff-fix002-line-contains-todo`

Port of Ruff's FIX002 (line-contains-todo): reads every `#` comment the way Ruff's `TodoDirective::from_comment` does. It walks the comment's `#`-delimited segments in order. For each one it skips leading `#` characters and then Unicode whitespace, as Rust's `trim_start` does: U+0085 and U+3000 count as whitespace, U+FEFF and U+200B do not. Then it takes the first run of alphanumeric characters (Unicode `Alphabetic` or numeric, as Rust's `char::is_alphanumeric`). The first segment whose word is `fixme`, `todo`, `hack` or `xxx`, in any case, decides the comment. This rule reports only when that first word is `todo`, and the finding covers just the four characters of the tag. So `# FIXME # TODO` is silent here, while `# foo # TODO`, `## TODO`, `#TODO`, `# TODO(user):`, `# TODO-x` and `# TODO_x` report. The word must end at a non-alphanumeric character: `# TODOs`, `# TODO1`, `# TODOé`, `# éTODO`, `# TODO²` and `# TODOⅫ` stay silent, while `# TODO→`, `#　TODO` (ideographic space) and `#\u0085TODO` report. An empty first word ends the segment, as in `#: TODO`, `# %% TODO` or `#﻿TODO`. Strings, docstrings and f-string format specs are never comments. A comment inside a multi-line f-string replacement field is a comment. These suppress the finding: `# noqa: FIX002`, a blanket `# noqa` anywhere in the comment, `# ruff: noqa: FIX002` (even after a tag in the same comment), and `# flake8: noqa`. `# TODO: noqa` is not a suppression and reports. Matches Ruff on its own tests and on 37 probe files, with identical start and end positions. The probes cover multiple `#` segments, code and f-string contexts, Unicode words, whitespace and control characters, astral characters before the tag, BOM files, CR-only and CRLF line endings, noqa and file-exemption forms, match and type statements, stubs, and end of file without a newline.

### `ruff-fix003-line-contains-xxx`

Port of Ruff's FIX003 (line-contains-xxx): reads every `#` comment the way Ruff's `TodoDirective::from_comment` does. It walks the comment's `#`-delimited segments in order. For each one it skips leading `#` characters and then Unicode whitespace, as Rust's `trim_start` does. Then it takes the first run of alphanumeric characters (Unicode `Alphabetic` or numeric, as Rust's `char::is_alphanumeric`). The first segment whose word is `fixme`, `todo`, `hack` or `xxx`, in any case, decides the comment. This rule reports only when that first word is `xxx`, and the finding covers just the three characters of the tag. So `# HACK # XXX` is silent here, while `# noqa:E501 # XXX`, `#### XXX`, `# xXx`, `# XXX:`, `# xxx-yyy` and `# XX#XXX` report. The word must end at a non-alphanumeric character: `# XXXX`, `# XXX123`, `# XXXFIXME`, `# XXX①` and `# xx` stay silent, and `# xxxx # xxx` reports on the second segment. Cyrillic `ххх` and fullwidth letters are not the tag. Strings, docstrings and f-string format specs are never comments. These suppress the finding: `# noqa: FIX003`, a blanket `# noqa` anywhere in the comment (so `# noqa # XXX` stays silent), `# ruff: noqa: FIX003`, and `# flake8: noqa`. Matches Ruff on its own tests and on 37 probe files, with identical start and end positions. The probes cover multiple `#` segments, code and f-string contexts, Unicode words, whitespace and control characters, astral characters before the tag, BOM files, CR-only and CRLF line endings, noqa and file-exemption forms, match and type statements, stubs, and end of file without a newline.

### `ruff-fix004-line-contains-hack`

Port of Ruff's FIX004 (line-contains-hack): reads every `#` comment the way Ruff's `TodoDirective::from_comment` does. It walks the comment's `#`-delimited segments in order. For each one it skips leading `#` characters and then Unicode whitespace, as Rust's `trim_start` does. Then it takes the first run of alphanumeric characters (Unicode `Alphabetic` or numeric, as Rust's `char::is_alphanumeric`). The first segment whose word is `fixme`, `todo`, `hack` or `xxx`, in any case, decides the comment. This rule reports only when that first word is `hack`, and the finding covers just the four characters of the tag. So `# XXX # HACK` is silent here, while `# a#b#hack`, `#HACK!`, `# hack-around`, `# HACK_`, `# HACḰ` (a combining mark that is not Alphabetic) and `# hacker # HACK` report. The word must end at a non-alphanumeric character: `# HACKY`, `# hacks` and `# HAC` stay silent. Strings, docstrings and f-string format specs are never comments. These suppress the finding: `# noqa: FIX004`, a blanket `# noqa` anywhere in the comment, `# ruff: noqa: FIX004`, and `# flake8: noqa`. The port matches the tag in ASCII case only. Ruff lowercases with Unicode rules, so it reads `HAC` followed by a Kelvin sign (U+212A) as `hack`. It then slices a 4-byte range that ends inside that 3-byte character and panics (directives.rs:321), linting nothing in the file. This port stays silent there. Matches Ruff on its own tests and on 37 probe files, with identical start and end positions. The probes cover multiple `#` segments, code and f-string contexts, Unicode words, whitespace and control characters, astral characters before the tag, BOM files, CR-only and CRLF line endings, noqa and file-exemption forms, match and type statements, stubs, and end of file without a newline.

### `ruff-fly002-static-join-to-f-string`

Port of Ruff's FLY002 (static-join-to-f-string): reports the whole call `<str literal>.join(<arg>)` whose joiner is a plain string literal: any `r`/`u` prefix, implicit concatenation or parentheses, but not bytes, f-strings or t-strings. A parenthesized callee such as `(" ".join)([a, b])` counts too. The call takes exactly one positional argument and no keywords or splats. That argument must be a non-empty list or tuple literal (parentheses allowed) with no starred element. Each element must be one of: a plain string literal, a number, `True`/`False`, a name, any attribute access, or a call with no arguments whose callee is a name or a chain of attributes on a name (parentheses and comments allowed throughout). Anything else makes the call exempt, as in Ruff: an f-string, bytes, `None`, `...`, a subscript, a call with arguments, a unary or binary operator. A list made only of string literals is reported with advice to write the single joined literal. Ruff skips such a list when its first literal is raw and the joined result contains a newline (`\n` or `\r`, escapes decoded) together with both `'''` and `"""`. The document checks each literal (or implicit concatenation) for these, and the joiner only when the list has two or more elements. It therefore still reports when a triple quote forms only across an element boundary, where Ruff skips. A mixed list is reported with f-string advice, unless the decoded joiner and the literal elements together contain both `'` and `"`, which is Ruff's quote bail. Escapes such as `\047`, `'` and `\N{APOSTROPHE}` are decoded, and quotes inside non-literal elements such as `"it's".upper` are ignored. Calls inside strings Ruff parses as type definitions are reported as well, on the call inside the string. That covers string annotations, `List["..."]` slices, `cast`/`TypeVar` arguments, type alias values and nested strings, but not `Literal[...]` or functions decorated with `no_type_check`.

### `ruff-int001-f-string-in-get-text-func-call`

Port of Ruff's INT001 (f-string-in-get-text-func-call): reports the first positional argument of a translation call when it is an f-string. That includes an implicit concatenation with any f-string part, in any prefix case (`F`, `rf`, `fR`). T-strings are ignored. A translation call has a callee, with parentheses ignored, that is one of two things. The first is a bare name `_`, `gettext` or `ngettext`, whatever it is bound to (Ruff's default `function-names`, which this port does not let you configure). Unbound names are compared after NFKC normalization, so `ｇｅｔｔｅｘｔ(...)` counts. The second is a name or attribute that resolves through the file's imports to `gettext.gettext`, `gettext.ngettext`, `builtins._`, `builtins.gettext` or `builtins.ngettext`. The `builtins` names resolve only when `builtins` is imported, which matches Ruff's `seen_module` check. Bindings are read the way Ruff reads them. An imported alias stops counting after a later rebinding or a `del`, and before its import. A function or lambda body sees the module's last binding. Keyword arguments are never checked, and nothing is reported when the first positional argument is starred. Calls inside string type definitions are checked too, as Ruff parses them: annotations, a `TypeVar` bound, the first argument of `cast`, and strings nested in these, but not strings inside `Literal[...]`. There the finding is the argument inside the string, or the whole string when Ruff reads the annotation as complex (escapes or implicit concatenation). Elsewhere the finding covers the argument expression without its surrounding parentheses. The plural (second) argument of `ngettext` is not checked, because Ruff checks it only in preview mode. Divergence: Ruff NFKC-normalizes every identifier, but a file-local binding spelled with compatibility characters (`def ｇｅｔｔｅｘｔ(s): ...` then `ｇｅｔｔｅｘｔ(f"{a}")`) is not matched, because the name regex reads the identifier as written.

### `ruff-int002-format-in-get-text-func-call`

Port of Ruff's INT002 (format-in-get-text-func-call): reports the first positional argument of a translation call when it is a call whose callee, with parentheses ignored, is an attribute named `format`. The receiver is not checked, so `t.format(a)`, `("{}").format(a)`, `("{}".format)(a)`, `str.format("{}", a)` and `format.format(a)` are all reported. These are not: an attribute that is never called (`"{}".format`), an object named `format` (`format.join(a)`), `getattr(t, "format")(a)`, `format_map`, and the correct form `_("{}").format(a)`. A translation call has a callee, with parentheses ignored, that is one of two things. The first is a bare name `_`, `gettext` or `ngettext`, whatever it is bound to (Ruff's default `function-names`, which this port does not let you configure). Unbound names are compared after NFKC normalization. The second is a name or attribute that resolves through the file's imports to `gettext.gettext`, `gettext.ngettext`, `builtins._`, `builtins.gettext` or `builtins.ngettext`, with the `builtins` names resolving only when `builtins` is imported. Bindings are read the way Ruff reads them, so an imported alias stops counting once it is rebound or deleted. Keyword arguments and a starred first argument are never reported. Calls inside string type definitions (annotations, a `TypeVar` bound, the first argument of `cast`, and strings nested in these, but not inside `Literal[...]`) are checked as Ruff parses them. There the finding is the inner call, or the whole string for a complex annotation. Elsewhere it covers the inner call without its surrounding parentheses. The plural argument of `ngettext` is not checked, because Ruff checks it only in preview mode. Divergence: identifiers spelled with NFKC compatibility characters are compared as written in two places, so Ruff reports `_("{}".ｆｏｒｍａｔ(a))` and a locally bound `ｇｅｔｔｅｘｔ` callee, and this port does not.

### `ruff-int003-printf-in-get-text-func-call`

Port of Ruff's INT003 (printf-in-get-text-func-call): reports the first positional argument of a translation call when it is a binary `%` whose left operand, with parentheses ignored, is a string literal. That means a plain string, or an implicit concatenation of plain strings, with no `f`, `b` or `t` prefix; the `u` and `r` prefixes count as plain. So `("%s") % a`, `"a %s" "b" % a` and `"%s" % "%s"` are reported. These are not: `b"%s" % a`, `f"%s" % a`, `a % "%s"`, `"%s" * 2 % a`, and a chained `"%s" % a % b`, where the outer left operand is itself a `%` expression. A translation call has a callee, with parentheses ignored, that is one of two things. The first is a bare name `_`, `gettext` or `ngettext`, whatever it is bound to (Ruff's default `function-names`, which this port does not let you configure). Unbound names are compared after NFKC normalization. The second is a name or attribute that resolves through the file's imports to `gettext.gettext`, `gettext.ngettext`, `builtins._`, `builtins.gettext` or `builtins.ngettext`, with the `builtins` names resolving only when `builtins` is imported. Bindings are read the way Ruff reads them, so an imported alias stops counting once it is rebound or deleted. Keyword arguments and a starred first argument are never reported. Calls inside string type definitions (annotations, a `TypeVar` bound, the first argument of `cast`, and strings nested in these, but not inside `Literal[...]`) are checked as Ruff parses them. There the finding is the inner `%` expression, or the whole string for a complex annotation such as an implicitly concatenated one. Elsewhere it covers the `%` expression without its outer parentheses. The plural argument of `ngettext` is not checked, because Ruff checks it only in preview mode. Divergence: a file-local binding spelled with NFKC compatibility characters (`def ｇｅｔｔｅｘｔ(s): ...`) is not matched as a callee, because the name regex reads the identifier as written.

### `ruff-isc003-explicit-string-concatenation`

Port of Ruff's ISC003 (explicit-string-concatenation): reports a `+` binary expression when both operands are string literals of the same kind, read through any number of parentheses. Plain strings and f-strings count as one kind (`u`, `r`, `f` and combined prefixes included), bytes form another and t-strings a third. An implicit concatenation such as `("a" "b")` also counts as an operand. The expression is reported only when there is a line break (`\n`, `\r\n` or a lone `\r`) somewhere between the end of the left literal and the start of the right one. That break can sit inside the operand parentheses, in a comment or after a backslash continuation. A break before the left literal inside its own parentheses, or inside a literal (a multi-line string or a backslash-continued single-quoted string), does not count. The reported span is the whole binary expression, including any parentheses around the operands but not the ones around the expression itself. In a chain such as `"a" + "b" + "c"`, only the innermost `"a" + "b"` is reported, because the outer left operand is not a literal. Mixed kinds (bytes with str, t-string with str or f-string), non-literal operands, other operators and `+=` are not reported. Expressions inside f-string replacement fields, annotations, lambda defaults and decorators are checked like any other. Divergence: Ruff also parses string (forward-reference) annotations such as `x: """'a' +\n'b'"""` and reports the concatenation inside them; the port sees only one string literal there and stays silent. Ruff's `allow-multiline = false` setting, which turns the rule off, is not modelled; the port follows Ruff's defaults.

### `ruff-isc004-implicit-string-concatenation-in-collection-literal`

Port of Ruff's ISC004 (implicit-string-concatenation-in-collection-literal): reports an implicit string concatenation (two or more adjacent string, f-string, bytes or t-string literals) that is an element of a list, tuple or set literal and is not wrapped in its own parentheses. A single-element list or set counts, and so does a one-element tuple with a trailing comma. Tuples without parentheses count too: assignment, augmented and annotated assignment right-hand sides, `return` and `yield` values, `for` iterables, bare expression statements such as `1, "a" "b"`, the Python 3 expression `print >> f, "a" "b"`, `match` subjects with a comma, and f-string replacement fields such as `{'a' 'b', 1}`. Multi-index subscripts such as `d[1, "a" "b"]` count, in expressions and in annotations alike: `Literal["a" "b", "c"]` or `tuple["a" "b", int]` in a parameter, return or variable annotation, a `type` alias value or a PEP 696 type parameter default is reported. As in Ruff, string (forward-reference) annotations such as `x: "Literal['a' 'b', 'c']"` and other strings Ruff reads as type definitions are parsed, and a concatenation inside them is reported at its place in the string. A single-index subscript `d["a" "b"]` is not, and neither is a concatenation used as the subscripted value, as in `"a" "b"[0, 1]`, even with a comment or line break before the `[`. `with ("a" "b", 1):` without `as` is not reported, because Ruff reads it as two context managers, while `with ("a" "b", 1) as x:` is. The reported span runs from the first literal to the last. Parenthesized elements, dict keys and values, comprehension elements, call arguments, `assert` operands, starred or walrus-wrapped strings, conditional expressions and `case` patterns are not reported (a `case` guard is an ordinary expression and is).

### `ruff-log001-direct-logger-instantiation`

Port of Ruff's LOG001 (direct-logger-instantiation): the callee of a call (parentheses transparent) that is a name or attribute resolving to `logging.Logger` through the file's imports (Ruff's `resolve_qualified_name`), reported on the callee expression itself (`call.func`, inside any parentheses).

### `ruff-log002-invalid-get-logger-argument`

Port of Ruff's LOG002 (invalid-get-logger-argument): in a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves to `logging.getLogger` through the file's imports, the `name` argument (Ruff's `find_argument_value("name", 0)`: the `name=` keyword, else the first positional argument) when it is (parentheses transparent) the name `__file__` or `__cached__` referring to the module global (no file-local binding shadows it: Ruff's `has_builtin_binding`). Reported on the name.

### `ruff-log004-log-exception-outside-except-handler`

Port of Ruff's LOG004 (log-exception-outside-except-handler) with default settings (no `logger-objects`): a call to `exception` on a logger candidate, or to a name imported as `logging.exception`, that is outside every `except` and `except*` clause; a logger candidate follows Ruff's `is_logger_candidate`: an inline call to `logging.getLogger` or `logging.Logger` (resolved through imports and aliases), the `logging` module or `flask.current_app.logger` through its import, or a name or attribute chain of plain names (no calls or subscripts) whose last segment starts with `log` or `LOG`, or ends with `logger`, `logging`, `LOGGER` or `LOGGING`, and whose head does not resolve to any other import, class, function or builtin. Only the clause itself counts as a handler (its type expression, name and body, including functions, classes, lambdas and comprehensions defined in it); `try` bodies and `else` and `finally` blocks are outside, wherever the `try` is nested. Parentheses around the callee or the logger are read through. Calls inside strings Ruff parses as type annotations (annotations of variables, parameters and returns, `TypeAlias` values, `typing.cast` types, `TypeVar` bounds and constraints, `NewType` bases, nested string annotations; not `Literal` or `Annotated` metadata) are read too, reported on the call when the string is a single plain literal and on the whole string when it is concatenated or escaped, as Ruff does. Reported on the whole call, with Ruff's `# noqa` honoured.

### `ruff-log007-exception-without-exc-info`
