Port of Ruff's LOG007 (exception-without-exc-info) with default settings: `exception` called on a logger candidate (same candidates as LOG004) or through a name imported as `logging.exception`, anywhere in the file (handlers included), whose first `exc_info=` keyword has a value Ruff's `Truthiness` proves falsy: `False`, `None`, a zero number (int, float or complex, any spelling, including floats that underflow to zero such as `1e-400`), an empty string or bytes, an empty f-string (only empty literals and interpolations of empty strings or nested empty f-strings, without `=`, a conversion or a format spec; like Ruff, an interpolated implicit concatenation counts only its f-string parts, so `f"{'a' f''}"` is empty), implicit concatenations of empty parts, an empty list, tuple or dict display (comments inside are ignored), or a call of builtin `list`, `tuple`, `set`, `dict` or `frozenset` (the name not rebound in the scope Ruff looks it up in) with no arguments or exactly one positional argument that is itself a falsy string, f-string, container or such call (a number, boolean, `None`, `...`, lambda, generator or t-string argument is unknown to Ruff and not reported); parentheses are read through. Anything Ruff cannot prove (`not x`, `-0`, `1 - 1`, `x or False`, `[*a]`, `{**a}`, comprehensions, walrus, `str()`, `bool()`, `exc_info` passed through `**kwargs`) is not reported. Calls inside strings Ruff parses as type annotations (annotations of variables, parameters and returns, `TypeAlias` values, `typing.cast` types, `TypeVar` bounds and constraints, `NewType` bases, nested string annotations; not `Literal` or `Annotated` metadata) are read too, reported on the call when the string is a single plain literal and on the whole string when it is concatenated or escaped, as Ruff does. Reported on the whole call, with Ruff's `# noqa` honoured.

### `ruff-log009-undocumented-warn`

Port of Ruff's LOG009 (undocumented-warn): a name or attribute expression resolving to `logging.WARN` through the file's imports (Ruff's `resolve_qualified_name`; e.g. `logging.WARN`, or `WARN` after `from logging import WARN`), reported on it.

### `ruff-log014-exc-info-outside-except-handler`

Port of Ruff's LOG014 (exc-info-outside-except-handler) with default settings: the first `exc_info=` keyword argument of a call whose value is a literal Ruff proves truthy (`True`, `...`, a non-zero number, a non-empty plain string or bytes, implicit concatenations of those; f-strings, t-strings, tuples, lists, sets, `-1`, walrus and calls are not literals), parentheses read through, in a call to `debug`, `info`, `warning`, `warn`, `error`, `critical` or `exception` on a logger candidate (same candidates as LOG004; `fatal` is never covered, and `log` is covered only as a name imported from `logging`) or to the same names imported from `logging` (`from logging import info`), outside every `except` and `except*` clause (`try` bodies and `else` and `finally` blocks count as outside). Calls inside strings Ruff parses as type annotations (annotations of variables, parameters and returns, `TypeAlias` values, `typing.cast` types, `TypeVar` bounds and constraints, `NewType` bases, nested string annotations; not `Literal` or `Annotated` metadata) are read too, reported on the call when the string is a single plain literal and on the whole string when it is concatenated or escaped, as Ruff does. Reported on the keyword argument (`exc_info=True`, from the keyword to the end of the value's closing parenthesis, as in Ruff's keyword range), with Ruff's `# noqa` honoured.

### `ruff-log015-root-logger-call`

Port of Ruff's LOG015 (root-logger-call): a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) is a name or attribute resolving through the file's imports (Ruff's `resolve_qualified_name`) to one of the `logging` module's functions `debug`, `info`, `warn`, `warning`, `error`, `critical`, `log` or `exception` (e.g. `logging.info(...)`, or `info(...)` after `from logging import info`), reported on the call. The message names the callee's last identifier, which differs from Ruff's (the resolved function's name) only when the function was imported under an alias.

### `ruff-n801-invalid-class-name`

Port of Ruff's N801 (invalid-class-name): reports the name of every class definition (top level, nested, inside functions or conditionals, decorated, PEP 695 generic, in .py and .pyi) whose name, after stripping leading underscores, does not start with an uppercase character (Rust char::is_uppercase, including titlecase letters and characters that Python's NFKC identifier normalization folds to an uppercase letter or an underscore, modelled with explicit Unicode ranges) or still contains an underscore. The reported span is the class name only. Names in pep8-naming's default ignore list (setUp, tearDown, setUpClass, tearDownClass, setUpModule, tearDownModule, asyncSetUp, asyncTearDown, setUpTestData, failureException, longMessage, maxDiff) are skipped; user-configured ignore-names are not readable. Divergence: Ruff matches the ignore list against the NFKC-normalized identifier, so an ignore-listed name written with compatibility characters (for example fullwidth setUp) is reported here but not by Ruff.

### `ruff-n802-invalid-function-name`

Port of Ruff's N802 (invalid-function-name): reports the name of every def (async, nested, methods, in .py and .pyi) containing an alphabetic character that is not lowercase (Rust char::is_lowercase semantics, with the characters Python's NFKC identifier normalization folds to uppercase modelled by explicit ranges). Skipped as in Ruff: pep8-naming's default ignore names (setUp, tearDown, ..., maxDiff); defs decorated with override or overload from typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed, resolved through imports (aliases, module aliases, class-body and function-local imports and parentheses work; the call form `@override()` is not exempt, and a name that is shadowed or bound only after the def at module level is not recognised, as in Ruff); `visit_*` methods that sit directly in a class body whose positional bases are ast.NodeVisitor or ast.NodeTransformer, directly or through classes the file defines (subscripts and parentheses included, and one `Outer.Inner` attribute step to a class nested in a module- or function-level class); and `do_*` methods likewise for http.server BaseHTTPRequestHandler, CGIHTTPRequestHandler and SimpleHTTPRequestHandler. As in Ruff, a base class bound in an enclosing class body is not followed (Ruff looks it up from the method's scope, where enclosing class scopes are invisible), a def under if/for/match in a class body is not a direct method, and a nested def inside a visit_ method is still reported. Two clauses differ only in message: methods directly in a class body (suggesting `@typing.override`), and all other functions. Divergences: user-configured ignore-names are unreadable, and an ignore-listed name spelled with NFKC-foldable compatibility characters (fullwidth setUp) is reported here but skipped by Ruff.

### `ruff-n804-invalid-first-argument-name-for-class-method`

Port of Ruff's N804 (invalid-first-argument-name-for-class-method): reports the first positional parameter (positional-only first, else the first regular one; `*args`, keyword-only and absent parameters report nothing) of a def whose nearest enclosing scope is a class body (also under if, try, for, with or match in the body; PEP 695 generic methods included) when it is not named `cls` and not in the default ignore names. A def counts as a class method as in Ruff's function_type::classify: unless a decorator is `staticmethod` or `abc.abstractstaticmethod` (builtin resolved through global or builtins, aliases included; wrapped in a call or parentheses is fine; static wins over class), it is a class method when a decorator is `classmethod` or `abc.abstractclassmethod`; otherwise `__init_subclass__` and `__class_getitem__` are class methods, `__new__` never is, and any other undecorated method is one when its class is definitely a metaclass. A metaclass is a class with a positional base that is `type`, `type[...]`, `builtins.type`, `abc.ABCMeta`, `enum.EnumMeta`, `enum.EnumType`, or a module-level class defined in the file that is itself one (through subscripts, parentheses and one `Outer.Inner` attribute step to a class nested in a module-level class; `metaclass=` keywords do not count, and `abc.ABCMeta[...]` is not one). As in Ruff's is_metaclass the class is not definitely a metaclass, and nothing is reported, when a call base comes before the matching base, either in its own bases or in the bases of an earlier local base class (`class M(print(), type)` silent, `class M(type, print())` reported). Bases are looked up from the module scope by their last binding, so function-local and class-local metaclass chains are not followed, as in Ruff. The span is Ruff's Parameter: the name with its annotation, never the default (`this: int = 3` ends before the `=`); a second clause with a regex capture anchors the annotated-with-default form. Divergences: the annotated-default span regex handles four levels of square brackets, three of parentheses, and quoted strings without escapes; a decorator or metaclass base name read before its module-level import or class definition is not resolved (Ruff resolves it against the module's final binding); NFKC-folded spellings of `cls` or of an ignore-listed name are reported here but not by Ruff; user-configured classmethod-decorators, staticmethod-decorators and ignore-names are unreadable; and the harness's not-overfit gate flags the default ignore-names list, which Ruff spells in pep8_naming/settings.rs.

### `ruff-n805-invalid-first-argument-name-for-method`

Port of Ruff's N805 (invalid-first-argument-name-for-method): reports the first positional parameter (positional-only or regular; its span covers the name and annotation, not the default) of a function defined directly in a class body (also under if/try/for/while/with/match, sync or async, in .py and .pyi) when it is not `self` (NFKC-equivalent spellings such as fullwidth `ｓｅｌｆ` count as `self`). Skipped: functions decorated with staticmethod or classmethod (builtins or abc, through aliases, parentheses and one call), `__new__`, `__init_subclass__`, `__class_getitem__`, methods of metaclasses (a base resolving to type, abc.ABCMeta, enum.EnumMeta or enum.EnumType, `type[...]`, a one-argument `type(...)` call including a lone generator argument, through parentheses, or a module-level class, or a class nested in one reached as `Outer.Meta`, that extends one, followed transitively) and of classes that may be metaclasses (with a positional base and a direct `__new__` taking exactly five parameters, no `*args`, a `**kwargs`, and the second to fourth annotated with builtin str, tuple and dict, bare, subscripted, parenthesized or as `builtins.X`), functions with no positional parameter, and names in the pep8-naming ignore-names option (default: setUp, tearDown, setUpClass, tearDownClass, setUpModule, tearDownModule, asyncSetUp, asyncTearDown, setUpTestData, failureException, longMessage, maxDiff). Divergences: base class names and decorators resolve to the binding visible where they are written, whereas Ruff uses the module's final binding (a metaclass defined or a name rebound later in a .py module); an ignore-listed name spelled with NFKC-compatibility characters is reported; attribute bases deeper than `Outer.Meta` are not followed; the span of an annotated parameter with a default is found by a regex that skips strings and up to four nested bracket or three parenthesis levels.

### `ruff-n806-non-lowercase-variable-in-function`

Port of Ruff's N806 (non-lowercase-variable-in-function): reports every name bound by a store in a function's own scope (assignment, annotated assignment with or without value, augmented assignment, for/with targets, tuple/list/starred/parenthesized targets, walrus, match capture, star and mapping-rest patterns; also walrus targets in a nested def's or lambda's defaults, in decorators and in the first iterable of a comprehension, which Python evaluates in the enclosing function) whose name has an alphabetic character that is not lowercase (Rust char::is_lowercase semantics with NFKC folding modelled). Not reported: stores inside comprehension scopes (the element, conditions, targets and later iterables), lambda bodies, class bases and keywords, and a nested def's parameter and return annotations (type-parameter scope); names declared global in the function; assignments whose value is a call to collections.namedtuple or typing's NamedTuple, TypedDict, TypeVar or NewType (also via typing_extensions or _typeshed); TypeAlias-annotated assignments and `type` statements; `x = <...>.get_model(...)` when the model_name argument (or the last positional argument, if there is no `*args`) is not a string literal or equals the name, also for implicitly concatenated literals; `x = import_string(...)` (django.utils.module_loading) when its dotted_path argument or first argument is a string literal whose last dotted part equals the name; and the pep8-naming ignore-names option (default: setUp, tearDown, setUpClass, tearDownClass, setUpModule, tearDownModule, asyncSetUp, asyncTearDown, setUpTestData, failureException, longMessage, maxDiff). Divergences: an ignore-listed name written with NFKC-compatibility characters is reported here but not by Ruff; a name stored before a (syntactically invalid) later `global` declaration is exempt here but reported by Ruff; string arguments are compared without decoding escape sequences.

### `ruff-n807-dunder-function-name`

Port of Ruff's N807 (dunder-function-name): reports the name of every function definition (sync or async, decorated or not, at module level, nested in functions, under if/try/for/while/with/match, generic, in .py and .pyi) whose name starts and ends with two underscores after Python's NFKC identifier normalization (so `_＿x__` with a fullwidth low line counts), except functions defined directly in a class body (methods) and module-level `__getattr__` and `__dir__` (PEP 562, also when spelled with NFKC-compatibility characters such as `__ｇｅｔａｔｔｒ__`); a `__getattr__` or `__dir__` nested in a function is still reported. The default pep8-naming ignore-names contain no dunder names, so they never apply; user-configured ignore-names are not readable.

### `ruff-n811-constant-imported-as-non-constant`

Port of Ruff's N811 (constant-imported-as-non-constant): reports an aliased import (`import X as y`, `import pkg.X as y`, `from m import X as y`, relative or not, at any scope, in .py and .pyi) when the imported name (the last dotted component) is cased-uppercase (has an uppercase letter and no lowercase one) and the alias is not; exempt are single-character names (after NFKC, so `Ǆ` or `Ⅱ` count as two characters) whose alias is CamelCase (cased neither way and free of underscores, NFKC underscore variants included), and aliases in the default pep8-naming ignore-names list (setUp, tearDown, setUpClass, tearDownClass, setUpModule, tearDownModule, asyncSetUp, asyncTearDown, setUpTestData, failureException, longMessage, maxDiff). Reports the whole `name as alias` span; the noqa comment may sit on the statement's first line. Unicode case rules (titlecase, modifier letters, NFKC folded letters) are modelled with explicit ranges; user-configured ignore-names are not readable. Divergences: a single uppercase letter written as a base letter plus a combining mark that NFKC composes into one character (`A` + U+0300) is treated as two characters, and Python 3.15 lazy imports are not parsed.

### `ruff-n812-lowercase-imported-as-non-lowercase`

Port of Ruff's N812 (lowercase-imported-as-non-lowercase): reports the whole `name as alias` clause of an `import` or `from ... import` statement when the imported name (the last dotted segment for `import a.b as c`) is cased lowercase (at least one lowercase letter, no uppercase letter) and the alias holds an alphabetic character that is not lowercase. Aliases in Ruff's default ignore-names list (tearDownClass, setUpModule, asyncSetUp, asyncTearDown, setUpTestData, failureException, longMessage, maxDiff, setUp, ...) are skipped; the lint.pep8-naming.ignore-names options are not read. Ruff classifies identifiers after NFKC normalisation; the port reproduces it for titlecase digraphs (U+01C5, U+01C8, U+01CB, U+01F2), modifier capital letters (U+1D2C..U+1D42, U+2C7D, U+A7F1..U+A7F4, U+A7F8) and the compatibility letters U+0149, U+1E9A, U+02E4, U+107B6..U+107B9, but not the halfwidth voiced-sound marks U+FF9E and U+FF9F inside an alias. Lazy imports (`lazy import`, `lazy from ... import`) are not parsed and not reported.

### `ruff-n813-camelcase-imported-as-lowercase`

Port of Ruff's N813 (camelcase-imported-as-lowercase): reports the whole `name as alias` clause of an `import` or `from ... import` statement when the imported name (the last dotted segment for `import a.b as c`) is CamelCase in Ruff's sense (no underscore, and neither cased lowercase nor cased uppercase, so `mixedCase` and `日本` count while `Camel_Case` and `CAMEL` do not) and the alias is cased lowercase. Imported names in Ruff's default ignore-names list (setUp, tearDown, maxDiff, ...) are skipped; the lint.pep8-naming.ignore-names options are not read. Case classification mirrors Ruff's after NFKC normalisation for titlecase digraphs and modifier capital letters (a name such as `ǅx` counts as having an uppercase letter). Lazy imports (`lazy import`, `lazy from ... import`) are not parsed and not reported.

### `ruff-n814-camelcase-imported-as-constant`

Port of Ruff's N814 (camelcase-imported-as-constant): reports the whole `name as alias` clause of an `import` or `from ... import` statement when the imported name (the last dotted segment for `import a.b as c`) is CamelCase in Ruff's sense (no underscore, neither cased lowercase nor cased uppercase), the alias is cased uppercase and has at least two characters after NFKC normalisation (so a single ligature or Roman numeral such as `Ǆ`, `Ĳ`, `Ŀ` or `Ⅻ` counts as two or more), and the alias is not the acronym of the name (the name's uppercase letters in order, which Ruff leaves to its acronym rule); the acronym is computed with a transform that deletes every non-uppercase character of the name and compared with the alias text. Imported names in Ruff's default ignore-names list (setUp, tearDown, maxDiff, ...) are skipped; the lint.pep8-naming.ignore-names options are not read. Case classification mirrors Ruff's after NFKC normalisation for titlecase digraphs and modifier capital letters; the acronym comparison reads raw characters. Lazy imports (`lazy import`, `lazy from ... import`) are not parsed and not reported.

### `ruff-n815-mixed-case-variable-in-class-scope`

Port of Ruff's N815 (mixed-case-variable-in-class-scope): reports a mixedCase name stored in class scope, anchored on the name. A name is mixedCase when, after at most one leading underscore, it starts with a lowercase letter and holds an uppercase letter anywhere (`myVar`, `_myVar`, `my_Var`; not `__myVar`, `my_var`, `MyVar`). Store positions covered: plain, annotated (with or without a value) and augmented assignment targets including chained, parenthesised, tuple, list and starred targets; for-loop targets; with-item targets (parenthesised, tuple, list and starred); walrus targets; `type` alias names; and match-case captures (`case x`, `case ... as x`, `*x`, `**x`, keyword-pattern values, alternatives of an or-pattern). Class scope follows Ruff: statements nested in if/for/while/try/with/match blocks of a class body count, as do decorators, parameter defaults, lambda defaults and the iterable of a comprehension's first `for` clause; function bodies, parameter and return annotations, lambda bodies, the rest of a comprehension, `type` alias values, and class base lists and keywords do not. Exempt as in Ruff: a plain (not annotated) assignment statement whose value, parentheses ignored, is a call of an imported collections.namedtuple, typing.NamedTuple, typing_extensions.NamedTuple or _typeshed.NamedTuple (any target of that statement, shadowing respected); every statement of a class that derives from typing.TypedDict, typing_extensions.TypedDict or _typeshed.TypedDict directly, through a visible local class defined outside a class body, or through `Local.Nested` where `Nested` is a TypedDict-derived class anywhere in the body of the visible local class `Local` (parentheses transparent, at most one subscript stripped, so `TypedDict[int][str]` is not recognised, as in Ruff); and Ruff's twelve default ignore-names (setUp, tearDown, setUpClass, tearDownClass, setUpModule, tearDownModule, asyncSetUp, asyncTearDown, setUpTestData, failureException, longMessage, maxDiff). Divergences from Ruff: attribute chains of two or more levels to a nested TypedDict class (`Outer.Mid.DeepTD`) are not recognised; a local base class is resolved where the base is written, while Ruff looks its name up from the class body at each store, so a body that rebinds the base name keeps the exemption here; the lint.pep8-naming.ignore-names options are not read. Case classification mirrors Ruff's after NFKC normalisation for titlecase digraphs and modifier capital letters.

### `ruff-n816-mixed-case-variable-in-global-scope`

Port of Ruff's N816 (mixed-case-variable-in-global-scope): reports each name bound in module scope whose spelling is mixedCase, meaning it contains an uppercase letter and its first character (after one optional leading underscore) is lowercase, at the identifier itself. It covers plain, chained, tuple, list and starred assignments, annotated assignments with or without a value, augmented assignments (including a parenthesised target such as `(name) += 1`), walrus targets, for and with targets, match captures (as, star and mapping rest) and type alias names. It does not report names bound in functions, lambdas, classes or comprehensions (a walrus is in module scope only when every comprehension around it holds it in its first iterable, so `[[y for y in (n := x)] for x in z]` is silent), names bound through global from inside a function, except, import, def, class and parameter names, attribute and subscript targets, or walrus targets inside class bases, parameter and return annotations and type alias values. An unannotated assignment whose value is a call of collections.namedtuple or typing/typing_extensions/_typeshed NamedTuple (through imports, aliases, parentheses and chained targets) is exempt, together with every name bound inside that statement, as in Ruff. Ruff's default ignore-names (setUp, tearDown, setUpClass, tearDownClass, setUpModule, tearDownModule, asyncSetUp, asyncTearDown, setUpTestData, failureException, longMessage, maxDiff) are exempt. Case classification follows Ruff's Unicode properties, including the compatibility characters that NFKC-normalise to other letters. A user-configured ignore-names list cannot be expressed.

### `ruff-n817-camelcase-imported-as-acronym`

Port of Ruff's N817 (camelcase-imported-as-acronym): reports an aliased import (`import a.b.Name as X` or `from m import Name as X`, at the whole `Name as X` alias, with noqa read from the statement's first line) when the imported name (last dotted segment) is CamelCase, meaning it has both an uppercase and a lowercase letter and no underscore, and the alias is exactly the name's uppercase letters in order (`CamelCase` as `CC`; a digit, underscore or lowercase letter in the alias, or a different letter sequence, does not match). The acronym is computed with a transform that deletes every non-uppercase character and compared to the alias. Ruff's default import convention is exempt: `xml.etree.ElementTree` aliased to `ET`, through `import xml.etree.ElementTree as ET` or an absolute `from xml.etree import ElementTree as ET` (white space and line continuations inside the dotted path allowed); relative imports and any other module path (`xml.etree.cElementTree`, `from xml.etree.ElementTree import ElementTree`) are still reported. Ruff's default ignore-names (setUp, tearDown, setUpClass, tearDownClass, setUpModule, tearDownModule, asyncSetUp, asyncTearDown, setUpTestData, failureException, longMessage, maxDiff) are exempt as imported names. Divergences from Ruff: identifiers written with NFKC-compatibility characters (fullwidth, modifier, titlecase-digraph or decomposed letters) are classified and compared as written, not normalised; a user-configured ignore-names list or flake8-import-conventions alias map cannot be expressed.

### `ruff-n818-error-suffix-on-exception-name`

Port of Ruff's N818 (error-suffix-on-exception-name): reports, at the class name, a class definition whose name does not end in `Error` and whose positional bases include a plain name (not an attribute, call, subscript, walrus, keyword or starred base) that is exactly `Exception` or ends in `Error`, looking through any number of parentheses around the base. No name resolution is done, as in Ruff: `class A(Base)` where Base derives from Exception is silent, `class A(mod.Error)` and `class A(BaseException)` are silent, and a local class named Exception is treated like the builtin. Keyword arguments such as `metaclass=...` do not count as bases, and generic, nested and stub classes are handled the same way. The `Error` suffix and the `Exception` base are matched as Ruff reads them after NFKC normalisation, so fullwidth, mathematical, modifier and small-roman-numeral compatibility spellings of their letters count (`class MyＥrror(Exception)` is silent, `class A(Ｅxception)` is reported). Ruff's default ignore-names (setUp, tearDown, ..., maxDiff) are exempt. A user-configured ignore-names list cannot be expressed.

### `ruff-npy001-numpy-deprecated-type-alias`

Port of Ruff's NPY001 (numpy-deprecated-type-alias): reports a read of any name or attribute chain that resolves through the file's imports (aliases, `from numpy import int`, `import numpy.linalg`, parentheses, comments, line continuations) to `numpy.int`, `numpy.float`, `numpy.complex`, `numpy.object`, `numpy.str` or `numpy.unicode`, on the reference itself (`np.int`, or `int` after `from numpy import int`); `np.bool`, `np.long`, `np.int_`, `np.str_` and shadowed names are not reported. Like Ruff it also reports inside string annotations (nested ones, `cast` and `TypeVar` string arguments), anchoring on the name inside the string, and on the dotted names of `match` patterns, and skips `Literal[...]` strings and plain strings. Attribute chains in assignment or `del` targets are reported as Ruff does; a bare name only when read. Two clauses give different advice: `unicode` becomes `str`, the other five become the builtin of the same name. Keyword-argument names, parameters and the attribute part of an unrelated chain are not reported; class patterns with a bare name (`case int():`) and mapping-pattern keys are. Each message tells the agent to drop the NumPy import along with the alias. No divergence from live Ruff over the porter's 25 probes and 20 review probes.

### `ruff-npy002-numpy-legacy-random`

Port of Ruff's NPY002 (numpy-legacy-random): reports the callee (the attribute or name, not the whole call) of a call that resolves through the file's imports to `numpy.random.<name>` for the 50 legacy functions Ruff lists (seed, get_state, set_state, rand, ranf, sample, randn, randint, random, random_integers, random_sample, choice, bytes, shuffle, permutation and the 35 distributions). Parentheses, comments, line continuations and decorator calls are transparent; references that are not called (`f = np.random.rand`, `@np.random.seed` without a call), `default_rng`, `Generator`, `RandomState`, `mtrand`, star imports and shadowed names are not reported. Callees inside string annotations are reported too, anchored inside the string. Clauses differ by advice: seed, get_state/set_state, rand, randn, randint, random_integers, the random/ranf/sample/random_sample family, and the same-named Generator methods (distributions, choice, shuffle, permutation, bytes) each name the Generator replacement and say to keep the legacy call where seeded results must match. No divergence from live Ruff over the porter's 17 probes and 20 review probes (aliases of `numpy.random`, `.__call__` chains, subscripts, keyword names, shadowing parameters, class attributes).

### `ruff-npy003-numpy-deprecated-function`

Port of Ruff's NPY003 (numpy-deprecated-function): reports a read of any name or attribute chain that resolves through the file's imports (aliases, from-imports, parentheses, decorators, store and del attribute targets) to `numpy.round_`, `numpy.product`, `numpy.cumproduct`, `numpy.sometrue` or `numpy.alltrue`, on the reference itself, with one clause per name advising `numpy.round`, `numpy.prod`, `numpy.cumprod`, `numpy.any` and `numpy.all`. References inside string annotations and the dotted names of `match` patterns are reported by a sixth clause whose message lists the five replacements instead of naming the single one, anchored on the name inside the string. No divergence from live Ruff over the porter's 27 probes and 20 review probes (keyword names, scopes, late imports, store and del targets).

### `ruff-npy201-numpy2-deprecation`

Port of Ruff's NPY201 (numpy2-deprecation): reports a read of any name or attribute chain that resolves through the file's imports to one of the 70 members Ruff lists as removed from NumPy's main namespace, on the reference itself, in 56 clauses grouped by advice. Backwards-compatible moves name the replacement (`numpy.all`, `numpy.exceptions.ComplexWarning`, `numpy.lib.add_docstring`, `inspect.getsource`, ...); the three breaking ones (`byte_bounds`, `trapz`, `RankWarning`) say to keep the old name while NumPy 1.x must be supported; pure-Python replacements (`issubclass`, `-np.inf`, `0.0`, `-0.0`) and the manual guidelines (`np.errstate`, `np.isin`, `np.genfromtxt`, ...) each have their own text, and the members with no replacement share one message. Ruff's try/except fallback exemption is reproduced by one shared utility: an attribute is skipped when it sits in an except handler (not crossing a def or class body; lambdas and comprehensions do not reset it) of the nearest enclosing try, that try catches the builtin `AttributeError` as a handler's type, a direct element of its tuple, or `builtins.AttributeError`, through parentheses (bare except, `Exception`, a nested tuple or a call such as `type(AttributeError)` do not count, as in Ruff) and its body uses the member's replacement as an attribute; a name is skipped when its binding is a from-import inside an except handler whose nearest try catches `ImportError` or `ModuleNotFoundError` and whose body imports the replacement with `from <module> import <name>` (the original name, whatever the alias; a relative `from .<module> import <name>` counts too, since Ruff compares the module text without the dots). Only replacements Ruff can auto-import are exempt, never the manual or pure-Python ones. Each clause binds the replacement's last name with a transform and the shared exemption compares it, so the exemption is written once. String annotations and match-case patterns are covered by a catch-all clause with a generic message and an approximate exemption; see the divergences recorded for this rule.

### `ruff-pd002-pandas-use-of-inplace-argument`

Port of Ruff's PD002 (pandas-use-of-inplace-argument): flags a keyword argument named `inplace` whose value is the literal `True` (parentheses around it are transparent; `1`, `not False`, `bool(True)`, `"True"` and names are not) in a call whose callee is an attribute access (also when the callee is written in parentheses) named one of the 22 methods Ruff lists as accepting it: where, mask, query, clip, eval, backfill, bfill, ffill, fillna, interpolate, dropna, pad, replace, drop, drop_duplicates, rename, rename_axis, reset_index, set_index, sort_values, sort_index, set_names. As in Ruff only the last `inplace` keyword of a call is examined, so `drop(inplace=True, inplace=False)` is silent. Like Ruff it never looks at the receiver's type, but it only reports once an absolute `import pandas...` or `from pandas... import` has been visited in Ruff's traversal order (module body first, then function bodies level by level, then lambdas and type values), so a module-level call above the first import is silent while a function body defined above it is reported; relative imports and look-alike modules such as pandas_ta do not count. Annotations that Ruff defers (every parameter, return and variable annotation in a file with `from __future__ import annotations`, and in a .pyi stub) count as visited after every import, so they are reported when the file imports pandas anywhere. Calls inside string annotations are checked too. The reported span is the whole `inplace=True` keyword. Divergence: Ruff's notebook test (PD002_cell_start.ipynb) cannot be reproduced because the engine has no notebook dialect.

### `ruff-pd012-pandas-use-of-dot-read-table`

Port of Ruff's PD012 (pandas-use-of-dot-read-table): flags a call whose callee resolves to exactly `pandas.read_table` through any import form or alias (`pd.read_table`, `pandas.read_table` after `import pandas.io`, `from pandas import read_table as rt`; submodule paths such as `pd.io.parsers.read_table` do not resolve to it, as in Ruff, a name rebound or deleted is not matched, and a plain variable holding `pd.read_table` is not followed) and whose first `sep=` keyword (Ruff's find_keyword; a later duplicate is ignored) is a plain string literal equal to a single comma. Raw and unicode prefixes, escapes such as `"\x2c"`, `"\054"` or `"\N{COMMA}"`, a backslash line continuation, parentheses and implicit concatenation of plain parts (`"," ""`) count; `r"\x2c"`, `", "`, f-strings, bytes, names and expressions such as `"," + ""` do not. A callee written in parentheses is accepted, and the reported span is the callee alone, without those parentheses. Calls inside string annotations are checked as well.

### `ruff-pd101-pandas-nunique-constant-series-check`

Port of Ruff's PD101 (pandas-nunique-constant-series-check): flags a single comparison (chained comparisons are excluded) with `==`, `!=`, `<=` or `>` whose right operand is the integer literal 1 in any base or separator spelling (`0x1`, `0b_1`, `(1)`; `1.0`, `True`, `+1` and `1j` are excluded) and whose left operand is a call of a `.nunique(...)` attribute, parentheses allowed around the call and the callee. The receiver of `.nunique` must pass Ruff's relevance test: a name bound as a parameter other than `self`/`cls`, or by an assignment, annotation (resolved only in stubs, as Ruff resolves bare annotations), augmented assignment, for or comprehension target, walrus, global/nonlocal, match capture or type alias, or any non-name expression (calls, subscripts, attributes, operators, f-strings), but not literals, collections, comprehensions, or names bound by imports, def, class, with, except, type parameters, deletion or left unresolved. As in Ruff's binding table, a module-scope name bound by a statement whose first target is `__all__` (including `__all__ = x = ...`) is an export, and a comprehension target written in a `with` header is a with-item variable, so both are left alone. Like Ruff it only reports once a `pandas` import has been visited in Ruff's traversal order; annotations Ruff defers (all annotations under `from __future__ import annotations`, and in .pyi stubs) count as visited after every import in the file. Comparisons inside string annotations are checked. The span is the whole comparison. It is split into clauses per operator, so each gets its own replacement expression, plus a stub-annotation clause with a general message.

### `ruff-perf101-unnecessary-list-cast`

Port of Ruff's PERF101 (unnecessary-list-cast): reports the whole `list(...)` call (`(list)(x)` and `builtins.list(x)` through any import alias included, unless `list` is shadowed) when it is the iterable of a `for` or `async for` statement and has exactly one positional argument (keyword arguments and `**` splats are ignored, as in Ruff). Clause one reports a tuple, list or set display as that argument, parentheses transparent; a comprehension, generator, dict or `*` argument is not reported. Clause two reports a name whose binding value is such a display, a bare tuple such as `a = 1, 2` included (also through tuple unpacking of equal arity, chained, annotated or walrus assignments, and `with <display> as name`), read with `stopAtGlobal` as Ruff's lookup_symbol does; a name the loop's function declares `nonlocal` is not followed, since Ruff's binding there is the nonlocal declaration. It stays silent when the loop body (nested defs and classes included, the else clause not) has a statement that mutates the name the way Ruff's MutationVisitor sees it: an expression statement whose whole value, parentheses aside, is a call of append, insert, extend, remove, pop, clear, reverse or sort on it (`x.append(1), 2` is a tuple and does not count); an assignment or annotation whose target is a subscript of it; a `del` whose targets list a subscript of it (`del (x[0], y)` is one tuple target and does not count); or an augmented assignment to it. Ruff analyses a `for` statement after traversing it, so it reads the name's binding as it stands after the loop: the rule also stays silent when the target, body or else clause, in the loop's own scope, rebinds the name by for, with, import (`import x.y` binds `x`), def, class, del, match, an augmented assignment, or an assignment or walrus (one inside a comprehension too) whose value is not a tuple, list or set display; rebinding it to a display, a bare annotation, or `except ... as name` still reports. Divergence: unpacking assignments whose target and value differ in length, use a starred target, or unpack a set display are not followed (Ruff zips them).

### `ruff-perf102-incorrect-dict-iterator`

Port of Ruff's PERF102 (incorrect-dict-iterator): the stable `for` statement form only; Ruff's comprehension and generator form is preview-only in 0.16.9 and is not reported by default, so it is not ported. Reports the `X.items` attribute (Ruff's func range: parentheses around the object included, parentheses around the attribute itself, as in `(d.items)()`, excluded) of a call without positional arguments (keyword arguments and `**` splats are allowed, as in Ruff) when that call is the iterable of a `for` or `async for` statement, parentheses around the call transparent, and the target is a two-element tuple (`k, v`, `(k, v)`, `((k, v))`, trailing comma allowed; a `[k, v]` list target is not) of which exactly one element is unused. An element is unused when it is a tuple of unused elements (an empty tuple included) or a name that is never read anywhere its scope's bindings of that name are visible: like Ruff's `is_unused`, which looks at every binding of the name from the loop onward and counts reads copied forward from earlier bindings, the rule asks whether the latest binding of the name in the enclosing function, class or module scope has any read, so a later binding that is read, or an earlier one that was, keeps the loop silent, and `del name` counts as a read. Starred, attribute, subscript and list elements count as used. Two clauses with different advice: value unused (iterate `.keys()`) and key unused (iterate `.values()`).

### `ruff-perf203-try-except-in-loop`

Port of Ruff's PERF203 (try-except-in-loop): reports the first `except` clause (Ruff reports `handlers.first()`) of a `try` statement that is the only statement, comments aside, in the body of a `while` loop or of a non-async `for` loop (an `async for` and the loops' else clauses are not checked); a `try` with only a `finally` has no handler and is not reported. Like Ruff, it stays silent when the `try` body (not its handlers, else or finally) contains a `break` or `continue` anywhere in its statements, nested loops, ifs, with, match and nested try included, but not inside a nested `def` or `class`. The clause is gated with `when.pythonVersion` to 3.10 and earlier, the engine's default, which is what Ruff's default target version gives under --isolated; Ruff reports nothing for 3.11 and later. The reported span runs from `except` to the end of the handler's last statement: tree-sitter puts trailing and own-line comments after that statement inside the clause, so a witness-level regex group cuts them off (it reads string literals on the last code line as units, so a `#` inside a string is not taken for a comment). Divergence: when the handler's last statement is a multi-line triple-quoted string followed by a comment, the span keeps the comment.

### `ruff-perf401-manual-list-comprehension`

Port of Ruff's PERF401 (manual-list-comprehension): reports the `list.append(...)` call that is the only statement (trailing `;` and comments aside) of a `for` or `async for` body, or the only statement of an `if` without elif/else whose test does not mention the list and which is itself the only statement of the body. The call may be parenthesized; a statement that is a bare tuple (`x.append(1), 2`) is not a call statement and is not reported. The list must be a plain (possibly parenthesized) name whose one binding (it shadows no earlier binding in its scope) is a list display, list comprehension, `list(...)` call, a `with list() as name` item, a parameter or variable annotated `list`/`List` (bare or subscripted), or a `def` whose return annotation is `list`/`List`; parentheses, walrus, conditional expressions and `+`/`and`/`or` of list expressions are transparent in the initializer. The append takes exactly one positional argument that does not mention the list; a direct copy (`x.append(i)` with no `if`) is left to the list-copy rule except under `async for`. The loop target is a plain name (any parentheses) that is not global or nonlocal, is not read after the loop in its scope or from a nested scope, and is not redeclared `nonlocal` in a later nested function. The message offers a comprehension, or `extend` of a generator (of a list comprehension under `async for`) when the list is not a fresh empty list. Divergences from Ruff: a list declared nonlocal/global and rebound after the loop, and lists Ruff types through positional unpacking with a starred target, mismatched arity or a set display, are not reported; a loop target named only inside a string annotation after the loop does not stop the report.

### `ruff-perf402-manual-list-copy`

Port of Ruff's PERF402 (manual-list-copy): reports `x.append(t)` or `x.insert(t)` with exactly one positional argument that is the loop target name (parentheses allowed), as the only statement (trailing `;` and comments aside) of a non-async `for` body whose target is a plain name (any parentheses). An `if` around the call is not matched (Ruff's list-comprehension rule covers that), and, as in Ruff, the target may be global and may be read after the loop. `x` is a plain (possibly parenthesized) name different from the target whose one binding (it shadows no earlier binding in its scope) is a list display, list comprehension, `list(...)` call, a `with list() as x` item, a parameter or variable annotated `list`/`List`, or a `def` whose return annotation is `list`/`List`. The message offers `extend` or `list(...)` and warns that code reading the loop variable after the loop needs the loop. Divergences from Ruff: a list declared nonlocal/global and rebound after the loop, and lists Ruff types through positional unpacking with a starred target, mismatched arity or a set display, are not reported.

### `ruff-perf403-manual-dict-comprehension`

Port of Ruff's PERF403 (manual-dict-comprehension): reports the `d[key] = value` assignment that is the only statement (trailing `;` and comments aside) of a `for` or `async for` body, or of an `if` without elif/else whose test does not mention the dict and which is itself the only statement of the body. `d` is a plain (possibly parenthesized) name whose one binding is a dict display, comprehension, `dict(...)` call, `with dict() as d` item, `**kwargs` parameter, parameter or variable annotated `dict`/`Dict`, or a `def` whose return annotation is `dict`/`Dict`. The target is a name (key and value both equal to it) or a flat or nested tuple pattern without attribute or subscript elements in which the key and the value each equal an element, parentheses ignored on both sides (one-element subscript tuples `d[x,]` compare with the tuple form); the value is not a chained assignment; no target name is read after the loop or redeclared `nonlocal` in a later nested function. The message offers a dict comprehension, or `update` with one when the dict is not a fresh empty dict. Divergences from Ruff: nonlocal/global rebound dict names, an unparenthesized multi-name key matching a nested target tuple, and dicts Ruff types through positional unpacking with a starred target or mismatched arity are not reported; a target named only inside a string annotation after the loop does not stop the report.

### `ruff-pgh003-blanket-type-ignore`

Port of Ruff's PGH003 (blanket-type-ignore): flags a comment whose `#` is followed (after optional whitespace) by `type:` or `pyright:`, optional whitespace, `ignore`, and then nothing or another `#` comment, i.e. an ignore with no `[...]` tag; `# type: ignore[]`, `# type: ignore [a]`, `# type: ignoreme` and `# type: ignore[a` are left alone, as in Ruff. Every `#` inside a comment is a candidate, so `# type: ignore[a] # type: ignore` is reported at the second `#`. The reported span runs from the matching `#` to the end of the comment, as Ruff reports it. Up to three blank markers in one comment are reported (separate clauses for the first, second and third); a fourth is not. Honours `# noqa: PGH003` and a blanket `# noqa` on the line through suppress. Files that do not parse are not checked by the engine, whereas Ruff reads comments in them.

### `ruff-pgh004-blanket-noqa`

Port of Ruff's PGH004 (blanket-noqa): a shared define clause plus three clauses for Ruff's three cases. (a) A blanket inline `noqa`: the first `#`-then-whitespace-then-`noqa` marker of a comment (any case) followed by nothing, `#`, or whitespace and a non-colon, and not a missing-colon form; reported over `# noqa` only. (b) A missing colon, `# noqa F401`, `# noqa ,F401 more text`: the marker is followed by whitespace and a code list that Ruff's code lexer accepts (uppercase letters then digits, comma, whitespace or squashed separators, stopping at the first non-code word; an invalid suffix such as `F401x` or `F401F` makes it a plain blanket); reported from `#` through the whitespace after `noqa`. `# noqa: F401`, `# noqa : F401`, `# noqa:` and `#noqaF401` are not reported, and a comment whose first marker is coded or invalid is not reported even if a later `# noqa` is blanket. (c) A file-level `# ruff: noqa` or `# flake8: noqa` with no codes (`# ruff: noqa F401` counts as blanket) when it is the first file-level marker of a comment that stands on its own line (indented or inside brackets is fine; a trailing one is skipped as Ruff skips it), reported over the whole comment. When an own-line comment's first file-level marker lists PGH004 in a code list Ruff's lexer accepts (`# ruff: noqa: F401, PGH004`, `# flake8: noqa:PGH004`), nothing in the file is reported, as in Ruff; a trailing one, an inline `# noqa: PGH004`, a later marker in the comment, or a code list Ruff rejects (`PGH004 F401x`) does not exempt the file. The document has no suppress key because Ruff never lets a noqa comment suppress this rule. Files that do not parse are not checked by the engine.

### `ruff-pgh005-invalid-mock-access`

Port of Ruff's PGH005 (invalid-mock-access): two clauses matching Ruff's two stable checks. (1) An expression statement that is only an attribute read whose name is one of the mock assertion methods (assert_called, assert_called_once, assert_called_with, assert_called_once_with, assert_any_call, assert_has_calls, assert_not_called and the async assert_awaited family), reported over the attribute expression without the parentheses a statement may wrap it in: `m.assert_called`, `(m.assert_called)`; calls, tuples (`m.assert_called, x`), await and anything nested are not reported. (2) The whole test expression of an `assert` statement (not the message) when it is an attribute, or a call whose callee is an attribute (parenthesised callees included, `assert (m.not_called)()`), named like a method that does not exist on a mock (called_once, called_with, called_once_with, not_called, has_calls, any_call and the awaited, any_await, has_awaits, not_awaited family; `called` and `await_count` are real and not reported); parentheses around the test or the backslash continuation after `assert` are handled. The expression-statement form of the second check (`m.not_called()` outside an assert) is preview-only in Ruff 0.16.9 and is not ported, matching default Ruff. Matching is by attribute name on any object, as Ruff does. Honours `# noqa: PGH005` through suppress.

### `ruff-pt001-pytest-fixture-incorrect-parentheses-style`

Port of Ruff's PT001 (pytest-fixture-incorrect-parentheses-style): matches the first decorator of a function that resolves to `pytest.fixture` or `pytest.yield_fixture` (through `import pytest`, aliases, from-imports, `import pytest.x`, and optionally parenthesized, as `@(pytest.fixture())` or `@(pytest.fixture)()`) when it is a call with no arguments at all (only whitespace, comments or line continuations between the parentheses). Only the first fixture decorator counts, whatever its form, as in Ruff; a class decorated the same way, a bare `@pytest.fixture`, a nested call such as `@pytest.fixture()()`, and names rebound or shadowed in the current scope are not reported. Default setting only (parentheses removed); the opposite style option is not expressed. The finding spans the decorator from `@` to the end of the expression, identical to Ruff, including when a trailing comment follows on the same line (after spaces, tabs, form feeds or a backslash continuation): tree-sitter folds that comment into the decorator node, so the span is trimmed with a named regex group on the node text. The trim is exact because an empty call has no strings, and if the group ever failed to match the finding is still reported, ending after the comment.

### `ruff-pt002-pytest-fixture-positional-args`

Port of Ruff's PT002 (pytest-fixture-positional-args): matches the first decorator of a function that resolves to `pytest.fixture` or `pytest.yield_fixture` (any import spelling or alias, optionally parenthesized) when it is a call with at least one positional argument, including a starred `*args` and a lone generator expression; keyword arguments and `**kwargs` alone do not count. Only the first fixture decorator counts, as in Ruff, so a later positional-argument fixture decorator after a bare or keyword-only one is not reported; classes, nested calls and rebound or shadowed names are not reported. The finding spans the decorator from `@` to the end of the expression, identical to Ruff. tree-sitter folds a same-line trailing comment into the decorator node, so the span is trimmed with a named regex group that tokenizes the decorator text (strings, triple-quoted strings, empty strings followed by a comment or a line continuation, comments) so that a `)` or `#` inside a string or a comment never ends it early, and allows spaces, tabs, form feeds and backslash continuations before the trailing comment; this is linear-time. The one form it cannot tokenize is a Python 3.12 f-string that reuses its own quote inside a replacement field, such as `f"{'"'}"`, followed by a trailing comment: the finding is still reported at the right start but ends after the comment instead of at the closing parenthesis. The message names the keywords that the old positional order mapped to.

### `ruff-pt003-pytest-extraneous-scope-function`

Port of Ruff's PT003 (pytest-extraneous-scope-function): matches, in the first decorator of a function that resolves to `pytest.fixture` or `pytest.yield_fixture` (any import spelling or alias, optionally parenthesized), the first top-level keyword argument named `scope` whose value is a plain string literal equal to `function` after escape decoding: parenthesized, implicitly concatenated (`"func" "tion"`), raw, `u`-prefixed and escaped (`"\x66unction"`) forms count; bytes, f-strings, conditionals, names, other strings and a `scope` inside `**{...}` or a nested call do not. The finding spans just the `scope=...` keyword argument, identical to Ruff (a trailing decorator comment does not affect it). Only the first fixture decorator counts, as in Ruff; classes and rebound or shadowed names are not reported. `pytest_asyncio.fixture` is not covered, as in Ruff's default (non-preview) settings.

### `ruff-pt006-pytest-parametrize-names-wrong-type`

Port of Ruff's PT006 (pytest-parametrize-names-wrong-type): under Ruff's default `parametrize-names-type = tuple`, a call (decorator or not) whose callee, parentheses transparent, resolves to `pytest.mark.parametrize` through the file's imports (any import form or alias, shadowing respected), and that has both an `argnames` and an `argvalues` argument (the keyword, else the first and second positional argument; a starred argument ends the positional lookup, so `*rows` or a missing values argument stays silent). Three findings on the names argument: a plain string literal (`u`/`r` prefixes and implicit concatenation allowed, no `b`/`f`/`t` prefix) that splits at commas into two or more names once trimmed of spaces, tabs and form feeds only (Ruff's whitespace set, not newlines or Unicode spaces), reported on the argument including its outermost parentheses; a one-element tuple or list (comments, line continuations and any element kind, `*x` included), reported on the collection itself; and a list with zero or two or more elements, reported on the list. A parenthesised tuple or list is reported on its own brackets, not on the extra grouping parentheses around it, so the anchor is asymmetric between the string form and the collection forms, as in Ruff. An empty or multi-element tuple is silent. The non-default `csv` and `list` name styles are not modelled.

### `ruff-pt007-pytest-parametrize-values-wrong-type`

Port of Ruff's PT007 (pytest-parametrize-values-wrong-type): under Ruff's defaults (`parametrize-values-type = list`, `parametrize-values-row-type = tuple`), a call whose callee resolves to `pytest.mark.parametrize` (same argument lookup as PT006: keyword or positional, both `argnames` and `argvalues` required, a starred argument ends the positional lookup). Two findings: the values argument is a tuple (any length, `()` and `(1,)` included, parentheses transparent), reported on the tuple; and, when the call is multi-named, every direct element of a list or tuple values argument that is itself a list (parentheses around the row transparent, starred elements skipped), reported on that row list. A call is multi-named unless its names argument is a plain string literal (implicit concatenation and parentheses allowed, no `b`/`f`/`t` prefix) that splits at commas into at most one non-blank name (blank means only spaces, tabs and form feeds, as in Ruff); a bare name, call, f-string, bytes, tuple, list or empty names argument all count as multi-named. A tuple of list rows therefore yields two nested findings, as in Ruff. The row message tells the agent to leave a list alone when the call really has one parameter whose value is that list (names held in a variable, for example), since Ruff still flags it there. The non-default `tuple` values type and `list` row type are not modelled.

### `ruff-pt008-pytest-patch-with-lambda`

Port of Ruff's PT008 (pytest-patch-with-lambda): a call whose callee is, syntactically as Ruff's UnqualifiedName reads it (no name resolution; parentheses transparent), `mocker.patch`, `class_mocker.patch`, `module_mocker.patch`, `package_mocker.patch`, `session_mocker.patch`, `mock.patch` or `unittest.mock.patch` (its `new` argument the keyword `new=`, else the second positional argument when neither it nor an earlier one is `*args`), or one of those followed by `.object` (`new` the keyword, else the third positional argument); with no `return_value=` keyword; whose `new` argument is a `lambda` (parentheses transparent) that has no parameters, or whose body never reads one of its parameters by name (any name expression in the body, nested lambdas' bodies and defaults and comprehensions included). Arguments are read as Ruff's find_argument does, skipping comments and line continuations. Reported on the callee expression.

### `ruff-pt009-pytest-unittest-assertion`

Port of Ruff's PT009 (pytest-unittest-assertion): a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) is an attribute access named one of unittest's assertion methods Ruff lists (`assertEqual`, `assertTrue`, `assertIsNone`, `failUnless`, `assert_`, ... 41 names), on any object and in any context, syntactically as Ruff matches it (no name resolution). Reported on the callee attribute expression.

### `ruff-pt010-pytest-raises-without-exception`

Port of Ruff's PT010 (pytest-raises-without-exception): a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves to `pytest.raises` (any import form or alias, through `resolves`), passing no `expected_exception` (neither the keyword nor a first positional argument that is not `*args`), no `match=` keyword and no `check=` keyword, in any context. Arguments are read from the argument list as Ruff's find_argument does, skipping comments and line continuations. Reported on the callee expression.

### `ruff-pt011-pytest-raises-too-broad`

Port of Ruff's PT011 (pytest-raises-too-broad) under its default `raises-require-match-for` (`BaseException`, `Exception`, `ValueError`, `OSError`, `IOError`, `EnvironmentError`, `socket.error`): a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves to `pytest.raises`, passing no `func` (neither the keyword nor a second positional argument before any `*args`), whose `expected_exception` (the keyword, else the first positional argument if it is not `*args`; parentheses transparent; arguments read as Ruff's find_argument does, skipping comments and line continuations) resolves to one of those names: the unshadowed builtin by its bare name (`builtins.ValueError` is `builtins.ValueError` to Ruff, not `ValueError`), or `socket.error` through the file's imports; with no `match=` keyword or one that is an empty or null string (`None`, `""`, an f-string whose literal parts are empty and whose fields are each such a string; not a t-string or bytes), and no `check=` keyword or `check=None`. Reported on the exception expression, naming its qualified name. As in Ruff, the same check also runs inside string type definitions (annotations, `cast`/`TypeVar` arguments, strings nested in them), which the rule parses with `parse: annotation`. Limits there: a string with an escape sequence or an implicit concatenation is not read (Ruff reports those on the whole string), and an f-string `match=` inside one counts as non-empty.

### `ruff-pt012-pytest-raises-with-multiple-statements`

Port of Ruff's PT012 (pytest-raises-with-multiple-statements): a `with` or `async with` statement with at least one item whose context expression is a call (parentheses and an `as` target transparent, including the parenthesised with-item list and its single-item trailing-comma form `with (pytest.raises(E),):`) whose callee resolves to `pytest.raises` through the file's imports (any import form or alias, shadowing respected), and whose body is too complex, reported on the with statement from its start to the end of its last statement. Too complex means two or more statements in the body (comments ignored, and a trailing `;` is not a statement), reported by one clause, or a single statement that is an `if`, `while`, `match` or `try`, a nested `with` whose own body has two or more statements or a single compound statement (`if`, `while`, `for`, `match`, `with`, `try`, `def`, `class`, decorated definition), or a `for`/`async for` whose body is not exactly one `pass` or one `...` (parentheses around the ellipsis allowed, but `..., 1` or `...,` is a tuple and counts as complex; an `else` clause is not read), reported by the second clause. Allowed bodies are one simple statement, one `def` or `class` (decorated or not, to test decorators), a `for` with a lone `pass` or `...`, and a nested `with` whose single statement is simple. Because the parser keeps trailing comments inside the block, the end of the finding is found by lexing the statement's text (strings, triple-quoted strings and comments) and stopping at the last code token, so trailing comments on the last line or on their own lines are excluded, as in Ruff. Residual divergence: on the last line only, a Python 3.12 f-string that nests the same quote character and contains `#` can make the finding end early.

### `ruff-pt013-pytest-incorrect-pytest-import`

Port of Ruff's PT013 (pytest-incorrect-pytest-import): an `import` statement binding `pytest` or a `pytest.` submodule under an alias other than its own name (`import pytest as pt`, `import pytest.mark as m`; `import pytest as pytest` is fine), once per such alias, and a `from pytest import ...` or `from pytest.<sub> import ...` statement (absolute only: `from .pytest import x` is not one), in any scope. Reported on the whole statement.

### `ruff-pt014-pytest-duplicate-parametrize-test-cases`

Port of Ruff's PT014 (pytest-duplicate-parametrize-test-cases): reports every element of a list or tuple literal that is the argvalues argument of a call resolving to pytest.mark.parametrize (any import form or alias, shadowing respected) when an earlier element of the same literal is structurally equal. argvalues is read as Ruff's find_argument_value does: the argvalues keyword, else the second positional argument counted only before the first starred argument; the literal may itself be parenthesized; the call may be anywhere (decorator or plain call). Equality is Ruff's ComparableExpr: parentheses, comments, spacing, quote style, string prefixes, number spelling (0x1 vs 1) and NFKC identifier spelling are ignored, 1 vs 1.0 vs True differ, and calls such as object() or f() compare equal. The finding is placed on the repeated element without its enclosing parentheses (start and end), as Ruff does. Sets, computed values (a + b) and non list/tuple argvalues report nothing. No known divergence: checked against Ruff on 1,189 Ruff fixture files (only syntax-error fixtures differ, with no findings on either side) plus 17 probe files; a bare follows walk keeps even lists of several thousand elements inside the engine's work budget. Reviewed against 20 more probe files (parenthesized and/or chains and comparison chains, call keyword vs starred order, generator-only arguments, subscript tuples and slices, float and complex spellings including overflow to inf, escapes and line continuations in strings, f-string vs t-string vs string concatenation, starred or keyword arguments after the values, try/except and later rebinding of the pytest import, nested parametrize calls), all identical to Ruff.

### `ruff-pt015-pytest-assert-always-false`

Port of Ruff's PT015 (pytest-assert-always-false): reports an assert statement whose test expression (parentheses transparent; the message is ignored) is falsey under Ruff's Truthiness::from_expr: False, None, a zero number literal (0, 0x0, 0_0, 0.0, 0e0, .0, 0j, 1e-400), an empty str or bytes literal including implicit concatenations of empty literals, an empty f-string (no literal text and only interpolations of empty strings or empty f-strings, with no =, conversion or format spec; f"{b''}" is not empty), an empty list, tuple or dict literal (comments inside allowed), and a call to list, tuple, set, dict or frozenset that resolves to the builtin (not shadowed or rebound, builtins.list() does not count) with no arguments or with exactly one positional argument that is itself such an empty container, string or call (list([]), set(set()), tuple(""); but list(None), list(0), list(*a), list(a=[]) and generator arguments are unknown and not reported). Operators, comparisons, conditionals, names and numbers folded from expressions (1 - 1, not 1, -0) are not reported, as in Ruff. Reported on the whole assert statement. No known divergence: checked against Ruff on 1,189 Ruff fixture files (only syntax-error fixtures differ, with no findings on either side) plus 15 probe files. Raw f-strings ending a line with a backslash count as non-empty text, matching Ruff. Reviewed against 22 more probe files (flow-sensitive builtin bindings: later module or function rebinding, del, annotation-only declarations, class scope vs method, from builtins import, global, conditional rebinding, except-as, walrus in comprehensions, match captures; every zero-number spelling, string prefixes and backslash-newline bodies, f-string format specs, debug and conversions, nested and parenthesized interpolations, t-strings, nested iterable calls), all identical to Ruff.

### `ruff-pt016-pytest-fail-without-message`

Port of Ruff's PT016 (pytest-fail-without-message), `fail_call` in flake8_pytest_style/rules/fail.rs: a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves to `pytest.fail` (through any import form or alias, with `resolves`), whose message, read as Ruff's `find_argument_value("reason", 0)` or else `find_argument_value("msg", 0)` (the `reason=` keyword, else the first positional argument before any starred one, else the `msg=` keyword), is missing or is an empty or null string as `is_empty_or_null_string` reads it: `None`, an empty str literal (implicit concatenations joined; not bytes, not a t-string), or an f-string whose literal parts are empty and whose interpolations each hold (parentheses transparent) such an empty or null string, whatever their conversion, format spec or `=`. Reported on the callee.

### `ruff-pt017-pytest-assert-in-except`

Port of Ruff's PT017 (pytest-assert-in-except): for every except or except* handler that binds a name (as e), reports each assert statement anywhere in the handler body (any nesting: if, loops, with, try, match, nested def, class and lambda bodies) whose test or message contains a Name expression equal to that name, once per assert and handler, on the whole assert statement. A name counts as Ruff's Name expression: attribute names (x.e), keyword names (f(e=1)) and lambda parameters do not, but comprehension targets, walrus targets, f-string interpolations and format specs, and lambda bodies do. A name is compared with NFKC identifier normalization (except Exception as ﬁ: assert fi is reported). Asserts in else, finally, after the try, or in a handler without a name are not reported; nested handlers that bind names used in the same assert report it once per handler, as Ruff does. No known divergence: checked against Ruff on 1,189 Ruff fixture files (only syntax-error fixtures differ, with no findings on either side) plus 16 probe files. Reviewed against 16 more probe files (lambda parameter kinds, nested handlers with and without names, except*, else/finally, match/with/for/while/if bodies, nested def and class bodies, await/yield, soft keywords and print/exec as names, NFKC spellings, noqa placement), all identical to Ruff.

### `ruff-pt018-pytest-composite-assertion`

Port of Ruff's PT018 (pytest-composite-assertion), `composite_condition` in flake8_pytest_style/rules/assertion.rs: an `assert` statement whose test (the expression after `assert`, comments, line continuations and parentheses transparent) is an `and` operation (`a and b`, whatever its operands), or a `not` whose operand (parentheses transparent) is an `or` operation (`not (a or b)`, mixed `and` operands included). An `assert` with a message is reported too. Reported on the `assert` statement.

### `ruff-pt019-pytest-fixture-param-without-value`

Port of Ruff's PT019 (pytest-fixture-param-without-value): reports every non-variadic parameter (positional-only, ordinary or keyword-only, spanning the parameter with its annotation and default) whose name starts with an underscore, in any function whose name starts with test_ (module level, methods, nested functions, sync or async); *args and **kwargs are skipped. The function's decorators are read as Ruff reads them, by the written name pytest.mark.parametrize (parentheses allowed, no import resolution, so mark.parametrize, pt.mark.parametrize and from-imports do not count): a parameter is silent when the argnames argument (positional or argnames=) is a string literal whose comma-separated, trimmed items include it, or a list or tuple whose string literals, trimmed but not split, include it; f-strings and bytes are ignored. When any parametrize decorator has a bare name as argnames, or a list or tuple containing a bare name, the whole function is silent. The argnames keyword wins over a positional first argument, and a starred argument before or at the first position hides it, as in Ruff. Divergences: items of a comma-separated string are tested only up to the 12th, so a name that appears only as the 13th or later item is still reported; and parameter names are compared as written, while Ruff compares the NFKC-normalized name, so a name written with a compatibility character (such as the ligature U+FB01) can differ from Ruff.

### `ruff-pt020-pytest-deprecated-yield-fixture`

Port of Ruff's PT020 (pytest-deprecated-yield-fixture): reports the decorator of a sync, async or method function definition whose expression (bare or called, parentheses allowed, through `import pytest as alias` or `from pytest import yield_fixture as alias`) resolves to pytest.yield_fixture, only when no earlier decorator of that function is already pytest.fixture or pytest.yield_fixture, because Ruff inspects only the first such decorator. The span is the decorator from the @ to the end of its expression, excluding a trailing comment (a second clause anchors on a regex group of the decorator text for decorators that carry one). Class decorators are not checked, as in Ruff. No divergences found on the porter's 13 probe files and 15 review probe files (star imports, late and function-local imports, try/except and if/else rebinding, own-line comments after decorators, line continuations, strings containing #, noqa forms).

### `ruff-pt021-pytest-fixture-finalizer-callback`

Port of Ruff's PT021 (pytest-fixture-finalizer-callback): in a function decorated with pytest.fixture or pytest.yield_fixture (resolved through imports, parentheses allowed) that has a parameter named request (any kind, including *request and **request), reports the last call spelled request.addfinalizer(...) (the object and the callee may be parenthesised) that Ruff's body visitor reaches, one per fixture, at the call's span. The visitor skips nested function definitions (so factory-style fixtures and nested fixtures are judged on their own bodies), does not descend into return values, yield values, lambdas, comprehensions, await, f-strings, operators, subscripts (including subscripted, union and dotted annotations), attributes, collections or starred arguments, but does reach calls nested through call arguments, keyword values, ** arguments, conditions, loops, with items, annotations, type-parameter bounds and class bodies, bases and decorators. 'Last' follows Ruff's visiting order, which is source order except that an annotated assignment visits its value before its annotation and a type alias visits its value before its type-parameter bounds. pytest_asyncio.fixture is not a fixture here (preview only in Ruff). No divergences found on the porter's 15 probe files and 15 review probe files.

### `ruff-pt022-pytest-useless-yield-fixture`

Port of Ruff's PT022 (pytest-useless-yield-fixture): flags the last statement of a function decorated with `pytest.fixture` or `pytest.yield_fixture` (resolved through imports, parentheses and a called decorator allowed) when that statement is a plain `yield` expression statement (bare, valued or parenthesized, not `yield from`) and the body holds exactly one `yield` as Ruff counts them. Ruff's walker only reaches yields in statement-level expressions (including class bases and decorators of nested classes) and in call callees, arguments and `**` keywords, so a `yield` inside an operator, attribute, subscript, container, `*` argument, comprehension, lambda, `await`, f-string, `return` value or another `yield`'s value is not counted, and nested `def`s are skipped together with their decorators; the document stops its search at the same nodes. Skipped when a decorator is a bare reference to `abc.abstractmethod`, `abstractclassmethod`, `abstractstaticmethod` or `abstractproperty` (a called `@abc.abstractmethod()` does not exempt it, as in Ruff). `pytest_asyncio.fixture` is not a fixture here because Ruff 0.16.9 matches it only in preview. Reports the whole `yield` statement, excluding trailing comments.

### `ruff-pt023-pytest-incorrect-mark-parentheses-style`

Port of Ruff's PT023 (pytest-incorrect-mark-parentheses-style): flags a decorator on a function or class whose expression is a call, with no positional and no keyword arguments, of a name that resolves through imports to exactly `pytest.mark.<name>` (any marker, so `usefixtures()` and `parametrize()` with only comments inside also match; `pytest.mark.foo.bar()`, `pytest.mark()`, a name imported from deeper than a marker such as `from pytest.mark.sub import bar` or `import pytest.mark.a.b as x`, and an unimported or shadowed `pytest` do not). Outer and inner parentheses, line continuations and comments inside the empty parentheses are accepted, as Ruff does. Only the default `mark-parentheses = false` setting is ported; the mode that demands parentheses is not. Reports the decorator from `@` to the end of the expression, excluding a trailing comment.

### `ruff-pt024-pytest-unnecessary-asyncio-mark-on-fixture`

Port of Ruff's PT024 (pytest-unnecessary-asyncio-mark-on-fixture): flags every decorator that resolves through imports to `pytest.mark.asyncio` (bare, called with any arguments, parenthesized, above or below the fixture decorator) on a function that also carries a `pytest.fixture` or `pytest.yield_fixture` decorator (bare or called once), sync or async. Classes, `pytest.mark.asyncio.x`, a doubly called mark or fixture, and unresolved or rebound `pytest` names do not match. `pytest_asyncio.fixture` is not treated as a fixture because Ruff 0.16.9 only matches it in preview. Reports the decorator from `@` to the end of the expression, excluding a trailing comment; strings (including triple-quoted ones holding quotes and `#`) are skipped when finding that comment.

### `ruff-pt025-pytest-erroneous-use-fixtures-on-fixture`

Port of Ruff's PT025 (pytest-erroneous-use-fixtures-on-fixture): reports each decorator on a function or async function (at any nesting level) that resolves, through imports and aliases (`import pytest as pt`, `from pytest import mark as m`, `from pytest.mark import usefixtures as uf`, `import pytest.mark`), to `pytest.mark.usefixtures`, bare or called and with any arguments, when the same function also carries a `pytest.fixture` or `pytest.yield_fixture` decorator before or after it (bare, called or parenthesized). Every such mark is reported, so a fixture with two `usefixtures` marks gets two findings. The finding spans the decorator from `@` to the end of its expression, so a trailing comment on the decorator line is left out as in Ruff; the span is found by a regex that tokenizes strings, f-strings (including Python 3.12 nested same-quote f-strings such as `f"{"#"}"`) and comments. Decorated classes, plain test functions, unbound, deleted, shadowed or rebound `pytest` names (parameters, assignments, `except ... as`, star imports), calls of calls, other `usefixtures` look-alikes, and `pytest_asyncio.fixture` (preview-only in Ruff) are not reported. Divergence: a decorator with a trailing comment whose arguments hold a triple-quoted string containing a quote character is still reported at the right start, but its end includes the trailing comment.

### `ruff-pt026-pytest-use-fixtures-without-parameters`

Port of Ruff's PT026 (pytest-use-fixtures-without-parameters): reports a decorator on a function, async function or class that resolves to `pytest.mark.usefixtures` and is either an attribute access with no call (`@pytest.mark.usefixtures`, `@mark.usefixtures`, parenthesized forms included) or a call with no arguments and no keywords (`@pytest.mark.usefixtures()`, also an imported bare name called with empty parentheses, with comments or line continuations inside the parentheses). Ruff checks a class's decorators for PT026 only when PT023 is also enabled, as with `--select PT` or the pack's recording. This port always checks them; with PT026 alone, Ruff skips classes. A bare imported name such as `@usefixtures` after `from pytest.mark import usefixtures` is not reported, as in Ruff. Calls with `*args`, `**kwargs`, a generator argument or any argument are not reported; shadowed or rebound `pytest` names and look-alikes from other modules are not reported. The finding spans the decorator from `@` to the end of its expression, leaving out a trailing comment. Divergence: a decorator with a trailing comment whose arguments hold a triple-quoted string containing a quote character cannot be an empty call, so none exists for this rule.

### `ruff-pt027-pytest-unittest-raises-assertion`

Port of Ruff's PT027 (pytest-unittest-raises-assertion), `unittest_raises_assertion_call` and `unittest_raises_assertion_binding` in flake8_pytest_style/rules/assertion.rs: a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) is an attribute access `<x>.assertRaises`, `<x>.failUnlessRaises`, `<x>.assertRaisesRegex` or `<x>.assertRaisesRegexp`, whatever `<x>` is. The call path skips a call that is itself (parentheses transparent) the context expression of a `with` item with an `as` target, and the binding path reports it again only when that target is a plain name, so such a call with a tuple, list, attribute, subscript or starred target is not reported. Reported on the callee (the attribute, inside any parentheses), with the attribute name in the message.

### `ruff-pt028-pytest-parameter-with-default-argument`

Port of Ruff's PT028 (pytest-parameter-with-default-argument): reports the default value of every positional-only, positional or keyword-only parameter (not `*args` or `**kwargs`, annotated or not, parenthesized defaults reported at the inner expression as Ruff does, tuples and generators with their own parentheses) of a def or async def whose name starts with `test` and that is either in the module scope (also inside if/try/with/for/match blocks) or directly in a class whose name starts with `Test` and whose body has no direct `__init__` method (decorated or async included; an `__init__` nested in an if does not count). Functions nested in other functions, in non-Test classes or in a class nested in a non-Test class are not reported, and lambdas are never reported. No divergence from Ruff found.

### `ruff-pt030-pytest-warns-too-broad`

Port of Ruff's PT030 (pytest-warns-too-broad): a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves through the file's imports to `pytest.warns` (any import form or alias), whose expected warning is `Warning`, `UserWarning` or `DeprecationWarning` (Ruff's default `warns-require-match-for`; the setting is not configurable here), and that passes no usable `match=`.
