The warning is read as Ruff's find_argument_value does: the first `expected_warning` keyword if present, else the first positional argument unless a `*args` precedes it; `**kwargs` names no keyword; parentheses around the warning are transparent; comments and line continuations in the argument list are skipped. A `match` is unusable when there is no `match=` keyword (a second positional argument is not `match`) or the first one is `None`, an empty string (implicit concatenations of empty parts too), or an f-string whose literal parts are empty and whose interpolations are each an empty string or `None` (conversions, format specs and `=` ignored, as in Ruff; bytes and t-strings are never empty). The warning name must be the unshadowed builtin, or an import whose qualified name is exactly that word (so `import Warning as W` fires and `from builtins import Warning` does not, as in Ruff). The callable form `pytest.warns(Warning, func)` is reported too, as Ruff reports it, with its own message: there pytest ignores `match=`, so the advice is to switch to the context-manager form. Reported on the warning expression without its parentheses, naming it in the message. As in Ruff, the check also runs inside string type definitions (annotations, `cast`/`TypeVar` arguments, strings nested in them), which the rule parses with `parse: annotation`. Limits there: a string with an escape sequence or an implicit concatenation is not read (Ruff reports those on the whole string), and an f-string `match=` inside one counts as non-empty. Divergences (engine): a `global Warning` declaration in a function counts as a shadowing binding even when the module never binds the name, and in `.pyi` stubs an annotation-only module binding (`Warning: type`) shadows the builtin, where Ruff fires in both cases.

### `ruff-pt031-pytest-warns-with-multiple-statements`

Port of Ruff's PT031 (pytest-warns-with-multiple-statements): a `with` or `async with` statement with at least one item whose context expression is a call to `pytest.warns` (callee resolved through imports, parentheses transparent, with or without `as`, in parenthesized item lists and as the lone item before a trailing comma), whose body is not exactly one allowed statement. Allowed single statements: any simple statement; a `with`/`async with` whose own body is a single simple statement; a `def`, `async def` or `class`, decorated or not; a `for` or `async for` whose body is a lone `pass` or `...` (parenthesized `...` too; an `else` clause is ignored, as in Ruff). Not allowed: several statements (also `;`-separated ones), a lone `if`, `while`, `try`, `match`, or a `for` with any other body, and a nested `with` whose body has several statements or one compound statement. Comments, line continuations and `;` do not count as statements. Two messages: several statements (move the others out) and a single compound statement (move the control flow out). Reported from the start of the `with` statement to the end of its last statement, matching Ruff's statement range: a regex capture on the statement's text trims trailing comments, semicolons and whitespace, and reads string literals (single, triple, any prefix) so a `#` inside a string is not taken for a comment. Divergence: a Python 3.12 f-string that reuses its own quote inside a replacement field and holds a `#` (for example `f"{x["#"]}"`) on the last statement's line can end the span early; the start, count and suppression still agree.

### `ruff-pth124-py-path`

Port of Ruff's PTH124 (py-path): reports every call whose callee resolves through the file's imports to py.path.local (import py, import py.path, from py import path, from py.path import local, with or without aliases, and a parenthesized callee), at the callee, whatever the arguments are. Names shadowed by a parameter, a local, a match capture, a type parameter or a later rebinding do not match, and py.path.svnwc, py.path.local.sysfind, py._path.local.LocalPath and bare references are not calls of it, as in Ruff. A second clause reports the same call inside a string Ruff reads as a type definition (x: "py.path.local('f')", typing generics, TypeAlias values, not Literal or Annotated metadata), including strings nested up to three levels deep (x: "List['py.path.local(1)']") and a callee in up to two parentheses. Divergence: inside such a string a callee wrapped in three or more parentheses, or a fourth nested string level, is not reported.

### `ruff-pth201-path-constructor-current-directory`

Port of Ruff's PTH201 (path-constructor-current-directory): reports a call whose callee resolves to pathlib.Path or pathlib.PurePath (stable mode only; PosixPath, WindowsPath and PackagePath are not checked) when its single argument, with no keyword arguments, is a plain string literal whose decoded value is "" or ".". Escapes and implicit concatenation are decoded ("\x2e", "\N{full stop}", "" "."), a parenthesized string counts, and the finding is anchored at the string, parentheses excluded. Bytes, f-strings and t-strings (including such a part in a concatenation) do not match, nor do calls with two arguments, a starred argument or a keyword. A second clause reports the same call inside a string Ruff reads as a type definition (x: "Path('.')", typing generics, TypeAlias values, not Literal or Annotated metadata), including strings nested up to three levels deep and a callee in up to two parentheses. Divergence: inside such a string a callee in three or more parentheses, a string argument parenthesized more than once, or a fourth nested string level is not reported.

### `ruff-pth202-os-path-getsize`

Port of Ruff's PTH202 (os-path-getsize): reports a call whose callee resolves through the file's imports to os.path.getsize (import os, os.path, aliases, from os.path import getsize, a parenthesized callee) and whose only argument is one positional value or the keyword filename (Ruff's find_argument_value over a single argument), anchored at the callee. Calls with a second argument, a starred or double-starred argument, no argument or another keyword are not reported; a lone generator expression is. Shadowing by parameters, locals, comprehension, loop, match-capture and type-parameter bindings, and later rebinding follows Ruff; posixpath/ntpath spellings are not os.path. A second clause reports the same call inside a string Ruff reads as a type definition (x: "os.path.getsize('f')", typing generics, TypeAlias values, the first Annotated argument, not Literal or Annotated metadata), including strings nested up to three levels deep (x: "List['os.path.getsize(1)']"). Divergence: inside such a string a callee wrapped in three or more parentheses, or a fourth nested string level, is not reported.

### `ruff-pth203-os-path-getatime`

Port of Ruff's PTH203 (os-path-getatime): reports a call whose callee resolves through the file's imports to os.path.getatime (import os, os.path, aliases, from os.path import getatime, a parenthesized callee) and whose only argument is one positional value or the keyword filename (Ruff's find_argument_value over a single argument), anchored at the callee. Calls with a second argument, a starred or double-starred argument, no argument or another keyword are not reported; a lone generator expression is. Shadowing by parameters, locals, comprehension, loop, match-capture and type-parameter bindings, and later rebinding follows Ruff; posixpath/ntpath spellings are not os.path. A second clause reports the same call inside a string Ruff reads as a type definition (x: "os.path.getatime('f')", typing generics, TypeAlias values, the first Annotated argument, not Literal or Annotated metadata), including strings nested up to three levels deep (x: "List['os.path.getatime(1)']"). Divergence: inside such a string a callee wrapped in three or more parentheses, or a fourth nested string level, is not reported.

### `ruff-pth204-os-path-getmtime`

Port of Ruff's PTH204 (os-path-getmtime): reports a call whose callee resolves through the file's imports to os.path.getmtime (import os, os.path, aliases, from os.path import getmtime, a parenthesized callee) and whose only argument is one positional value or the keyword filename (Ruff's find_argument_value over a single argument), anchored at the callee. Calls with a second argument, a starred or double-starred argument, no argument or another keyword are not reported; a lone generator expression is. Shadowing by parameters, locals, comprehension, loop, match-capture and type-parameter bindings, and later rebinding follows Ruff; posixpath/ntpath spellings are not os.path. A second clause reports the same call inside a string Ruff reads as a type definition (x: "os.path.getmtime('f')", typing generics, TypeAlias values, the first Annotated argument, not Literal or Annotated metadata), including strings nested up to three levels deep (x: "List['os.path.getmtime(1)']"). Divergence: inside such a string a callee wrapped in three or more parentheses, or a fourth nested string level, is not reported.

### `ruff-pth205-os-path-getctime`

Port of Ruff's PTH205 (os-path-getctime): reports a call whose callee resolves through the file's imports to os.path.getctime (import os, os.path, aliases, from os.path import getctime, a parenthesized callee) and whose only argument is one positional value or the keyword filename (Ruff's find_argument_value over a single argument), anchored at the callee. Calls with a second argument, a starred or double-starred argument, no argument or another keyword are not reported; a lone generator expression is. Shadowing by parameters, locals, comprehension, loop, match-capture and type-parameter bindings, and later rebinding follows Ruff; posixpath/ntpath spellings are not os.path. A second clause reports the same call inside a string Ruff reads as a type definition (x: "os.path.getctime('f')", typing generics, TypeAlias values, the first Annotated argument, not Literal or Annotated metadata), including strings nested up to three levels deep (x: "List['os.path.getctime(1)']"). Divergence: inside such a string a callee wrapped in three or more parentheses, or a fourth nested string level, is not reported.

### `ruff-pth206-os-sep-split`

Port of Ruff's PTH206 (os-sep-split): reports a call to a method named `split`, on any receiver (a parenthesized callee such as `(p.split)(os.sep)` included), that has exactly one argument, given positionally or as `sep=`, whose value resolves through imports to `os.sep` (`os.sep`, `o.sep` after `import os as o`, `S` after `from os import sep as S`, with or without parentheses). A second argument (`maxsplit`), a lone starred or double-starred argument, and other separators (`os.altsep`, `os.pathsep`, `posixpath.sep`, a literal `"/"`, a local `sep`) are not reported; shadowed or rebound `os` and `sep` names do not resolve and are not reported. The finding is anchored on the `split` identifier, as in Ruff. Differs from Ruff only in that calls inside string (forward-reference) annotations, such as `x: "p.split(os.sep)"`, are not visited.

### `ruff-pth207-glob`

Port of Ruff's PTH207 (glob): reports calls that resolve through imports to `glob.glob` or `glob.iglob` (module attribute, from-import, aliases, parenthesized callee), anchored on the callee as Ruff does, unless the `dir_fd` keyword is present with a value other than `None` (a parenthesized `None` still counts as `None`; `**kwargs` and extra positional arguments are never read as `dir_fd`, so those calls are reported). Every other argument shape is reported, including a call with no arguments. Two clauses give `glob.glob` (returns a list) and `glob.iglob` (lazy) their own advice. Names bound locally to another object (a parameter or variable called `glob`) do not resolve and are not reported. Differs from Ruff only in that calls inside string annotations are not visited. Behaviour matches Ruff on its test file and on 30 probe files (porter's and reviewer's); only the not-overfit gate fails, because of the recording (see engine_gaps).

### `ruff-pth208-os-listdir`

Port of Ruff's PTH208 (os-listdir): reports calls resolving to `os.listdir`, anchored on the callee, unless the path argument (`path=` or the first positional before any starred argument) looks like a file descriptor: a plain integer literal; a name, or a call chain on a name, whose only binding in its scope is typed int (annotated `int`, also parenthesized or subscripted, a non-variadic parameter annotated `int`, a function returning `int`, a with-item `as name` or an assignment, walrus or same-arity unpacking whose value is an `int(...)` call or an expression Ruff's type inference calls int); or a `Class.attr` whose class-scope binding is an int-annotated or int-valued assignment or a method returning `int`. The int inference follows Ruff's, not the runtime value: integer literals, unary `-`/`+`/`~` and binary operators other than `/` and `@` over int or bool operands, and conditionals and `and`/`or` chains whose branches are all int or bool with at least one int are int; so `True & True` and `True or 1` are int, while `1 or 2.5`, `(1 < 2) + 1` and `int("3") + 1` are not. A call with no arguments is reported with current-directory advice, and one with only starred or unknown arguments with generic advice; a path that is a string, bytes, `Path`, an unknown call or an unknown name is reported. Rebound names, string annotations such as `"int"`, `Optional[int]`, `int | None` and `Annotated[int, ...]` are not file descriptors, as in Ruff. Limits: nested class attributes, a class attribute bound twice, unpacking with unequal arity or from a set, and tuple targets of with-items (see reason). Calls inside string annotations are not visited.

### `ruff-pth210-invalid-pathlib-with-suffix`

Port of Ruff's PTH210 (invalid-pathlib-with-suffix): reports a `.with_suffix(<string>)` call with exactly one argument, positional or `suffix=`, that is a plain string literal (implicit concatenation allowed; f-strings, t-strings and bytes are not), when the receiver is provably a pathlib path. The receiver is either a call of one of the six classes `Path`, `PosixPath`, `PurePath`, `PurePosixPath`, `PureWindowsPath`, `WindowsPath` (through imports, aliases and parentheses), or a name whose only binding in its scope, with no earlier binding it shadows (Ruff's only_binding), is typed that way: assigned such a call (also by walrus, a with-item with a plain name target, or tuple unpacking with matching arity), annotated with such a class, parenthesized or not (the annotation alone decides, whatever the value), a non-variadic parameter annotated with one, or a function whose return annotation is one. Attribute receivers such as `self.p`, subscripts, `Path().parent` and quoted or Optional annotations are not recognised, as in Ruff. A non-empty suffix not starting with a dot is reported with advice to add the dot; the exact suffix `"."` is reported with its own advice only when the target Python version is below 3.14, as in Ruff. An empty suffix and suffixes of two or more characters starting with a dot are not reported. The finding spans the whole call. Differs from Ruff in two contrived binding forms, unpacking with unequal arity (`a, b = Path(), 1, 2`) and a tuple with-target (`with (Path(), 1) as (c, d)`), which are not recognised as paths; calls inside string annotations are not visited either. Tested on Ruff's three PTH210 files and 43 probe files.

### `ruff-pth211-os-symlink`

Port of Ruff's PTH211 (os-symlink): reports calls resolving through imports to `os.symlink` (module attribute, aliases, from-import, parenthesized callee), anchored on the callee as Ruff does, unless the `dir_fd` keyword is present with a value other than `None` (a parenthesized `None` still counts as `None`; `**kwargs` and extra positional arguments are never read as `dir_fd`, so those calls are reported). Every other argument shape is reported, including unknown keywords, byte-string paths and a call with no arguments, as in Ruff. Locally rebound `os` or `symlink` names do not resolve and are not reported. Differs from Ruff only in that calls inside string annotations are not visited.

### `ruff-pyi006-bad-version-info-comparison`

Port of Ruff's PYI006 (bad-version-info-comparison): checks `.py` and `.pyi` files. It flags a single-operator comparison whose left operand resolves to `sys.version_info` (through import aliases, parentheses transparent, not subscripted or attribute-accessed) when the operator is anything but `<` or `>=`, namely `>`, `<=`, `==`, `!=`, `in`, `not in`, `is` or `is not`, whatever the right operand is. It looks at the test of an `if` and of every `elif`, and at each operand of a top-level `and`/`or` chain, but not at nested or negated boolean expressions, `while`, conditional expressions, asserts or comprehensions. The `<` comparisons that Ruff reports as a separate rule (branch ordering) are not part of this document. The reported span is the whole comparison. Four clauses (`>`, `<=`, `==`/`!=`, membership and identity) give operator-specific advice. No known divergence from Ruff on 32 porter probe files and 25 independent review probe files (name resolution and shadowing, nested boolean chains, new syntax, whitespace, suppression comments).

### `ruff-pyi013-ellipsis-in-non-empty-class-body`

Port of Ruff's PYI013 (ellipsis-in-non-empty-class-body): matches an expression statement whose whole expression is `...` (also when parenthesized, or followed by a semicolon) directly in a class body that has at least one other statement, in stubs and in regular Python files, anchored on the statement. A class body holding only `...` is left alone, and so is a tuple statement that starts with `...` (`..., x` or `...,`), which Ruff does not treat as an ellipsis.

### `ruff-pyi016-duplicate-union-member`

Port of Ruff's PYI016 (duplicate-union-member): walks `Union[...]`, `X | Y` chains and `Optional[...]` (resolved through typing, typing_extensions and _typeshed imports), through parentheses and nested unions, and reports each member structurally equal to an earlier one, on the later member; `Optional[X]` counts as a `None` member (reported on the `Optional` name when an earlier member already is `None`), and unions made only of `None` are reported too. A parenthesized tuple that is the whole slice of `Union[...]` is flattened, while a tuple beside other members or followed by a trailing comma is one member. Pipe chains are checked only where Ruff treats the expression as a type definition (annotations, TypeAlias values, cast, TypeVar, NewType, TypeForm and TypeAliasType arguments, NamedTuple and TypedDict functional field types, mypy_extensions Arg helpers, type statements, generic slices, string annotations), while `Union[...]` is checked anywhere. String annotations are parsed; for implicitly concatenated strings the report spans the whole string. Known divergences are listed under partial.

### `ruff-pyi018-unused-private-type-var`

Port of Ruff's PYI018 (unused-private-type-var): reports an underscore-prefixed name at module or function level (also at class level in stub files) whose latest binding is an unannotated single-target assignment of a call to TypeVar, ParamSpec or TypeVarTuple (from typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed, through aliases and parentheses) and that is never read; names listed in `__all__` count as used. Reports the name. A function that calls `locals()` makes the engine's usedness unknown, so a separate clause covers it the way Ruff does: there a private type variable is reported when no other occurrence of its name in that function reads it.

### `ruff-pyi019-custom-type-var-for-self`

Port of Ruff's PYI019 (custom-type-var-for-self): reports a method directly in a class that is not a metaclass (a base of `type`, `type[...]`, `abc.ABCMeta`, `enum.EnumMeta`/`EnumType`, also through local classes) and is not a static method, abstract method or overload, whose first positional parameter is annotated with a TypeVar (an unannotated assignment of a typing TypeVar call) or a PEP 695 type parameter of the method itself: `self: _S` for instance methods, `cls: type[_S]` (builtin `type`) for class methods, `__new__`, `__init_subclass__` and `__class_getitem__`, also inside a quoted annotation. In a quoted annotation the name is looked up by text as Ruff resolves it: the class's latest binding when it is a TypeVar, else the first enclosing function or the module that binds it, whose latest binding must be a TypeVar. The range runs from the end of the method name to the end of the return annotation, excluding parentheses around it and comments before the closing one, or to the closing parenthesis of the parameters when there is no return annotation.

### `ruff-pyi024-collections-named-tuple`

Port of Ruff's PYI024 (collections-named-tuple): flags every name or attribute reference that resolves to `collections.namedtuple` through the file's imports (`import collections`, `import collections as c`, `import collections.abc`, `from collections import namedtuple as nt`), in .py and .pyi files alike, whether it is called, annotated, used as a base, a decorator, a match value pattern (`case collections.namedtuple.x:` is reported on its `collections.namedtuple` prefix) or just referenced. Attribute references are reported in every context, as Ruff does: `del collections.namedtuple`, `collections.namedtuple = 1` and `collections.namedtuple += 1` are findings. Bare names are reported only when loaded: store, augmented-assignment and delete targets (`namedtuple += 1`, `del namedtuple`) and shadowed names are not. A second clause reads string annotations (forward references such as `x: "namedtuple"` or `cast("collections.namedtuple", v)`) the way Ruff does and anchors the finding on the name inside the string; strings Ruff cannot map back (escapes, implicit concatenation) anchor on the whole string. Strings that are not type definitions (plain assignments, Literal arguments) are left alone. No divergence from Ruff found.

### `ruff-pyi025-unaliased-collections-abc-set-import`

Port of Ruff's PYI025 (unaliased-collections-abc-set-import): flags each `from collections.abc import Set` binding whose local name is not `AbstractSet`, in any scope (module, function, class, conditional or try blocks), in .py and .pyi files. The finding spans the bound name: the bare `Set`, or the alias after `as` (so `Set as S`, `Set as Set` and `Set as _` are reported on the alias, while `Set as AbstractSet` is silent). Only from-imports count, as Ruff checks only from-import bindings: `import collections.abc.Set as S` is not reported. Relative imports, `import collections.abc`, `from collections import abc`, star imports and other members such as MutableSet are not reported. No divergence from Ruff found.

### `ruff-pyi030-unnecessary-literal-union`

Port of Ruff's PYI030 (unnecessary-literal-union): reports, anywhere in .py and .pyi code (annotations, values, type aliases, type parameters), the outermost union (a `|` expression or `typing.Union[...]`, not nested in another union through parentheses or a Union slice) whose members, flattened through nested unions, include two or more `Literal[...]` subscripts resolving to typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed; Optional is not traversed. String annotations are parsed: a simple string reports the union at its exact span; a complex one (implicitly concatenated or holding escape sequences) is reported at the whole string, reproducing Ruff's quirk that every name in it resolves like the last resolvable name, so it fires only when that name is `Literal` (or the typing module, for `typing.Literal` members) and then counts any name-valued subscript, e.g. `"list[int] | Literal[1]" ""`.

### `ruff-pyi032-any-eq-ne-annotation`

Port of Ruff's PYI032 (any-eq-ne-annotation): in .py and .pyi files, reports the annotation of the second regular parameter of `__eq__` or `__ne__` defined directly in a class when it resolves to `Any` from typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed (aliases and attribute forms included), written bare, parenthesized (reported without the parentheses) or as a string annotation (reported at the whole string). The method needs exactly two regular parameters, as Ruff counts them: positional-only parameters before `/` are not counted, and keyword-only, `*args` and `**kwargs` parameters do not matter. Divergence: in stubs, Rerail resolves the name from the module scope, so an alias imported later than its use, or a function- or class-local rebinding of `Any`, is not honoured as Ruff does.

### `ruff-pyi034-non-self-return-type`

Port of Ruff's PYI034 (non-self-return-type): reports the method name of a method defined in a class body (also under if, try, for, with and match inside the class, not inside a nested function) that has at least one positional parameter and a return annotation, in two forms. First, the annotation is the class's own name (bare, parenthesized, or a quoted string holding just that name, itself optionally parenthesized) and the method is an in-place operator dunder (`__iadd__`, `__isub__`, `__imul__`, `__imatmul__`, `__itruediv__`, `__ifloordiv__`, `__imod__`, `__ipow__`, `__ilshift__`, `__irshift__`, `__iand__`, `__ixor__`, `__ior__`), a non-async `__enter__` or `__new__`, or an `async def __aenter__`; the last three are skipped when the class is decorated with `final` from typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed. Second, a non-async `__iter__` annotated with `Iterable` or `Iterator` (subscripted or not, from typing or collections.abc) in a class that lists `Iterator` among its bases directly or through base classes defined in the same file (also when that base is subscripted, as in `class B(It[int])`), and likewise `__aiter__` with `AsyncIterable`/`AsyncIterator`. Methods decorated with an abc abstract decorator or with `overload` (resolved through imports) are skipped, as are classes that look like metaclasses: a base resolving to `type`, `abc.ABCMeta`, `enum.EnumMeta` or `enum.EnumType`, `type[...]`, a one-argument `type(...)` call, a same-file subclass of such a class (also through a subscripted base such as `Meta[int]`), or any class with bases that defines a five-parameter `__new__` whose second to fourth parameters are annotated `str`, `tuple` and `dict` and whose last is `**kwargs`. Quoted return annotations are read only for the class-name form, as Ruff does. Divergence: in `.pyi` files a base class that is imported, defined or rebound after the class using it is resolved by the engine to its final binding, while Ruff resolves it where it is written; `.py` files agree.

### `ruff-pyi036-bad-exit-annotation`

Port of Ruff's PYI036 (bad-exit-annotation): checks `__exit__` (non-async) and `async def __aexit__` methods defined directly in a class body, reporting at the same spans Ruff does: the parameter list when fewer than three positional parameters after `self` and no `*args` are present; a `*args` annotation that is not `object` (or `_typeshed.Unused`); positional parameters after the first three that lack a default; keyword-only parameters without a default; the annotation of the first, second or third argument unless it is `object`/`Unused` or a union whose non-`None` member is `type[BaseException]` (no trailing comma in the subscript), `BaseException` and `types.TracebackType` respectively. Unions are read as Ruff reads them: `Optional[X]`; `Union[X, None]` or `Union[None, X]` with exactly two members, also written as a single parenthesized tuple `Union[(X, None)]` and through attribute access such as `t.Union`; and `X | Y` takes `X` unless it is `None` (so a `|` union with no `None` is judged by its left member, and a plain annotation with no union is reported). For exactly two `overload`-decorated methods of the same sync/async kind with exactly four plain positional parameters each, it reports the parameter list of an overload that is neither the `None, None, None` form nor the `type[BaseException], BaseException, TracebackType` form (loosened by unannotated, `object` and `Unused`). Overloads are otherwise checked only through that last rule. No divergence found; the document is large (about 19 KB minified) because the three argument annotations and the overload pairing are written with shared utilities.

### `ruff-pyi042-snake-case-type-alias`

Port of Ruff's PYI042 (snake-case-type-alias): reports the name of a type alias that is not CamelCase, meaning it starts with an ASCII lowercase letter or digit, optionally after one leading underscore (`__x`, `_`, `_Name` and non-ASCII initials such as `éclair` are fine). Matches the target name (not the annotation) of an annotated assignment, with or without a value, whose annotation resolves through imports to `TypeAlias` from `typing`, `typing_extensions` or `_typeshed` (from-imports, `import ... as`, qualified names and a parenthesized annotation all count; a shadowed or rebound name, a `global` declaration in the function, a string annotation, a subscripted `TypeAlias[...]` and wrappers such as `Annotated[...]` or `X | None` do not), and the name of a PEP 695 `type` statement, with or without type parameters, at any nesting level. A plain name is reported, as is a parenthesized name such as `(x): TypeAlias = int`; attribute, subscript and tuple targets are not, and soft keywords used as names (`type: TypeAlias = int`) are reported like any other name. Applies to `.py` and `.pyi` alike. A module- or class-level statement sees the bindings made before it; a function body sees the whole module, as Ruff reads function bodies after the module. Divergences: in a stub or a module with `from __future__ import annotations`, a `TypeAlias` imported only later in the file is still recognised and one rebound later at module level is not (Ruff looks it up at the statement); and in a file with a syntax error Ruff reports nothing while the document still reports outside the error region.

### `ruff-pyi043-t-suffixed-type-alias`

Port of Ruff's PYI043 (t-suffixed-type-alias): reports the name of a private type alias (the name starts with an underscore) that ends in an ASCII lowercase letter followed by `T` and at most one digit (`_aT`, `_aT1`, `__aT`; not `_T`, `_AT`, `_0T`, `_aT12`, `_aTT`, `_a_T`, `_aTx` or a non-ASCII letter before the `T`). The alias is recognised exactly as for PYI042: the target name of an annotated assignment, with or without a value, whose annotation resolves through imports to `TypeAlias` from `typing`, `typing_extensions` or `_typeshed` (from-imports, `import ... as`, qualified names and a parenthesized annotation all count; a shadowed or rebound name, a `global` declaration in the function, a string annotation, a subscripted `TypeAlias[...]` and wrappers such as `Annotated[...]` or `X | None` do not), a parenthesized name target, or the name of a PEP 695 `type` statement, with or without type parameters. Attribute, subscript and tuple targets are not reported. Applies to `.py` and `.pyi`. The suffix test is spelled as three regexes (starts with `_`, ends in `T` plus an optional digit, and not preceded by a non-lowercase character) which together equal Ruff's character test. A module- or class-level statement sees the bindings made before it; a function body sees the whole module, as Ruff reads function bodies after the module. Divergences: in a stub or a module with `from __future__ import annotations`, a `TypeAlias` imported only later in the file is still recognised and one rebound later at module level is not (Ruff looks it up at the statement); and in a file with a syntax error Ruff reports nothing while the document still reports outside the error region.

### `ruff-pyi045-iter-method-return-iterable`

Port of Ruff's PYI045 (iter-method-return-iterable): in a class body (including under `if`/`try`/`with`/`for`/`while`/`match`, and classes nested in functions or other classes), reports the return annotation of a method named `__iter__` that resolves through imports to `typing.Iterable` or `collections.abc.Iterable` (bare, subscripted once or twice, with parentheses anywhere in the chain such as `(Iterable[int])[str]`), and of a method named `__aiter__` that resolves to `typing.AsyncIterable` or `collections.abc.AsyncIterable`, each under its own message. The finding covers the annotation expression only (without surrounding parentheses). A bare name unresolved where it is written still counts when the module's last binding of that name is an import of the target (Ruff's definitions pass looks it up in the final module scope). Not reported: module-level or nested-function `__iter__`, three subscripts, string annotations, unions and `Optional[...]`, `Annotated[...]` wrappers, `typing_extensions.Iterable`, shadowed or rebound names, an `Iterable` return on `__aiter__` or an `AsyncIterable` return on `__iter__`. A method named `__iter__` is checked whether `async`, decorated, a static or class method, overloaded or a stub. Applies to `.py` and `.pyi`. Divergences: a module-alias attribute (`typing.Iterable`) whose import is placed later in the file than a runtime-evaluated module- or class-level annotation is not seen, and the double subscript `Iterable[int][str]`, which tree-sitter-python rejects unless parenthesized, is not detected.

### `ruff-pyi046-unused-private-protocol`

Port of Ruff's PYI046 (unused-private-protocol): reports the name of a class whose name starts with an underscore (`_` alone included) and whose positional bases include typing.Protocol (also typing_extensions and _typeshed, through any import form or alias, optionally subscripted as Protocol[T] and optionally parenthesized), when that class is the live binding of its name in its scope and nothing in the file reads it. Annotations, string annotations, base classes, keyword arguments such as metaclass=, match class patterns, f-strings, PEP 695 type statements and bounds, calls and __all__ entries all count as reads, as in Ruff. A Protocol reached only through a keyword argument, a starred base or an alias variable (P = Protocol) does not match, as in Ruff. Like Ruff it checks module and function scopes in non-stub files and every scope, class bodies included, in .pyi stubs. Divergences come from the engine: a module whose __all__ is extended by a non-literal expression makes usedness unknown, so nothing is reported there while Ruff still reports; and a string in a functional TypedDict keyword field is not read as a reference. noqa comments and file-level noqa directives are honoured; Ruff's range suppression comments (disable and enable brackets) are not.

### `ruff-pyi047-unused-private-type-alias`

Port of Ruff's PYI047 (unused-private-type-alias): reports the name of an underscore-prefixed alias that is the live binding of its name in its scope and that nothing in the file reads. It matches an annotated assignment with a value whose annotation resolves to typing.TypeAlias (also typing_extensions and _typeshed, through any import form or alias, optionally parenthesized), and a PEP 695 `type _X = ...` statement, generic or not. A bare `_X: TypeAlias` without a value, a string annotation "TypeAlias", a locally shadowed TypeAlias name, an unannotated `_X = int` and a type parameter do not match, as in Ruff. Reads, including string annotations, string alias values and __all__ entries, count as in Ruff; Annotated metadata strings do not. Scopes follow Ruff: module and function scopes in non-stub files, every scope (class bodies too) in .pyi stubs. In stubs Ruff also reports an unused underscore-prefixed comprehension variable inside the value of a type alias whose target is a plain name (private or not), at the variable, because Ruff ties that binding to the alias statement; a separate clause reproduces this with its own message. Divergences come from the engine: a function that calls locals() makes its aliases count as used, and a module whose __all__ is extended by a non-literal expression makes usedness unknown, so nothing is reported there while Ruff still reports. noqa comments and file-level noqa directives are honoured; Ruff's range suppression comments (disable and enable brackets) are not.

### `ruff-pyi049-unused-private-typed-dict`

Port of Ruff's PYI049 (unused-private-typed-dict): reports the name of an underscore-prefixed binding that is the live binding of its name in its scope, that nothing in the file reads, and that is either a class with typing.TypedDict among its positional bases (also typing_extensions and _typeshed, through any import form or alias, optionally parenthesized; a subscripted TypedDict[...] base, a metaclass= keyword and a starred base do not count, as in Ruff) or a plain single-target assignment whose value is a call of TypedDict, parenthesized or not. An annotated assignment, a chained assignment, a tuple or list target, a walrus, an augmented assignment, a locally shadowed TypedDict and a bare reference to TypedDict do not match, as in Ruff. Reads, including string annotations, strings in a functional TypedDict's field dict and __all__ entries, count as in Ruff. Scopes follow Ruff: module and function scopes in non-stub files, every scope (class bodies too) in .pyi stubs. In stubs Ruff also reports an unused underscore-prefixed comprehension variable inside such a TypedDict call, or in the decorators, bases, keywords or type parameters of such a class, at the variable, because Ruff ties that binding to the defining statement; a separate clause reproduces this with its own message. Divergences come from the engine: a function that calls locals() makes a functional TypedDict assignment count as used, a module whose __all__ is extended by a non-literal expression makes usedness unknown, and a string in a functional TypedDict keyword field (`TypedDict("_B", x="_B")`) is not read as a reference. noqa comments and file-level noqa directives are honoured; Ruff's range suppression comments (disable and enable brackets) are not.

### `ruff-pyi051-redundant-literal-union`

Port of Ruff's PYI051 (redundant-literal-union): reports each value inside a `Literal[...]` that is a member of a union (`Union[...]`, `|`, nested unions; `Optional` is not traversed) together with the builtin type that already covers it: a str literal beside `str`, bytes beside `bytes`, int beside `int`, float beside `float`, complex beside `complex`, bool beside `bool`. Unions are checked anywhere, in annotations and in runtime expressions. Builtins are matched only when unshadowed. `Literal` and `Union` resolve through typing, typing_extensions and _typeshed imports and aliases. The literal value is the reported span. These are skipped: a value that is itself a tuple nested in the `Literal` (`Literal[("a", "b"), "c"]`), None, ellipsis and negated numbers. String type definitions (annotations, typing generic slices, cast/TypeVar arguments, TypeAlias values) are parsed and checked when they are simple strings. As in Ruff, implicitly concatenated or escaped string annotations are not checked. Applies to `.py` and `.pyi` files.

### `ruff-pyi053-string-or-bytes-too-long`

Port of Ruff's PYI053 (string-or-bytes-too-long): in `.pyi` files, reports a string, bytes or f-string literal longer than 50 characters, including an implicit concatenation summed across its parts. Counting: an escape counts once, a line continuation not at all, an f-string interpolation counts its source length, and t-strings are never reported. Exceptions: docstring-like expression statements; strings inside type annotations or other type definitions; a TypeAlias value; the argument of warnings.deprecated; module-scope `__all__` assignments (a comprehension or lambda inside one is not exempt). Type definitions here are subscripts of typing and builtin generics, the type arguments of cast, TypeForm, NewType, TypeVar, TypeAliasType, NamedTuple and TypedDict (including their type keywords), mypy_extensions argument constructors, a TypedDict class's `extra_items`, and the type part of Annotated, but not runtime Annotated metadata. The literal is the reported span. Divergences: octal escapes and `{{` in f-strings can be over-counted, unknown bytes escapes under-counted, and concatenations with triple-quoted or raw f-string parts are not summed.

### `ruff-pyi055-unnecessary-type-union`

Port of Ruff's PYI055 (unnecessary-type-union): reports a union (`X | Y`, `Union[X, Y]`, including nested and parenthesised unions and the PEP 695 `type X = ...` form) that has two or more `type[...]` members (builtin `type` only, subscripted with a single non-tuple, non-starred argument); the whole outermost union is reported, never a union nested inside another. In stub files every annotation slot, `type` statement and `if TYPE_CHECKING:` body is checked; in other files only annotations that are not evaluated at runtime (local variable annotations, any annotation under `from __future__ import annotations`, `type` statements, type parameters and `TYPE_CHECKING` blocks) plus string annotations. A locally rebound `type` is not reported. In a string annotation that is implicitly concatenated or contains an escape sequence, Ruff resolves names by start offset, which every node there shares, so `Union[...]` is not recognised as a union and only `|` unions are checked; the document mirrors that. Divergence: the contrived `global type` case described in the report.

### `ruff-pyi056-unsupported-method-call-on-all`

Port of Ruff's PYI056 (unsupported-method-call-on-all): reports a call to `.append()`, `.extend()` or `.remove()` on a bare name `__all__` (parentheses, whitespace and comments allowed), at the attribute expression `__all__.append`, including calls written inside string annotations; other methods, attribute access chains like `self.__all__` and subscripted `__all__[0]` are not reported, and the check does not depend on scope or on the file being a stub. Where the tree-sitter grammar folds a leading `*` into the call (a starred element of a list, set or tuple display, or a later starred call argument, as either `*__all__.append` or a list_splat callee), the reported range still starts at the attribute, skipping the star and any comment after it. No known divergence.

### `ruff-pyi057-byte-string-usage`

Port of Ruff's PYI057 (byte-string-usage): reports `ByteString` from `typing` or `collections.abc`, both where it is imported (`from typing import ByteString`, aliased, relative or line-continued forms, at the imported name or alias) and where it is used through the module (`typing.ByteString`, `collections.abc.ByteString`, aliased modules, in any expression context including assignment and `del` targets, and inside string annotations), at the attribute expression. In match patterns (class, value, union, mapping-key and keyword patterns) the dotted name is reported up to `ByteString`, so `case typing.ByteString.x:` reports `typing.ByteString`. A bare use of an imported `ByteString` name is not reported separately (Ruff reports only the import). No known divergence.

### `ruff-pyi058-generator-return-from-iter-method`

Port of Ruff's PYI058 (generator-return-from-iter-method): reports the name of an `__iter__` method annotated as returning `Generator` (or an `__aiter__` method annotated as returning `AsyncGenerator`) when the method is a plain (not `async def`) method directly in a class body, taking exactly one parameter (positional-only or regular, defaults and annotations allowed, no `*args`, `**kwargs` or keyword-only marker). `Generator`/`AsyncGenerator` resolve through `typing`, `typing_extensions` and `collections.abc` imports (aliases, module attributes, parentheses); a string annotation is never resolved, as in Ruff. A subscript must have exactly three (two for async) type arguments, plain or as a parenthesized tuple, and every argument after the first must be `None` or `Any` (typing, typing_extensions, _typeshed). In `.py` files every body statement must be `pass`, a single plain string or `...` expression (no f-strings, bytes or bare tuples), a `yield`/`yield from` expression statement, or a return whose value is `None` once a yield has been seen; stubs skip the body check. Divergence: Ruff's fix and its applicability are not modelled; in a file with `from __future__ import annotations` or a stub, the port resolves the return annotation after the module, so it also sees a module-level import written after the class, where Ruff (which resolves at the `def`) does not.

### `ruff-pyi059-generic-not-last-base-class`

Port of Ruff's PYI059 (generic-not-last-base-class): reports the whole base-class argument list of a `class` statement when `Generic` or `Generic[...]` (resolved through `typing`, `typing_extensions` or `_typeshed` imports, aliases, module attributes and parentheses) appears as a positional base and is followed by another positional base (names, calls, subscripts and starred bases all count; keyword arguments and `**kwargs` do not). A `Generic` wrapped in a call, conditional, walrus or star is not recognized, as in Ruff. Divergence: Ruff's fix and its safety classification are not modelled; in a stub the port resolves class bases after the module, so it also sees a module-level import of `Generic` written after the class, where Ruff does not.

### `ruff-pyi061-redundant-none-literal`

Port of Ruff's PYI061 (redundant-none-literal): reports each `None` member of a `Literal[...]` subscript (`Literal` resolved through `typing`, `typing_extensions` or `_typeshed` imports, aliases and module attributes) wherever the subscript appears, annotation or runtime expression, including members of nested `Literal`s and of a parenthesized tuple slice, and inside string annotations nested to any depth; like Ruff it stays silent when the file imports nothing from typing. Members of a tuple member or of a non-Literal subscript member are not traversed. The finding spans the `None` member (the whole string for a string annotation Ruff cannot map). A `Literal` holding only `None` and one with other members get different advice (plain `None` versus `Literal[...] | None` / `Optional[Literal[...]]`). Divergence: Ruff's fix and its target-version dependent wording are not modelled; the port names both union spellings in one message.

### `ruff-pyi062-duplicate-literal-member`

Port of Ruff's PYI062 (duplicate-literal-member): reports each repeated member of a `Literal[...]` subscript (`Literal` resolved through `typing`, `typing_extensions` or `_typeshed` imports, aliases and module attributes) after its first occurrence, in annotations and runtime expressions alike, after flattening nested `Literal`s and parenthesized tuple slices, including inside string annotations nested to any depth; members are compared as expressions by value (`"a" "b"` equals `"ab"`, `0x1` equals `1`, `r"\n"` equals `"\\n"`), so values of different type (`1`, `True`, `1.0`) or str versus bytes are not duplicates, and members of a non-Literal subscript member are not compared. The finding spans the repeated member. Divergence: Ruff's fix is not modelled.

### `ruff-pyi063-pep484-style-positional-only-parameter`

Port of Ruff's PYI063 (pep484-style-positional-only-parameter): reports the name of the first parameter of a `def` (the second one for a method) when the name starts with a double underscore and does not end with one (after Python's NFKC identifier normalisation, so fullwidth and other compatibility low-line characters count as underscores), the signature has no `/`, and the parameter comes before any `*`, `*args` or keyword-only marker. The finding spans the parameter name only, not its annotation or default. Which leading parameter is skipped follows Ruff's function classification: methods and class methods skip the first parameter (including undecorated `__init_subclass__` and `__class_getitem__`), while static methods (decorated with the builtin `staticmethod` or `abc.abstractstaticmethod`, called or parenthesized forms included) and an undecorated `__new__` skip nothing; a `def` nested in a function inside a class is a plain function; decorator names resolve through imports and shadowing. Applies to both `.py` and `.pyi` files, async defs included, lambdas are not checked. Divergences: Ruff stays silent when `target-version` is below 3.8 and honours the pep8-naming `classmethod-decorators` and `staticmethod-decorators` settings; the port always fires and recognises only the builtin and `abc` decorators.

### `ruff-pyi064-redundant-final-literal`

Port of Ruff's PYI064 (redundant-final-literal): reports an annotated assignment (any target, any scope, `.py` or `.pyi`, with or without a value) whose annotation is `Final[Literal[x]]`, with `Final` and `Literal` resolved from `typing`, `typing_extensions` or `_typeshed` (bare, dotted or aliased imports; a shadowed or foreign `Final`/`Literal` does not match). `x` must be a single string, bytes, number (int, float, complex; not negative), `True`/`False`, `None` or `...` literal; implicit concatenation of plain strings and parentheses are accepted, while f-strings, tuples, trailing commas, negative numbers, expressions and nested subscripts are not. Quoted annotations and other wrappers (`ClassVar[Final[...]]`, `Annotated[...]`, `list[...]`) are not reported, as in Ruff. The finding spans the whole statement from the target to the value. Three clauses give different advice according to Ruff's fix availability: the value is equal to the literal as Ruff's ComparableExpr sees it (`compare` with `text: structure`: escapes, prefixes, quoting, implicit concatenation, integer bases, digit separators, big integers and parentheses are normalised), there is no value, or the value differs from the literal (including a stub's `...` placeholder). Divergences: the `typing-modules` setting is not honoured and the message omits the literal snippet Ruff prints (truncated); Ruff's seen-typing precondition is implied by the import resolution.

### `ruff-rse102-unnecessary-paren-on-raise-exception`

Port of Ruff's RSE102 (unnecessary-paren-on-raise-exception): reports the empty argument list `()` of a call that is directly the exception of a `raise` statement, also through redundant parentheses and line continuations after `raise`; the cause after `from` is never checked. Comments inside the parentheses still count as empty; a call with any positional, keyword, starred or generator argument is skipped. The callee is judged as Ruff's lookup_attribute judges it. A name is read as Ruff's lookup_symbol reads it: in a function that declares it `global` or `nonlocal` the declaration is the binding (so the naming heuristic decides), otherwise the latest binding the read sees. A name that resolves to an unshadowed builtin or to a class definition is reported, one bound to a function definition (decorated, async and conditional ones included) is skipped. For an attribute chain `X.a.b.m` whose head resolves to a class, each segment is looked up as the latest binding in the previous class's body, through any depth and through parentheses such as `(A.B).m`: a def as the last member is skipped, a nested class is reported, anything else (an assignment, an import, a deleted or missing member, a non-class in the middle) falls to the naming heuristic. The heuristic skips a callee whose last identifier, after one optional leading underscore, starts with a Unicode lowercase letter, and reports it otherwise; a callee that is not a name or attribute (a call, subscript, lambda and so on) is reported, and ctypes.WinError through any import form is skipped. The report spans exactly the parentheses, and the file's noqa comments suppress it. No fix is attached: Ruff offers its fix only as unsafe when the callee may be a function, so the message says to keep the parentheses if the callee returns the exception. Untested divergence: a `nonlocal` declaration of the head of an attribute chain is not modelled.

### `ruff-ruf001-ambiguous-unicode-character-string`

Port of Ruff's RUF001 (ambiguous-unicode-character-string): reports each character of Ruff's confusables table that imitates an ASCII character, with Ruff's word rule: a word (maximal run of alphanumeric characters) has its look-alikes reported when it holds an ASCII character or when every character in it is a look-alike, and a look-alike that is not alphanumeric (punctuation, space) is always reported. Docstrings are excluded (RUF002; a docstring is the first statement of a module, class or function that is a plain, possibly parenthesised or concatenated string, not a tuple); string prefixes r, u, f and t are accepted, bytes strings are skipped, and f/t-string literal parts are checked while interpolations and format specs are not. A parameter, return or variable annotation that is a plain string is not checked in a stub, after `from __future__ import annotations`, or when the target Python is 3.14 or later, because Ruff defers it as a string type definition. Divergences: in text that also holds an unambiguous non-ASCII letter or digit, a multi-letter word made only of look-alikes is not reported, and in a word with an ASCII character only its first look-alike and the first look-alike after each ASCII character are reported, because a regex capture is reported once per non-overlapping match; a one-letter look-alike word right after an underscore, combining mark or joiner that ends another one-letter look-alike word is missed, and one right after a non-letter numeric such as `²` is reported. In a stub, strings Ruff reads as other type definitions (a `TypeAlias` value, the type arguments of `cast`, `TypeVar` and `NewType`, subscripts of typing special forms) are still checked.

### `ruff-ruf002-ambiguous-unicode-character-docstring`

Port of Ruff's RUF002 (ambiguous-unicode-character-docstring): reports each character of Ruff's confusables table that imitates an ASCII character, with Ruff's word rule: a word (maximal run of alphanumeric characters) has its look-alikes reported when it holds an ASCII character or when every character in it is a look-alike, and a look-alike that is not alphanumeric (punctuation, space) is always reported. Only docstrings are checked: the first statement of a module, class or function when it is a plain string (prefixes r and u), possibly parenthesised or implicitly concatenated; a tuple such as `"doc",`, an f-string, a bytes string or a later string is not a docstring (RUF001 covers those). Divergences: in text that also holds an unambiguous non-ASCII letter or digit, a multi-letter word made only of look-alikes is not reported, and in a word with an ASCII character only its first look-alike and the first look-alike after each ASCII character are reported, because a regex capture is reported once per non-overlapping match; a one-letter look-alike word right after an underscore, combining mark or joiner that ends another one-letter look-alike word is missed, and one right after a non-letter numeric such as `²` is reported.

### `ruff-ruf003-ambiguous-unicode-character-comment`

Port of Ruff's RUF003 (ambiguous-unicode-character-comment): reports each character of Ruff's confusables table that imitates an ASCII character, with Ruff's word rule: a word (maximal run of alphanumeric characters) has its look-alikes reported when it holds an ASCII character or when every character in it is a look-alike, and a look-alike that is not alphanumeric (punctuation, space) is always reported. Every comment is checked, from its `#` to the end of the line, including comments inside f-string replacement fields; string contents are never checked. Divergences: in text that also holds an unambiguous non-ASCII letter or digit, a multi-letter word made only of look-alikes is not reported, and in a word with an ASCII character only its first look-alike and the first look-alike after each ASCII character are reported, because a regex capture is reported once per non-overlapping match; a one-letter look-alike word right after an underscore, combining mark or joiner that ends another one-letter look-alike word is missed, and one right after a non-letter numeric such as `²` is reported.

### `ruff-ruf005-collection-literal-concatenation`

Port of Ruff's RUF005 (collection-literal-concatenation): reports the outermost `+` expression (one that is not an operand of another `+`, parentheses ignored) that Ruff can rewrite as a single list or tuple literal with unpacking. A `+` reduces when one side is a list or tuple literal, or a nested `+` that itself reduces, and the other side is a name, attribute, call, slice subscript (not `x[0]` or `x[1:, 2]`), or a literal or reduced `+` of the same kind (list with list, tuple with tuple). The arm order matches Ruff: a left list or tuple decides the kind, so `(1,) + [2]` and `([1, 2, 3] + b) + (4, 5, 6)` are not reported, and an inner `+` that does not reduce (`a + b + [1]`) keeps the whole chain silent, while `[1] + a + b` is reported. A right operand such as `[1] + (a + [2])` is reduced through its parentheses. Comments only disable Ruff's fix, so commented expressions are still reported. The span is the whole `+` expression without enclosing parentheses. The outermost `+` is found from its parent (a node that is neither a `+` nor parentheses), so the rule still runs around the error regions of a file with syntax errors. Strings Ruff reads as type definitions (`def f(a: "foo + [1]")`, `List["[1] + foo"]`, `cast("(1,) + foo", x)`, TypeVar bounds) are parsed as annotations and checked the same way, one finding per reducible `+` inside them, except in files with syntax errors; operands are told apart by their position around the `+` token so the same logic runs inside the parsed strings.

### `ruff-ruf006-asyncio-dangling-task`

Port of Ruff's RUF006 (asyncio-dangling-task): three clauses. The first reports a call used as an expression statement (parentheses ignored, not a tuple statement, so `await asyncio.create_task(x)` and `x = ...` are not reported) to `asyncio.create_task` or `asyncio.ensure_future` resolved through imports and aliases, or to `.create_task` on a name whose binding is an assignment from `asyncio.get_event_loop`, `asyncio.get_running_loop` or `asyncio.new_event_loop` (read through the binding's assignment, annotated, parenthesized and chained forms). The second reports the same call when it is the single value of an assignment (annotated and parenthesized targets and tuple, list or starred unpacking included, once per name target like Ruff; names inside attribute or subscript targets such as `x.b, c = ...` are not targets; `a = b = call()`, attribute and subscript targets, walrus and `for`, `with` and `except` targets are not reported) and the live binding of that name in the same class, function or module scope is an unused, non-global, non-nonlocal assignment-like binding, which reproduces Ruff's shadowed-binding chain: `if x: t = create_task() else: t = None` is reported but the same followed by `await t` is not. The third reproduces Ruff's check of comprehension scopes: an unused comprehension variable inside such an assignment (`t = asyncio.create_task(gather(*[f() for _ in xs]))`) makes Ruff report the call once more, even when `t` is awaited. The span is the call. Divergences: usage is unknown in a scope that calls `locals()` and, at module level, when `__all__` is not a literal list, so Ruff's reports there are missed; Ruff's end-of-module name lookup for a callee used above its import, and a walrus inside a loop getter's arguments, are not reproduced (both only in code that fails at run time).

### `ruff-ruf007-zip-instead-of-pairwise`

Port of Ruff's RUF007 (zip-instead-of-pairwise): reports the `zip` callee (the builtin `zip` or `builtins.zip` through imports and aliases, not a shadowed name, parentheses ignored; only when the target Python is 3.10 or newer) of a call with exactly two positional arguments (keyword arguments and `**kwargs` allowed, any `*args` excluded), where the first is a name or a subscript slice of a name and the second is a subscript slice of the same name. Slice steps must be absent or the integer literal 1, upper bounds are ignored as in Ruff, and each start is absent (0) or an integer literal (hex, octal, binary and underscores are normalized by value, non-integers and negatives such as `-1` do not qualify); the second start must be exactly the first start plus one, a name counting as 0, and fit in a 32-bit integer. A subscript with a comma such as `x[1:, 2]` is not a slice. Without arithmetic, the successor test is done on the decimal text: the first start with its trailing 9s removed and its last digit incremented must equal the second start with its trailing 0s removed, and the count of those 9s must equal the count of those 0s, so carries such as `x[9:]` with `x[10:]` and `x[1099:]` with `x[1100:]` are reported. The reported span is the callee only.

### `ruff-ruf010-explicit-f-string-type-conversion`

Port of Ruff's RUF010 (explicit-f-string-type-conversion): reports a call of the builtin str, repr or ascii (unshadowed, or builtins.str through any import form, optionally parenthesized) that is the whole expression of a replacement field of an f-string, anchored on the call without its parentheses. The field must have no conversion flag and no debug `=`, and the call must have exactly one argument: for str that argument positional or object=, for repr and ascii positional; a sole generator expression argument counts. Starred and keyword-splat arguments, extra arguments and other keywords are not reported. As in Ruff, a repr/ascii call with a sole starred argument, or a matching call whose field has debug text, ends the check for the rest of that f-string literal (so later fields in the same literal are not reported), while implicitly concatenated f-string pieces are judged one by one. Calls inside a format spec, plain strings, byte strings and template strings (t-strings) are not reported. The three clauses give str, repr and ascii their own replacement flag.

### `ruff-ruf015-unnecessary-iterable-allocation-for-first-element`

Port of Ruff's RUF015 (unnecessary-iterable-allocation-for-first-element): reports list(x)[0], tuple(x)[0], [comprehension][0], a single-starred list [*x][0], and the same with .pop(0), on the whole subscript or call, where list and tuple are the unshadowed builtins (or builtins.list and builtins.tuple), the inner call has exactly one positional argument (a starred one included; keyword arguments are ignored, as in Ruff), .pop takes exactly the one positional argument and no keywords, and the index is the int literal zero in any spelling (0x0, 0_0, 0b0, parenthesized allowed). A comprehension of the form [i for i in x] counts as a copy of x; with a filter, a second generator, an async generator or a differing target it is a lazy-generator case. Two clauses give different advice: iterating a generator or comprehension with next(...) versus next(iter(x)) for a plain copy. String annotations are read through the annotation grammar. Divergence: [*list(x)[0]] with one element (tree-sitter grammar quirk) misses the inner finding.

### `ruff-ruf016-invalid-index-type`

Port of Ruff's RUF016 (invalid-index-type): reports an index that is a string or t-string, bytes, float, complex, ellipsis, None, starred expression, or a list/set/dict/tuple/comprehension/generator/lambda, when subscripting a list, list comprehension, tuple, or plain (non-t-string) string/bytes literal, looking through parentheses. Each slice bound or step that is not an integer, None or a boolean literal is reported likewise. A tuple index (`x[1, 2]`) is reported at the tuple's span, from its first element through the last element or trailing comma, skipping leading comments and reading strings, comments and up to three levels of brackets. Subscripts inside string (forward-reference) annotations are parsed and checked too, at Ruff's ranges. Names and calls are not inferred. Divergence: a tuple index inside a string annotation is not reported.

### `ruff-ruf017-quadratic-list-summation`

Port of Ruff's RUF017 (quadratic-list-summation): reports `sum(iterable, [])`, `sum(iterable, start=[])`, `sum(iterable, list())` and `sum(iterable, start=list())` at the call's span. `sum` and `list` must resolve to the builtins, through shadowing and `builtins` imports, and parentheses, comments and line continuations are looked through. There is no report when no positional argument precedes the start, when a starred argument comes before the second positional, or when the start is not an empty list. Calls inside string (forward-reference) annotations are parsed and checked too, as Ruff does (not inside `Literal[...]`).

### `ruff-ruf018-assignment-in-assert`

Port of Ruff's RUF018 (assignment-in-assert): reports a walrus inside an `assert` (test or message) when some read outside an assert counts as a use of its binding. Ruff counts a later read that resolves to the walrus. It also counts reads that come before the walrus in the same scope, because Ruff copies the reads of the binding a new binding shadows. These include reads from comprehensions, class bodies and default arguments evaluated in that scope, reads of a bare annotation, and reads of a `global` binding. The copy chain is broken by an unconditional `del` and by the end of an `except ... as name` handler. It also counts a `nonlocal` declaration in a nested function, when the walrus is the function's last binding of the name, and an `__all__` entry, when the walrus is the module's live binding. Divergence: reads inside string annotations are not seen.

### `ruff-ruf019-unnecessary-key-check`

Port of Ruff's RUF019 (unnecessary-key-check): an `and` of exactly two operands, `key in dct` (one `in` comparison, parentheses allowed) then `dct[key]` (one subscript), whose key and dictionary expressions are structurally equal as Ruff's ComparableExpr reads them (parentheses, quote style, string escapes and number spelling aside), reported on the whole `and` expression. Only where Ruff's checker is in a boolean test: the test of `if`, `elif`, `while`, `assert`, a conditional expression, a comprehension `if` or a `match` guard, the operand of `not` (anywhere, lambdas and decorators included), or the first positional argument of builtin `bool()` (also `builtins.bool`, parenthesized, or after comments), reaching through parentheses and nested `and`/`or` operands; not when the `and` is the unparenthesized left side of a longer `and` chain (Ruff sees three operands). Skipped when the key or dictionary expression has a side effect as Ruff's side_effect reads it: a call other than an empty builtin `list()`, `tuple()`, `set()`, `dict()` or `frozenset()` (not shadowed; a parenthesized callee or comments in the empty argument list still count as empty), a walrus, `await`, `yield`, a subscript, a comprehension or generator (also inside a lambda body), or a binary operator unless both operands are literals, f-strings or displays (a t-string, unary or nested operator operand counts as a side effect); an f-string interpolation is still reported. A tuple key written `(x, y) in d and d[x, y]` (or `d[(x, y)]`, extra parentheses around the tuple, a trailing comma, a one-element `(x,)` with `d[x,]`, or `(*x,)` with `d[*x]`) is matched up to four elements. Also read inside strings Ruff parses as type definitions (string annotations, `cast` arguments), reported on the `and` inside the string. Divergence: tuple keys of five or more elements are missed. Ruff panics on a few string annotations (an implicitly concatenated string, a lambda), so those are unprobed. Ruff's `# noqa` is honoured.

### `ruff-ruf020-never-union`

Port of Ruff's RUF020 (never-union): an operand of `|` (through parentheses, including the annotation-only grouping tree-sitter builds for `list[int] | Never`), or a member of a `Union[...]` with at least two members, that resolves through an import to `Never` or `NoReturn` of `typing`, `typing_extensions` or `_typeshed`; one finding per such operand or member, reported on that operand or member (parentheses excluded), so `Never | Never` and `Union[Never, NoReturn]` give two. `Union` resolves to those same three modules, in expression position and in annotation position, and also in the tuple spelling `Union[(Never, int)]`; `Union[Never]`, `Union[Never,]` and `Union[(Never, int),]` (a one-member union whose member is a tuple) are not reported. Aliased, from-imported and attribute forms (`import typing as t`, `from typing import Never as N`) resolve as in Ruff, and a rebound, shadowed, relative-imported or star-imported name does not. No annotation context is needed: a runtime `Never | int` is reported too, but `|=` is not. Also read inside simple strings Ruff parses as type definitions (string annotations, `cast`/`TypeVar` arguments, `List["..."]`), reported inside the string. Divergence: implicitly concatenated strings and strings with escapes are not read, because Ruff's result there comes from name-range collisions (`a: "int | Never" ""` reports twice at the whole string, `a: "Never | int" ""` not at all). Ruff's `# noqa` is honoured.

### `ruff-ruf021-parenthesize-chained-operators`

Port of Ruff's RUF021 (parenthesize-chained-operators): an `and` expression that is a direct, unparenthesized operand of an `or` expression, reported on the `and` expression (outer parentheses excluded), one finding per such operand; so `a or b and c`, `a and b or c`, `a or not b and c` and `a or b and c and d or e` are reported, while parenthesized operands, a lone `and` chain, `or` inside `and` and `not` operands are not. Applies in every expression position, including f-string fields, and also inside strings Ruff parses as type definitions (string annotations, `cast` arguments), reported on the `and` inside the string. Divergence: an implicitly concatenated string annotation whose joined text holds two such `and` operands (`x: "a or b and c" "d or e and f"`) yields two findings at the same whole-string span in Ruff and one here, because the engine collapses identical spans. Ruff's `# noqa` and file-level `# ruff: noqa` are honoured.

### `ruff-ruf022-unsorted-dunder-all`

Port of Ruff's RUF022 (unsorted-dunder-all): reports a list, a parenthesized tuple or an unparenthesized tuple whose elements are all plain string literals (implicit concatenation and redundant parentheses allowed; no f-strings, bytes, starred or other items) when it defines or extends the module's `__all__` and some element sorts before the one preceding it. The order is Ruff's isort-style one: names of at least two bytes with an uppercase letter and no lowercase letter first, then names starting with an uppercase letter, then everything else. Each group is in natural order as Ruff's natord computes it: digit runs compared by number, a run that begins with 0 compared digit by digit with the shorter run first, white space ignored (white space between two digits ends the run), the underscore ranked between capital and small letters, and code point order for characters above U+FFFF. Recognised forms: `__all__ = ...` and `__all__: T = ...` with a single target (`(__all__) = ...` too), `__all__ += ...`, and `__all__.extend(<one display>)` with no other argument. They count only where Ruff's current scope is the module: not in function or lambda bodies, class definitions, function parameter and return annotations, function type parameters or `type` alias statements, and in a comprehension or generator only inside its first iterable (function defaults and decorators count as module scope). Sets, dicts and calls are not sorted, as in Ruff. The span is the whole display, brackets included. Divergence: ASCII punctuation other than the underscore is ranked by natural-compare's table rather than code point order, an underscore facing a capital Z followed by a non-ASCII character ranks too low, and zero-led digit runs longer than eleven digits are only approximated.

### `ruff-ruf023-unsorted-dunder-slots`

Port of Ruff's RUF023 (unsorted-dunder-slots): reports a list, tuple (parenthesized or not), set or dict display assigned to `__slots__` directly in a class body (also under if, try, for, while, with and match inside the class, and with an annotation). It fires when all the elements, or all the dict keys, are plain string literals and some element sorts before the one preceding it. The order is natural order as Ruff's natord computes it: digit runs compared by number, a run that begins with 0 compared digit by digit with the shorter run first, white space ignored (white space between two digits ends the run), the underscore ranked between capital and small letters, and code point order for characters above U+FFFF. A dict with a `**` splat or a non-string key is left alone, as are augmented assignments, chained or tuple targets, and `__slots__` assigned in a method or at module level. `(__slots__) = ...` is read as the name. The span is the whole display. Divergence: ASCII punctuation other than the underscore is ranked by natural-compare's table rather than code point order, an underscore facing a capital Z followed by a non-ASCII character ranks too low, and zero-led digit runs longer than eleven digits are only approximated.

### `ruff-ruf024-mutable-fromkeys-value`

Port of Ruff's RUF024 (mutable-fromkeys-value): reports a call to `dict.fromkeys` (the builtin `dict` through any alias, `builtins.dict`, or parentheses; not a shadowed or rebound `dict`) that has exactly two positional arguments. The second argument must be a list, dict or set display, a list, dict or set comprehension, or a call to `dict`, `list`, `set`, `collections.Counter`, `OrderedDict`, `defaultdict` or `deque`. The callee may come through imports and aliases and may be subscripted once, as in `list[int]()`; a callee subscripted twice, such as `list[int][str]()`, is not resolved, as in Ruff. Keyword arguments after the two positional ones are ignored, as in Ruff, and tuples, strings, names and generator expressions are not mutable here. The span is the whole call. Divergence: Ruff also lints expressions inside string annotations, which the engine does not parse, so `def f(x: "dict.fromkeys(['a'], [])"): ...` is reported by Ruff only.

### `ruff-ruf026-default-factory-kwarg`

Port of Ruff's RUF026 (default-factory-kwarg): reports a call whose callee resolves to `collections.defaultdict` through imports (plain, aliased, from-import, attribute access, parenthesised callee), that has no positional arguments and a `default_factory=` keyword whose value is not definitely non-callable (numbers, strings and f-strings, None, booleans, ellipsis, list, dict, set, tuple, comprehensions and generators are skipped, as in Ruff). The whole call is reported. Other keywords and `**` unpacking do not matter; any positional argument, including a starred one, suppresses it. Calls inside a simple string annotation (one string with no escapes, such as `x: "defaultdict(default_factory=int)"`) are parsed and reported at their position inside the string, as Ruff does; implicitly concatenated or escaped annotation strings are skipped, matching Ruff, whose name resolution there sees every name at the string's start. No fix is offered.

### `ruff-ruf028-invalid-formatter-suppression-comment`

Port of Ruff's RUF028 (invalid-formatter-suppression-comment): reports `# fmt: off`, `# fmt: on`, `# fmt: skip`, `# yapf: disable` and `# yapf: enable` comments (whitespace limited to space, tab and form feed, as Ruff trims it) that the formatter ignores. Reasons expressed: the comment sits where Ruff's comment visitor makes an expression, pattern, argument list, parameter list, alias or with item the enclosing node (inside brackets and calls, after an expression inside parentheses, after an imported name or with item); a comment right after the opening parenthesis of an expression that is a direct child of a statement, clause, case guard, decorator or return or variable annotation is accepted, as Ruff's tree has no parentheses node. A comment trailing a statement that holds another suppression comment is attached to the statement's last expression unless that expression is parenthesised (end of line), and an own-line comment in an indented block after such a statement (descending through compound statements) is attached to it when that expression starts mid-line, so both are reported. Also: skip on its own line; on at the end of a line; off or on after a decorator; off directly above the first elif, else, except or finally, or at the start of an else or finally body that follows except clauses. Divergences: Ruff compares comment columns, which the DSL cannot, so over-indented comments after decorators or statements and else-body comments at the else column can differ (see the status reason).

### `ruff-ruf030-assert-with-print-message`

Port of Ruff's RUF030 (assert-with-print-message): reports a call to the builtin `print` (resolved so a shadowed or reassigned `print` is not reported, `builtins.print` is) used as the message of an `assert` statement, including a parenthesised callee or message, comments and line continuations before the message, and asserts in any nesting. The call is reported, not the assert. Only the message position counts: a print call in the test expression or nested deeper in the message is ignored. No fix is offered.

### `ruff-ruf032-decimal-from-float-literal`

Port of Ruff's RUF032 (decimal-from-float-literal): a call whose function resolves to `decimal.Decimal` (through `import decimal`, `from decimal import Decimal`, aliases, a parenthesized callee or object, NFKC-equivalent spellings; a locally rebound or shadowed name does not count, and function bodies see the module's latest binding as in Ruff) and whose first positional argument is a float literal, optionally preceded by any chain of unary `+` and `-` (`-+--4.0`, `-(1.0)`). Reported on that argument only: the sign chain and the number, without enclosing parentheses. A second clause finds the same calls inside strings Ruff reads as type definitions (`x: "Decimal(1.5)"`, `cast("Decimal(1.5)", v)`, nested quoted annotations), anchored inside the string as Ruff does; strings in `Literal[...]`, `Annotated` metadata and ordinary strings are not read. A first argument that is an integer, a complex literal (`1j`, `1.0j`), a string, a name, `~4.0`, a starred expression or an expression such as `1.0 + 1` is not reported, and neither is a float passed by keyword (`Decimal(value=1.0)`) or as a later positional argument. Known divergence (engine): Ruff's annotation cache makes it skip all but the first nested string inside an escaped or implicitly concatenated string annotation; this port reads each.

### `ruff-ruf033-post-init-default`

Port of Ruff's RUF033 (post-init-default): a default value on any non-variadic parameter (positional-only, positional, keyword-only, `self` included) of a method named `__post_init__` (def or async def, decorated or not) that sits directly in a class body (also inside if/try/for/with/while/match blocks of that body, but not inside a nested function or class) of a class whose first recognized decorator is the standard-library `dataclasses.dataclass`, bare, called, aliased, attribute-accessed or parenthesized. As in Ruff, the decorator is resolved where the class is defined: an import that comes later at module level does not count, while a class inside a function sees the module's final binding. A class where an attrs decorator (`attrs.define/frozen/mutable`, `attr.define/frozen/mutable/s/attributes/attrs`) comes before the dataclass decorator is not reported, and neither are plain classes, other `dataclass` functions, `@dataclass()()`, or a `dataclass` name that is shadowed or not imported from `dataclasses`.
