Reported once per default, on the default expression without enclosing parentheses (a parenthesized tuple or generator keeps its own span, as in Ruff). Defaults of lambdas inside a default are not reported. Known divergence (engine): a method name spelled with NFKC-equivalent characters (`__ｐost_init__`) is reported by Ruff, which normalizes identifiers, but not here, because the name is matched by regex on the source text.

### `ruff-ruf034-useless-if-else`

Port of Ruff's RUF034 (useless-if-else): a conditional expression `body if test else orelse` whose body and orelse are structurally equal under Ruff's ComparableExpr (`compare` with `structure`: parentheses, comments, whitespace and quote style ignored; literals compared by type and value, so `1` equals `0x1` but not `1.0` or `True`, `'a'` equals `"a"`, an implicit concatenation equals the joined string; `a and b and c` differs from `(a and b) and c`, as Ruff flattens only the unparenthesized chain). Reported on the whole conditional expression, without enclosing parentheses. Only the conditional whose own two branches are equal is reported: `a if b else a if c else a` reports the inner one. Conditionals inside f-string replacement fields are found, and a second clause finds them inside strings Ruff reads as type definitions (`x: "a if b else a"`, `cast(...)`, `TypeVar` bounds, type alias values, nested quoted annotations such as `"list['a if b else a']"`), reported at the span inside the string as Ruff does; `Literal[...]` strings, `Annotated` metadata and ordinary strings are not read. Known divergences: a file with an unparenthesized starred operand starting with `(` or `[` (for example `w = 1, *(a if b else a)`) does not parse in the grammar, so a finding inside the affected statement is missed; and inside an escaped or concatenated string annotation holding several nested quoted strings, Ruff reads only the first nested string while this port reads each.

### `ruff-ruf036-none-not-at-end-of-union`

Port of Ruff's RUF036 (none-not-at-end-of-union): reports a union that contains `None` but does not end with it, written as `X | Y` or `Union[...]` (typing, typing_extensions and _typeshed, through aliases, from-imports and attribute access), anchored on the whole outermost union; unions nested in another union and unions outside type expressions (for example `isinstance(x, None | int)`) are not reported. It reproduces Ruff's notion of a type expression: annotations, quoted annotations (nested string forms included), PEP 695 aliases, bounds and defaults, the slots of runtime generics (`list[...]`, `Dict[...]`, `Optional[...]`, `Callable[...]`, ...) except inside a subscript that is an assignment, `for`, `with` or `del` target, `Annotated` (first slot only), `TypeAlias` values, the value slots of `TypedDict[{...}]`, the type arguments of `cast` (first or `typ=`), `TypeForm` (first), `NewType` (second or `tp=`), `TypeVar` (constraints, `bound=`, `default=`), `TypeAliasType` (second, `value=`, `type_params=`), `mypy_extensions` argument constructors (first positional, else `type=`), `NamedTuple` field types, keyword fields and `**{...}` values, and `TypedDict` field types, `extra_items`, `**` arguments and legacy keyword fields (when the call has only a name, or a name and `None`, as for Ruff's default target version before Python 3.13); a starred argument counts as positional. Divergence: syntax-error recovery is not reproduced (the engine finds nothing in a file it cannot fully parse), a TypedDict class is recognised for `extra_items` only when `TypedDict` is a direct base, and a parenthesized `(None)` second argument does not enable legacy TypedDict keyword fields.

### `ruff-ruf037-unnecessary-empty-iterable-within-deque-call`

Port of Ruff's RUF037 (unnecessary-empty-iterable-within-deque-call): reports a `collections.deque(...)` call (resolved through imports, aliases, attribute access and parentheses, respecting shadowing) with at most two arguments whose iterable, the `iterable=` keyword or else the first positional argument before any starred one, is an empty list, tuple or dict, an empty string, bytes or template string (implicit concatenations included), an f-string whose parts are empty and whose replacement fields hold only empty strings or f-strings without conversion, format spec or `=` (a bytes or template string inside a replacement field is not empty), or a call of builtin `list`, `tuple`, `set` or `dict` with no arguments. With a `maxlen` (by keyword or second positional) the message tells to pass it by keyword; without, to drop the argument. Calls inside quoted annotations are reported too. The report spans the whole call.

### `ruff-ruf040-invalid-assert-message-literal-argument`

Port of Ruff's RUF040 (invalid-assert-message-literal-argument): reports the message of an `assert` statement when it is an integer, float or imaginary number, boolean, `None`, ellipsis or bytes literal (including implicit concatenations of bytes), also when wrapped in parentheses. Strings, f-strings, names, unary and binary expressions, containers and a parenthesized tuple condition are not reported. The report spans the literal (the innermost expression, without its parentheses).

### `ruff-ruf041-unnecessary-nested-literal`

Port of Ruff's RUF041 (unnecessary-nested-literal): reports a `Literal[...]` (the subscript form, or the generic form an annotation parses to) whose head resolves, through the file's imports and shadowing, to `typing.Literal`, `typing_extensions.Literal` or `_typeshed.Literal` (parentheses around the head are transparent), when one of its own members is, directly, another such `Literal[...]`. A member counts through parentheses, and when the whole slice is a single parenthesized tuple (no comma beside it in the brackets) that tuple's elements count as members. A tuple that is one of several slice elements, or is followed by a trailing comma, is not unpacked: a `Literal` inside it is not a member of the outer one and is judged on its own, so `Literal[(1, Literal[2, Literal[3]]), 4]` reports the inner `Literal[2, Literal[3]]`, as Ruff does. A `Literal` buried in another expression (a call argument, a `Union[...]`, an `Annotated[...]`) is likewise judged on its own. Only the outermost `Literal` of a nest is reported, once per nest, on its whole expression from the head through the closing bracket, in .py and .pyi files alike. A string Ruff reads as a type definition (an annotation, a typing generic's slice, `cast`/`TypeVar` arguments) is read the same way and reported on the inner expression's range inside the string; strings inside a `Literal[...]` are not read. A local `Literal` that is not the typing one, a star import from `typing`, and files that never import it are not reported. No known divergence.

### `ruff-ruf043-pytest-raises-ambiguous-pattern`

Port of Ruff's RUF043 (pytest-raises-ambiguous-pattern): a call whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves to `pytest.raises` through the file's imports and aliases, whose first `match=` keyword argument is a plain string literal: a `str` literal or an implicit concatenation of `str` literals, possibly parenthesized, in which no part carries an `r`, `b`, `f` or `t` prefix (so raw parts, bytes, f-strings and concatenations holding any of them are skipped; `u` is fine). The decoded value is scanned as Ruff scans it: a backslash escapes the next decoded character (a lone trailing backslash is ignored, and the backslash may sit at the end of one concatenated part and the escaped character at the start of the next), and the string is reported when it holds an unescaped `. ^ $ * + ? { [ | ( )` or an escaped regex metasequence (`\A \b \B \d \D \s \S \w \W \z \Z`), so `"\\N{EN DASH}"` is reported as in Ruff. Reported on the string literal alone (all concatenated parts, parentheses excluded). A positional second argument, `**kwargs`, a second duplicate `match=`, and `match=` passed to `pytest.warns` or other calls are not reported.

### `ruff-ruf046-unnecessary-cast-to-int`

Port of Ruff's RUF046 (unnecessary-cast-to-int): a call to the builtin `int` (the unshadowed name or `builtins.int`; parentheses around the callee are transparent) with exactly one positional argument and no keywords, reported on the whole `int(...)` call, when the argument (parentheses transparent, a walrus allowed) is either an expression Ruff's simple type inference types as `int`, or a call Ruff knows returns an `int`. The first is an integer literal (not complex), `+`, `-` or `~` of an int or bool expression, one of `+ - * % // ** & | ^ << >>` applied to two int or bool expressions, a conditional or `and`/`or` whose branches are int or bool with at least one int, or a walrus of such a value; `int(True)`, `int(not x)`, `int(1 / 2)`, `int(1.5)`, `int(1j)`, `int(x + 1)` and `int(x if y else 1)` are left alone. The second is a call to `len`, `id`, `hash`, `ord` or `int` (unshadowed builtins or `builtins.*`), `math.comb`, `math.factorial`, `math.gcd`, `math.lcm`, `math.isqrt`, `math.perm`, `math.ceil`, `math.floor` or `math.trunc` (any arguments), or to `round` under Ruff's argument reading: at most two arguments, no `*` or `**` unpacking, a number argument (positional or `number=`), and either no ndigits (or `None`) with any number, or an int-typed ndigits (positional or `ndigits=`) with an int-typed number. An int-typed name is one whose only binding in its scope is annotated `int` (assignment or non-variadic parameter, also `builtins.int` and `int[...]`), assigned an int-typed value or an `int(...)` call (plain, tuple-unpacked, chained or walrus), bound by `with int(...) as n`, or a function declared `-> int`. Two clauses give different advice: integer-valued arguments and always-int builtins, and `round`/`math.ceil`/`math.floor`/`math.trunc` calls (where the message warns about operands overriding the rounding dunder). Strings Ruff reads as type definitions (`x: "int(1)"`, `List["int(len(x))"]`, `cast("int(round(y))", z)`) are parsed as annotations and checked the same way, one finding per call. A single generator argument to `round` counts as Ruff counts it. The fix applicability is not modelled, so unsafe-fix cases are reported too, as in Ruff.

### `ruff-ruf048-map-int-version-parsing`

Port of Ruff's RUF048 (map-int-version-parsing): matches a call of the builtin `map` (shadowing respected, `builtins.map` and aliases included) with exactly two positional arguments and no keywords, the first the builtin `int` and the second a call `__version__.split(".")` or `x.__version__.split(".")` whose only argument is a plain string whose value is "." (escapes and implicit concatenation decoded; positional or `sep=`). Parentheses are looked through everywhere, including around the callee (`(__version__.split)(".")`), and comments and line continuations may sit between tokens. Also checked inside string annotations and forward references. Reported on the `map(...)` call, starting at `map` even when written as `*map(...)`, with a comment or line continuation after the star. Divergence: a starred `*map(...)` inside a string annotation is reported from the star.

### `ruff-ruf049-dataclass-enum`

Port of Ruff's RUF049 (dataclass-enum): matches the `@dataclasses.dataclass` decorator (bare, called, parenthesized, aliased, across line continuations) on a class with a positional base that is an enum.Enum family class (Enum, Flag, IntEnum, IntFlag, StrEnum, ReprEnum, CheckEnum) by qualified name, directly, subscripted or parenthesized, or a class defined in the file (by name, or one level through a class attribute such as `Outer.Inner` when that class is the latest binding of the name in its class body) that itself derives from one. A base name declared `global` in the enclosing function is not followed, and a base that names the decorated class itself (`class E(E)`) is followed only by qualified name, as Ruff resolves it to the new class. Reported on the decorator from `@` to the end of its expression (a trailing comment excluded), unless an earlier decorator of the class is a dataclass or attrs decorator. Divergences: enum bases reached through two or more nested class attributes (`Deep.Mid.Inner`) are missed, and base-class names rebound between class definitions are followed where they are written rather than at the decorated class.

### `ruff-ruf051-if-key-in-dict-del`

Port of Ruff's RUF051 (if-key-in-dict-del): matches an `if KEY in DICT:` with no elif/else whose body is exactly one `del DICT[KEY]` (a trailing semicolon or comma allowed), DICT a plain name on both sides (parentheses aside) and KEY the same name or an equal literal on both sides (str, bytes, int, float, complex, bool, None, Ellipsis compared by value as Ruff does, so `0x1` equals `1` and `'a'` equals `"a"`, but `1` differs from `1.0` and `True`). DICT must have a single unshadowed binding known to be a dict as Ruff's is_known_to_be_of_type_dict decides: assigned (also through tuple unpacking, a walrus, or a `with` item, plain or tuple target) a dict display, dict comprehension, `dict(...)` call, or a conditional or boolean expression of those; a parameter or variable annotated `dict`, `dict[...]` or `typing.Dict` (string annotations excluded); a `**kwargs` parameter; or a function whose return annotation is one of those. `defaultdict`, `OrderedDict`, `Counter` and other mappings are not matched. Reported on the `del` statement.

### `ruff-ruf053-class-with-mixed-type-vars`

Port of Ruff's RUF053 (class-with-mixed-type-vars): reports the first positional base of a class with a PEP 695 type parameter list that is a subscript `Generic[...]` whose `Generic` resolves to typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed (parentheses around the base or around `Generic` allowed; the reported span is the subscript, parentheses excluded). Keyword, starred and nested uses are ignored, and a type parameter named `Generic` shadows it, as in Ruff. Only applies for target Python 3.12 or newer, as in Ruff. The port-readiness recording lints Ruff's fixture at the CLI default target version, below 3.12, so its fires check has nothing to reproduce; the document was verified separately at 3.12 against live Ruff and Ruff's snapshot.

### `ruff-ruf057-unnecessary-round`

Port of Ruff's RUF057 (unnecessary-round): reports a call to builtin `round` (including `builtins.round`, parentheses allowed) with at most two arguments and no `*args`/`**kwargs`, where the rounded value (the `number=` keyword if present, else the first positional) is an integer (integer literal, simple integer expression including bool operands that yield an int, or a name whose only binding is annotated `int`, assigned an integer or `int(...)` call directly or through tuple unpacking, a walrus, a `with int(...) as` target, or a function returning `int`) and `ndigits` (keyword, else second positional) is absent, `None`, or a non-negative integer literal that fits in 64 bits. Other keyword arguments do not block the report, as in Ruff. Literal ndigits beyond i64, negative or computed ndigits, and names as ndigits are not reported. Divergence: Ruff resolves `int` in an annotation as seen from the call site; the port resolves it at the annotation and additionally only discounts it when `int` is a parameter or comprehension variable around the call, so a local or later module rebinding of `int` (`int = float`, `from m import int`, `def int`, `for int in ...`) seen from the call site is not honoured.

### `ruff-ruf059-unused-unpacked-variable`

Port of Ruff's RUF059 (unused-unpacked-variable): reports each name bound in a function or lambda scope by an assignment statement that Ruff counts as unpacking (`a, b = t`, `(a, b) = t`, `[a, b] = t`, `a, *b = t`, chained forms such as `(a, b) = c = t`), or by a `with` item whose target is a tuple (`with f() as (a, [b, c])`, not `with f() as [a, b]`), when its latest binding is never read, it is not declared global or nonlocal, and the function does not call `locals()` (a lambda calling `locals()` still reports). A target is not unpacking when the value is a tuple, list or set display and every target of the chain is a tuple or list (the empty `()` and `[]` targets included); walrus bindings inside such a statement or inside a tuple `with` target count, as Ruff's statement-level check does (`a, b = (y := t)` reports `y`). Names matching the dummy-variable pattern (`_`, `_x`, an option with Ruff's default) and `__tracebackhide__`-style names are exempt. It reuses the usedness logic of the F841 port (binds with form, scope, latest, outer, used). Divergence: Ruff's own `# ruff: ignore[...]` and `# ruff: disable[...]` suppression comments are not honoured, only noqa.

### `ruff-ruf060-in-empty-collection`

Port of Ruff's RUF060 (in-empty-collection): reports a single-operator `in` or `not in` comparison (chained comparisons such as `a < b in []` are skipped, `(a < b) in []` is not) whose right operand is an empty list, tuple or dict literal, an empty string, bytes or f-string literal, or a call with no arguments of a non-shadowed builtin `list`, `tuple`, `set`, `frozenset`, `dict`, `bytes`, `bytearray` or `str`, or `set`/`frozenset` whose first positional argument (before any starred argument) is itself empty (`set(set())`, `frozenset([])`, `set(list())`). Empty strings include empty implicit concatenations and backslash-newline-only strings unless raw; an f-string is empty when its literal text is empty (backslash-newlines allowed unless raw) and every interpolation has no conversion, format spec or `=` and holds an empty string or f-string; inside an interpolation, as in Ruff, the plain-string parts of an implicit concatenation that contains an f-string are ignored (`f"{'x' f''}"` is empty), and bytes are never empty. Builtins are also recognised through `builtins.` and `from builtins import`, and respect shadowing and `global` declarations. Comparisons inside string type annotations and `cast(...)` strings are checked too, reported at the comparison inside the string. Parentheses around the operand are transparent, t-strings are not empty, and the span is the whole comparison including operand parentheses. Two clauses (`in`, `not in`) give different replacement advice, noting that an empty string is still found in an empty str or bytes. Divergence: Ruff's own `# ruff: ignore[...]` and `# ruff: disable[...]` suppression comments are not honoured, only noqa.

### `ruff-ruf061-legacy-form-pytest-raises`

Port of Ruff's RUF061 (legacy-form-pytest-raises): reports a call to `pytest.raises`, `pytest.warns` or `pytest.deprecated_call`, resolved through imports and aliases (`import pytest as pt`, `import pytest.sub`, `from pytest import raises as r`) with shadowing and rebinding respected, that passes the callable to run: a `func=` keyword anywhere in the call, or the second positional argument for raises and warns, or the first for deprecated_call, provided no starred argument comes before it; a lone generator argument counts for deprecated_call. `match=` and other keywords alone, `expected_exception=` alone and `**kwargs` do not report. Calls inside string type annotations and `cast(...)` strings are checked too, reported at the call inside the string. The span is the call itself, so `pytest.raises(...).match(...)` reports the inner call, and parenthesized callees are transparent. One clause each for raises, warns and deprecated_call with its own replacement advice. Divergence: Ruff's own `# ruff: ignore[...]` and `# ruff: disable[...]` suppression comments are not honoured, only noqa.

### `ruff-ruf063-access-annotations-from-class-dict`

Port of Ruff's RUF063 (access-annotations-from-class-dict): reports `X.__dict__.get("__annotations__")` with an optional second argument, and `X.__dict__["__annotations__"]`, for any receiver X. The call form needs no keyword or `**` arguments, at most two arguments, and a first positional argument (before any starred one) that is a plain string literal equal to `__annotations__`; raw and u prefixes, escapes, implicit concatenation and parentheses are looked through, while bytes and f-strings do not match. The subscript form needs a single string subscript (a tuple subscript does not match). Load, store and delete positions all report, on the whole call or subscript, and parenthesised receivers and callees and backslash continuations are handled. Calls and subscripts inside string annotations (forward references, cast, TypeVar bound and similar) are reported at their position inside the string, as Ruff does, and the same text in an ordinary string is not. Divergence: Ruff skips the rule when the target version is below 3.10 and `lint.typing-extensions` is off; that setting defaults to on, so the port matches Ruff's defaults and differs only for projects that turn it off on an older target. The message names `annotationlib`, `inspect` and `typing_extensions` replacements so it fits every version.

### `ruff-ruf064-non-octal-permissions`

Port of Ruff's RUF064 (non-octal-permissions): reports an integer-literal mode that is not an octal literal: decimal, hex or binary, and multi-digit zeros such as `000` or `0_0`, but not `0` alone, not `0o`/`0O` literals, and not computed, negative, boolean, float or complex values. Parentheses around the literal are looked through and the literal itself is reported. Mode arguments covered: `os.umask` (position 0), `os.chmod`, `fchmod`, `lchmod`, `mkdir`, `makedirs`, `mkfifo` and `mknod` (position 1), `os.open`, `dbm.open`, `dbm.gnu.open` and `dbm.ndbm.open` (position 2), and `chmod`, `lchmod`, `mkdir` and `touch` on `pathlib.Path`, `PosixPath` and `WindowsPath` (position 0). The argument is found as Ruff's find_argument does: a `mode=` keyword wins, else the positional argument at that index before any starred one. Comments and backslash continuations in the argument list do not count as arguments, and `**kw` passes nothing. The `parent_mode` keyword of `os.makedirs` and `Path.mkdir` is checked as well and can report alongside `mode`. Callees, possibly parenthesised, resolve through imports and aliases and are not matched when shadowed. A Path receiver is a direct `Path(...)` call or a name whose latest binding value is such a call (plain, annotated, walrus, element-wise unpacking with matching arity, chained assignment, or `with ... as name`); names from parameters, loops, comprehensions, match patterns or another name do not count. Known divergences: Ruff also pairs unpacked names by index when arities differ, around starred targets, from set displays and through tuple `with` targets, and it reports calls inside string annotations; the port does none of these (see reason).

### `ruff-ruf068-duplicate-entry-in-dunder-all`

Port of Ruff's RUF068 (duplicate-entry-in-dunder-all): reports each later repeat of a string entry in a list, tuple or bare tuple (`__all__ = "A", "A"`) assigned to `__all__`. The assignment can be `=` (a single target; chained assignment is skipped), `+=` (other augmented operators are not), an annotated assignment with a value, or the only positional argument of `__all__.extend(...)` (comments and backslash continuations in the argument list are not arguments). Only module scope counts. Statements inside `if`, `try`, `with`, loops and `match` at module level are in scope. Function bodies, class bodies and headers (bases, keywords), parameter and return annotations, type parameters, `type` aliases, lambda bodies, comprehension bodies, conditions and targets are not. Parameter defaults, decorators, variable annotations, lambda defaults and the first comprehension iterable are in scope. `__all__.extend(...)` written inside a module-level string annotation, or another string Ruff reads as a type (a forward reference, `cast`, a `TypeAlias` value), is reported at its position inside the string, as Ruff does; lambdas and comprehensions inside such a string are skipped. Entries are compared by decoded string value, so quote style, prefixes, escapes, implicit concatenation and parentheses do not matter, and only plain string literals take part (bytes, f-strings and names are skipped). The target may be parenthesised. The report is on the later duplicate itself, inside any parentheses, one per extra repeat. Duplicates are found only within one literal, not across separate statements. No known divergence from Ruff outside lambdas or comprehensions written inside string annotations.

### `ruff-slot000-no-slots-in-str-subclass`

Port of Ruff's SLOT000 (no-slots-in-str-subclass): flags a class whose positional bases include `str`, resolved to the builtin (shadowing and `builtins.str` are handled, parenthesized bases included). A class is exempt when any base is an `enum` class (Enum, Flag, IntEnum, IntFlag, StrEnum, ReprEnum, CheckEnum). The enum base may be named directly, subscripted, or reached through a local class, a mixin chain, or a nested-class attribute path of any depth (`Outer.Deep.Deeper`). As in Ruff's lookup_attribute, a member counts when its class is the live binding of that name anywhere in the enclosing class's own scope, inside `if` or `try` included but not inside methods; a member rebound later in the class body does not count. A class is exempt when its body directly assigns `__slots__` (plain, annotated or chained assignment to the name `__slots__`, with or without redundant parentheses; tuple, list and starred targets do not count, as in Ruff's helper). Skips stub files. Reports the class name identifier. A type parameter named like the builtin (`class A[str](...)`) does not shadow it, since Ruff resolves the builtin outside the type-parameter scope; the port applies this only when the file neither imports `builtins` nor binds that name anywhere else, so such a class in a file that also binds the name in an unrelated scope is not reported. Divergence: Ruff resolves the bases of a local base class from the scope of the class being checked, and the port resolves them where they are written. So a str subclass whose base class is defined in another scope (a nested class, or a module class seen from a function that shadows one of its base names) can be exempted where Ruff reports it.

### `ruff-slot001-no-slots-in-tuple-subclass`

Port of Ruff's SLOT001 (no-slots-in-tuple-subclass): flags a class with a positional base that resolves to builtin `tuple` or to `Tuple` from `typing`, `typing_extensions` or `_typeshed`. The base may be bare or subscripted (`tuple[int]`, `Tuple[int]`); Ruff unwraps one subscript level, so `Tuple[int][0]` is not flagged. Parenthesized bases are handled. Such a class is flagged when it does not define `__slots__`. A class is exempt when its body directly assigns `__slots__` (plain, annotated or chained assignment to the name `__slots__`, with or without redundant parentheses; tuple, list and starred targets do not count, as in Ruff's helper). Skips stub files. Reports the class name identifier. A type parameter named like the builtin (`class A[tuple](...)`) does not shadow it, since Ruff resolves the builtin outside the type-parameter scope; the port applies this only when the file neither imports `builtins` nor binds that name anywhere else, so such a class in a file that also binds the name in an unrelated scope is not reported.

### `ruff-slot002-no-slots-in-namedtuple-subclass`

Port of Ruff's SLOT002 (no-slots-in-namedtuple-subclass): flags a class whose positional bases are only calls to `collections.namedtuple` or `typing.NamedTuple` (at least one) and, optionally, builtin `object`. Keywords, `**` splats, comments and line continuations in the base list are ignored. Any other base exempts the class, as in Ruff's `namedtuple_base`: another class, a starred base, a call whose callee does not resolve, or an `enum` call. A class is exempt when its body directly assigns `__slots__` (plain, annotated or chained assignment to the name `__slots__`, with or without redundant parentheses; tuple, list and starred targets do not count, as in Ruff's helper). Skips stub files. Reports the class name identifier. A type parameter named like the builtin (`class A[object](...)`) does not shadow it, since Ruff resolves the builtin outside the type-parameter scope; the port applies this only when the file neither imports `builtins` nor binds that name anywhere else, so such a class in a file that also binds the name in an unrelated scope is not reported.

### `ruff-t201-print`

Port of Ruff's T201 (print): matches a call whose callee resolves to the builtin `print` (a bare name that no assignment, def, parameter, import, except-as, match capture, type parameter or comprehension variable rebinds, `builtins.print` through `import builtins` or an alias, or an alias of `from builtins import print`), through any number of parentheses, and reports the callee (`print`, not the whole call) at the same start and end as Ruff. A call is skipped when it has a `file=` keyword whose value is not `None`, `sys.stdout` or `sys.stderr` (read through parentheses and imports, so `from sys import stderr as e` counts as `sys.stderr`, while `sys.stdout.buffer` or `sys.__stdout__` exempt the call); only a real keyword counts, so a positional second argument and `**kwargs` do not exempt it, as in Ruff. Calls inside string type definitions (`x: "print(1)"`, `List["print(1)"]`, `cast("print(1)", v)`, `TypeVar(bound=...)`, not inside `Literal[...]`) are parsed and reported at their position in the string, one finding per call, as Ruff does. A first-position star argument (`[*print(x)]`, `{*print(x)}`, `[*builtins.print(x)]`), which tree-sitter parses as a splat applied to the callee, is matched at Ruff's span. A file that Ruff cannot parse as Python 3 but tree-sitter accepts is skipped as a whole, because Ruff then reports only the syntax error: a Python 2 `print` statement without a chevron, an `exec` statement, a decimal integer with a leading zero (`0777`) or a long suffix (`10L`), a backtick repr or `ur` string prefix, the `<>` operator, `raise E, msg`, and a tuple parameter (`def f(a, (b, c))`, `lambda (a, b): a`); `print >>f, x`, `00`, `07j` and `except A, B:` are valid Python 3 and do not trigger this. One divergence: inside a function or class that rebinds `print`, a string annotation's `print(...)` is reported by Ruff (forward references resolve at module scope, where the builtin lives) but not by this rule. Suppression is `# noqa: T201` (line, multi-line string and continuation ranges) and the file-level forms.

### `ruff-t203-p-print`

Port of Ruff's T203 (p-print): matches a call whose callee resolves through imports to `pprint.pprint` (`import pprint` then `pprint.pprint(x)`, `import pprint as p`, `from pprint import pprint`, `from pprint import pprint as pp`, including an alias named `print`), through any number of parentheses, and reports the callee at the same start and end as Ruff. `pprint.pp`, `pprint.pformat`, `PrettyPrinter().pprint`, an assignment alias (`pp = pprint.pprint`), a star import and the module name called by itself do not match, and a name that a def, assignment, parameter, except-as or other import rebinds does not resolve to `pprint.pprint`. A call is skipped when it has a `stream=` keyword whose value is not `None`, `sys.stdout` or `sys.stderr` (read through parentheses and imports); only a real keyword counts, so a positional second argument (`pprint.pprint(x, sys.stderr)` or an open file), a `file=` keyword and `**kwargs` do not exempt the call, as in Ruff. Calls inside string type definitions (`x: "pprint(1)"`, `List["pprint(1)"]`, `cast`, `TypeVar(bound=...)`) are parsed and reported at their position in the string, resolving the name at module scope first as Ruff does. A first-position star argument (`[*pprint.pprint(x)]`), which tree-sitter parses as a splat applied to the callee (the attribute form starts at the star), is matched at Ruff's span. A file that Ruff cannot parse as Python 3 but tree-sitter accepts is skipped as a whole, because Ruff then reports only the syntax error: a Python 2 `print` statement without a chevron, an `exec` statement, a decimal integer with a leading zero (`0777`) or a long suffix (`10L`), a backtick repr or `ur` string prefix, the `<>` operator, `raise E, msg`, and a tuple parameter (`def f(a, (b, c))`, `lambda (a, b): a`); `print >>f, x`, `00`, `07j` and `except A, B:` are valid Python 3 and do not trigger this. Suppression is `# noqa: T203` and the file-level forms.

### `ruff-tc001-typing-only-first-party-import`

Port of Ruff's TC001 (typing-only-first-party-import): reports an import binding of a module or function scope that is used only in annotations Ruff treats as typing-only (annotations of local variables, any annotation under `from __future__ import annotations` except a singledispatch implementation's non-variadic parameters and return, and except the `InitVar`/`KW_ONLY` head of a `@dataclass` field annotation), in string type definitions, or inside an `if TYPE_CHECKING:` block (also parenthesized, `typing.TYPE_CHECKING`, aliases); it is never used at runtime, not exported through `__all__`, not itself inside an `if TYPE_CHECKING:` block, not a `__future__`, `typing` or `typing_extensions` import, and does not share its module name with another import used at runtime in the same or an enclosing scope, class bodies included (Ruff's non-strict default). Other `__future__` features than `annotations` leave annotations runtime-evaluated. Arguments after the first of `Annotated[...]` count as runtime uses. Findings are anchored on the bound name; `.pyi` files are skipped. First-party means a relative import (`from . import x`, `from .pkg import y`, `from .. import z`) or `__main__`. Divergences: Ruff also treats an absolute import as first-party when the module exists under the project's `src` roots or package, which cannot be seen from the document (those imports are classified as TC002 or TC003 here); and Annotated metadata written inside a string annotation is not recognized as a runtime use.

### `ruff-tc002-typing-only-third-party-import`

Port of Ruff's TC002 (typing-only-third-party-import): reports an import binding of a module or function scope that is used only in annotations Ruff treats as typing-only (annotations of local variables, any annotation under `from __future__ import annotations` except a singledispatch implementation's non-variadic parameters and return, and except the `InitVar`/`KW_ONLY` head of a `@dataclass` field annotation), in string type definitions, or inside an `if TYPE_CHECKING:` block (also parenthesized, `typing.TYPE_CHECKING`, aliases); it is never used at runtime, not exported through `__all__`, not itself inside an `if TYPE_CHECKING:` block, not a `__future__`, `typing` or `typing_extensions` import, and does not share its module name with another import used at runtime in the same or an enclosing scope, class bodies included (Ruff's non-strict default). Other `__future__` features than `annotations` leave annotations runtime-evaluated. Arguments after the first of `Annotated[...]` count as runtime uses. Findings are anchored on the bound name; `.pyi` files are skipped. Third-party means an absolute import whose top-level module is not `__main__`, `__future__` or a Python 3.10 standard library module. Divergences: Ruff classifies an absolute import as first-party (TC001) when the module exists under the project's `src` roots or package, which cannot be seen from the document; and Annotated metadata written inside a string annotation is not recognized as a runtime use.

### `ruff-tc003-typing-only-standard-library-import`

Port of Ruff's TC003 (typing-only-standard-library-import): reports an import binding of a module or function scope that is used only in annotations Ruff treats as typing-only (annotations of local variables, any annotation under `from __future__ import annotations` except a singledispatch implementation's non-variadic parameters and return, and except the `InitVar`/`KW_ONLY` head of a `@dataclass` field annotation), in string type definitions, or inside an `if TYPE_CHECKING:` block (also parenthesized, `typing.TYPE_CHECKING`, aliases); it is never used at runtime, not exported through `__all__`, not itself inside an `if TYPE_CHECKING:` block, not a `__future__`, `typing` or `typing_extensions` import, and does not share its module name with another import used at runtime in the same or an enclosing scope, class bodies included (Ruff's non-strict default). Other `__future__` features than `annotations` leave annotations runtime-evaluated. Arguments after the first of `Annotated[...]` count as runtime uses. Findings are anchored on the bound name; `.pyi` files are skipped. Standard library means the import's top-level module is in Python 3.10's standard library module list (Ruff's default target version). Divergence: Annotated metadata written inside a string annotation is not recognized as a runtime use.

### `ruff-tc004-runtime-import-in-type-checking-block`

Port of Ruff's TC004 (runtime-import-in-type-checking-block): reports the live import binding of a module or function scope (not class scope, not in .pyi stubs) made inside the body of an `if TYPE_CHECKING:` block (bare name, an imported alias of typing/typing_extensions/_typeshed TYPE_CHECKING, any `x.TYPE_CHECKING` attribute, parenthesized; not `elif`/`else`) when its name has a runtime reference. As in Ruff, a re-binding inherits the reads of every earlier binding of the name in the same scope (so `import foo; foo(); if TYPE_CHECKING: import foo` is reported), except across an unconditional `del` (one outside if/while/match bodies, except handlers and try-else). Typing-only contexts are excluded: other TYPE_CHECKING bodies, strings, `type` alias statements and PEP 695 type parameters, local variable annotations in functions, and every annotation under `from __future__ import annotations` except singledispatch implementation parameters/returns and the `ClassVar`/`InitVar`/`KW_ONLY` head of a field annotation in a `@dataclass` class. Names listed in a module-scope `__all__` (list/tuple literals, `+` chains, `list()`/`tuple()` calls, augmented and annotated assignments) count as runtime uses unless the module binds `__getattr__` (def, assignment, import, class, at any nesting, and not later deleted); an explicit `import a as a` re-export alone is not a use. Default settings only (quote-annotations, runtime-evaluated base classes and decorators are not modeled); a name left unbound by an `except ... as` clause is not modeled as breaking inherited reads. The span is the bound name; noqa applies on that line or the import statement's first line.

### `ruff-tc005-empty-type-checking-block`

Port of Ruff's TC005 (empty-type-checking-block): reports a lone `pass` that is the entire body of an `if TYPE_CHECKING:` statement with no `elif`/`else` clauses (test is `TYPE_CHECKING` by name, an imported alias of typing/typing_extensions/_typeshed TYPE_CHECKING, or any attribute `.TYPE_CHECKING`, parenthesized allowed). A `pass` sharing the block with other statements (including `;`-joined ones) is not reported; comments around it are ignored. Span is the `pass` statement.

### `ruff-tc006-runtime-cast-value`

Port of Ruff's TC006 (runtime-cast-value): reports the first positional argument in source order (or the `typ=` keyword value) of a call to `typing.cast` (also via typing_extensions/_typeshed, aliases, attribute access and a parenthesized callee) unless it is a plain string literal (implicit concatenation of plain strings and parenthesized strings count; bytes, f-strings and t-strings do not). Calls inside string type definitions (a quoted annotation or a quoted cast type) are found by parsing the string as Ruff does. Not checked in .pyi stubs. A sole unparenthesized generator-expression argument is reported on the expression without the call parentheses or surrounding comments, as Ruff does, through a clause anchored on a regex capture; a trailing comment there that contains a quote character is still included in the span.

### `ruff-tc007-unquoted-type-alias`

Port of Ruff's TC007 (unquoted-type-alias): reports each name in the value of an explicit `X: TypeAlias = ...` assignment (typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed TypeAlias, a plain or parenthesized name target, outside an `if TYPE_CHECKING:` block and outside stub files) that resolves to a binding but has no runtime binding in the module scope before it. As in Ruff, the runtime lookup is always made in the module scope, whatever scope holds the alias: a name bound only inside an `if TYPE_CHECKING:` block, an annotation-only declaration or a `del` does not count, a module-level binding later in the file does not count, and an alias inside a function or class body is reported for names bound only locally there (a local class, a parameter, a class attribute). Names are collected as Ruff does: through binary operators, parentheses, list and tuple elements, starred items, the object of attribute chains and subscript values and slices, skipping the slice of `Literal[...]` and every `Annotated[...]` argument but the first (a lone `Annotated[*X]` slice is a one-element tuple and is read). The TC004-enabled exemption is not modelled because Ruff applies it only when that rule is enabled. Divergences: a type-checking-only or local name that shadows a builtin is reported where Ruff finds the builtin; a walrus inside the alias value makes Ruff report each name twice, the port once.

### `ruff-tc010-runtime-string-union`

Port of Ruff's TC010 (runtime-string-union): reports each string (and bytes) literal that is an operand of a `|` chain in a type definition evaluated at runtime, that is, outside `if TYPE_CHECKING:` blocks, not a local variable annotation inside a function, not in a stub annotation, and, for annotations, not after a `from __future__ import annotations` (other `__future__` imports do not count) and not for target versions 3.14 and later. Type definitions outside annotations (cast, TypeVar, NewType, NamedTuple and TypedDict fields, TypeAlias values, typing generics) are recognised as Ruff recognises them, including the legacy keyword fields of `TypedDict("T", a=...)` before Python 3.13 (not `total`, and only when the field mapping is omitted or None). Chains are judged from their root, so operands directly inside `Union[...]` (including a tuple slice) are not reported, as Ruff skips nested unions; `Annotated[...]` metadata and call arguments are not type definitions. f-strings and t-strings are ignored. Divergence: bytes members and strings inside `Literal[...]` are reported only in annotations and legacy TypedDict keyword fields, not in other type definitions such as `cast(b"x" | B, v)`.

### `ruff-td001-invalid-todo-tag`

Port of Ruff's TD001 (invalid-todo-tag): reports the tag of a comment whose directive is `FIXME` or `XXX` in any capitalisation (not `TODO`, `HACK`), covering just the tag word. A comment is read the way Ruff's `TodoDirective::from_comment` does: split on `#`, in each segment skip `#` characters and whitespace and take the first alphanumeric (Unicode alphabetic or numeric) run; the first segment whose word is `todo`, `fixme`, `hack` or `xxx` in any case decides the comment, so `# TODO # FIXME` is a TODO. HACK is never reported. The word must end at a non-alphanumeric character (`# TODOS`, `# FIXME2` are silent; a combining accent after the tag does not extend it). Strings and docstrings are never comments; `# noqa: TD001` and file-level `# ruff: noqa: TD001` are honoured. Start and end positions match Ruff on its own tests and on about 60 probe files (multiple `#` segments, inline, header and block comments, comments before closing brackets and `else`, decorators, match, f-string replacement fields, Unicode whitespace and digits, BOM, CRLF, bare CR, form feed, stubs, EOF without newline, noqa and type-ignore pragmas). A HACK tag spelled with the Kelvin sign makes Ruff 0.16.9 panic on the file, so that case cannot be compared.

### `ruff-td002-missing-todo-author`

Port of Ruff's TD002 (missing-todo-author): reports a `TODO`, `FIXME` or `XXX` tag (any case) with no author, covering just the tag word. After the tag and any whitespace, a `(` (closed or not) starts an author, and so does an `@` followed somewhere by whitespace or a colon (so `@bob text` and even `@: text` count); anything else reports: nothing, text, a colon (`# TODO: x`, `# TODO : x`), or an `@` run that ends the comment (`# TODO @bob`, `# TODO @`). That last form has its own clause and message, since the author is there but needs a colon and a description after it. A comment is read the way Ruff's `TodoDirective::from_comment` does: split on `#`, in each segment skip `#` characters and whitespace and take the first alphanumeric (Unicode alphabetic or numeric) run; the first segment whose word is `todo`, `fixme`, `hack` or `xxx` in any case decides the comment, so `# TODO # FIXME` is a TODO. HACK is never reported. The word must end at a non-alphanumeric character (`# TODOS`, `# FIXME2` are silent; a combining accent after the tag does not extend it). Strings and docstrings are never comments; `# noqa: TD002` and file-level `# ruff: noqa: TD002` are honoured. Start and end positions match Ruff on its own tests and on about 60 probe files (multiple `#` segments, inline, header and block comments, comments before closing brackets and `else`, decorators, match, f-string replacement fields, Unicode whitespace and digits, BOM, CRLF, bare CR, form feed, stubs, EOF without newline, noqa and type-ignore pragmas). A HACK tag spelled with the Kelvin sign makes Ruff 0.16.9 panic on the file, so that case cannot be compared.

### `ruff-td003-missing-todo-link`

Port of Ruff's TD003 (missing-todo-link): reports a `TODO`, `FIXME` or `XXX` tag (HACK is skipped) unless the rest of its line after the tag contains `http://`/`https://` or `#` followed by a (Unicode) digit, or a following comment in the same block (comments separated only by whitespace, stopping at the next directive comment of any kind including HACK) is a URL, a number, or an uppercase issue id such as `ABC-12` on its own line. Any code or bracket between comments ends the block, including a closing bracket or an `else:`; a header comment after `if x:` reaches the comments at the top of the body. Covers just the tag word. A comment is read the way Ruff's `TodoDirective::from_comment` does: split on `#`, in each segment skip `#` characters and whitespace and take the first alphanumeric (Unicode alphabetic or numeric) run; the first segment whose word is `todo`, `fixme`, `hack` or `xxx` in any case decides the comment, so `# TODO # FIXME` is a TODO. HACK is never reported. The word must end at a non-alphanumeric character (`# TODOS`, `# FIXME2` are silent; a combining accent after the tag does not extend it). Strings and docstrings are never comments; `# noqa: TD003` and file-level `# ruff: noqa: TD003` are honoured. Start and end positions match Ruff on its own tests and on about 60 probe files (multiple `#` segments, inline, header and block comments, comments before closing brackets and `else`, decorators, match, f-string replacement fields, Unicode whitespace and digits, BOM, CRLF, bare CR, form feed, stubs, EOF without newline, noqa and type-ignore pragmas). A HACK tag spelled with the Kelvin sign makes Ruff 0.16.9 panic on the file, so that case cannot be compared.

### `ruff-td004-missing-todo-colon`

Port of Ruff's TD004 (missing-todo-colon): reports a `TODO`, `FIXME` or `XXX` tag whose text after the optional author does not begin with a colon. The author is a `(...)` up to the first `)` (an unclosed `(` takes the rest of the comment, so it always reports) or an `@` run up to the first whitespace or colon; with no author the text after the tag's leading whitespace is checked, so `# TODO : x` is fine while `# TODO(a) : x`, `# TODO @a text`, `# TODO @a` and a bare `# TODO` report. Covers just the tag word. A comment is read the way Ruff's `TodoDirective::from_comment` does: split on `#`, in each segment skip `#` characters and whitespace and take the first alphanumeric (Unicode alphabetic or numeric) run; the first segment whose word is `todo`, `fixme`, `hack` or `xxx` in any case decides the comment, so `# TODO # FIXME` is a TODO. HACK is never reported. The word must end at a non-alphanumeric character (`# TODOS`, `# FIXME2` are silent; a combining accent after the tag does not extend it). Strings and docstrings are never comments; `# noqa: TD004` and file-level `# ruff: noqa: TD004` are honoured. Start and end positions match Ruff on its own tests and on about 60 probe files (multiple `#` segments, inline, header and block comments, comments before closing brackets and `else`, decorators, match, f-string replacement fields, Unicode whitespace and digits, BOM, CRLF, bare CR, form feed, stubs, EOF without newline, noqa and type-ignore pragmas). A HACK tag spelled with the Kelvin sign makes Ruff 0.16.9 panic on the file, so that case cannot be compared.

### `ruff-td005-missing-todo-description`

Port of Ruff's TD005 (missing-todo-description): matches a comment whose first TODO, FIXME or XXX directive (found by Ruff's '#'-section scan, case-insensitive; a HACK directive first suppresses the check) is followed by nothing but an optional author ('(...)', an unclosed '(' to the end of the comment, or '@name:') and an optional colon, and reports the directive. Divergences: comments inside syntax-error regions and '# ruff: disable[...]' range suppressions are not honored.

### `ruff-td006-invalid-todo-capitalization`

Port of Ruff's TD006 (invalid-todo-capitalization): matches a comment whose first directive word, found by Ruff's '#'-section scan, is a case variant of todo other than exactly TODO, and reports the tag. Only the first directive in a comment counts, so a HACK, FIXME or XXX tag before it hides later ones. Divergences: comments inside syntax-error regions and '# ruff: disable[...]' range suppressions are not honored.

### `ruff-td007-missing-space-after-todo-colon`

Port of Ruff's TD007 (missing-space-after-todo-colon): matches a comment whose first directive (TODO, FIXME or XXX, case-insensitive; a HACK directive first suppresses the check) is followed by an optional '(author)' or '@author' and then a colon directly followed by a non-whitespace character, and reports the directive. Divergences: comments inside syntax-error regions and '# ruff: disable[...]' range suppressions are not honored.

### `ruff-tid252-relative-imports`

Port of Ruff's TID252 (relative-imports): reports a `from` import whose relative prefix has two or more dots, Ruff's default `parents` strictness (the `all` setting that also bans single-dot imports is not ported). Dots separated by spaces or backslash continuations still count (`from . . import x` is level 2), and `from ..import x` and `from ..import*` with no space before `import` are caught. Like Ruff it reports once per imported name, including `*` and `as` aliases, each anchored on the whole `from` statement (so `from ..m import a, b, c` gives three findings at one span), and it reports imports at any depth (inside functions, classes, try blocks and `if TYPE_CHECKING`) and in .pyi stubs. `# noqa: TID252` suppresses it. It gives no autofix: the message names the relative module and asks for its absolute dotted path, and says to keep it relative only where the package layout makes that impractical. Divergences: a PEP 810 `lazy from .. import x` statement (Python 3.15 syntax) is not in the parser's grammar, so it is still reported once per name but anchored from `from` instead of from `lazy`; and Ruff skips files with real parse errors entirely, while this port still reports imports outside the error region. Ruff's own tests agree on all 20 findings with identical start and end positions, and the porter's 26 and the reviewer's 18 probe files agree with live Ruff apart from those two cases.

### `ruff-try002-raise-vanilla-class`

Port of Ruff's TRY002 (raise-vanilla-class): matches a name or attribute that resolves to the builtin `Exception` or `BaseException` (global, `builtins.` attribute, or a from-import of `builtins`), or a call of one, when it is the exception operand of `raise` (parentheses ignored, also with a `from` cause present). The cause operand after `from` is not reported. Shadowed names (class, import, parameter, handler, type parameter, module-level rebinding seen from a function body) are not reported, and a binding later in the same function does not shadow an earlier `raise`, as in Ruff. The report spans the exception expression, not the whole raise statement.

### `ruff-try003-raise-vanilla-args`

Port of Ruff's TRY003 (raise-vanilla-args): matches a call that is the exception operand of `raise` (parentheses ignored, not the `from` cause) whose first positional argument is a plain or f-string literal (also implicitly concatenated, parenthesized) containing a whitespace character (Unicode White_Space) in its literal text, typed or written as any escape (`\n`, `\x20`, `\040`, `　`, `\N{SPACE}`, ...); a backslash-newline line continuation is not whitespace, and in raw strings escapes stay literal. Bytes literals, t-strings and f-string interpolations (including format specs and `=` debug text) are ignored, and calls of the builtin `NotImplementedError` are skipped. Divergence: a named escape such as `\N{SPACE}` in an f-string that also has replacement fields is not detected.

### `ruff-try004-type-check-without-type-error`

Port of Ruff's TRY004 (type-check-without-type-error): for an `if` whose test is made only of builtin `isinstance`/`issubclass`/`callable` calls combined with `and`/`or`, `not`, unary operators and parentheses, reports each top-level `raise` of one of 15 builtin exceptions (Exception, ValueError, RuntimeError, AttributeError, ...) in its body, in each `elif` body (while every `elif` test is also a type check) and in the `else` body, spanning the whole raise statement. Scanning of a body stops at the first earlier statement containing `return`, `break` or `continue` (nested functions and classes excluded). When the `if` is directly nested in another `if` (any of its branches), the outer if's main test must also be a type check. Shadowed names are not reported. When `builtins` is not imported, a check function rebound anywhere in the same if statement's scope (assignment, loop target, import, def or class name), even after the raise, also turns the check off, as Ruff's late builtin lookup does. Divergence: such a rebinding in a parent if's body after a nested if suppresses the nested if's report, which Ruff still makes.

### `ruff-try201-verbose-raise`

Port of Ruff's TRY201 (verbose-raise): reports the name in `raise e` (the exception expression only, not the whole statement) when `e` is the name bound by an enclosing `except ... as e` handler and the raise has no `from` cause. Reproduces how far Ruff's raise-statement visitor reaches: nested if/elif/else, for/while (with else), with, match cases, and def and class bodies (decorated ones too) are searched. For a nested try reached directly, only its body and finally block are walked, so a raise sitting directly in them is not collected and the try's handlers and else are not searched. They are searched when that try itself sits in the body or finally of another such try. Parenthesized names count and are reported at the name. When two nested handlers bind the same name and both reach the raise, Ruff reports it twice and so does the document (through a second clause). Divergence: with three or more such handlers Ruff reports once per handler, but the document stops at two.

### `ruff-try203-useless-try-except`

Port of Ruff's TRY203 (useless-try-except): when every handler of a try statement begins with a bare `raise`, or with `raise name` for the handler's own `as name` (parenthesized names count), and has no `from` cause, every handler is reported. Only the first statement is checked, and comments before it are skipped. There is one finding per handler, spanning from `except` to the end of its last statement. except* handlers are included. A try with any handler that does something else reports nothing. The engine's handler node also covers trailing comments and semicolons, so the end is recovered with a regex that tokenizes the handler text into strings (single- and triple-quoted, with escapes), comments and other characters. A `#` or quote inside a string is therefore never taken for a comment, and trailing comments and `;` are trimmed.

### `ruff-try300-try-consider-else`

Port of Ruff's TRY300 (try-consider-else): reports the final `return` statement of a try body when the body has more than one statement, the try has at least one handler (except* included) and no else clause, and the returned value has no side effects as judged by Ruff's contains_effect. A value qualifies when it is a bare return, or when it has no calls, awaits, yields, subscripts, walrus assignments, comprehensions or generators and every binary operation in it has only literal operands. Empty `list()`, `tuple()`, `set()`, `dict()` and `frozenset()` calls on unshadowed builtins do not count as calls. Literal operands are strings, bytes, f-strings, numbers, booleans, None, ellipsis and list/tuple/set/dict displays; template strings are not. Lambda bodies and defaults and f-string interpolations are searched. The whole return statement is reported.

### `ruff-try301-raise-within-try`

Port of Ruff's TRY301 (raise-within-try): flags a `raise` with an exception (not a bare `raise`; the cause after `from` is not compared) anywhere in the body of a `try` statement, including inside nested functions and classes and inside `if`, loop, `with` and `match` blocks, but not inside a nested `try` statement (its body, handlers, `else` or `finally`), when one of that try's handlers catches it. A handler catches it when its type, or an element of its tuple, is the same expression as the raised one (the callee for a call), ignoring parentheses, or is `Exception` or `BaseException` resolved to the builtins (including `builtins.Exception` through an import); a shadowed `Exception` does not count. `except*` handlers and unparenthesized `except A, B:` lists are included. The whole `raise` statement is reported. No divergence found in 33 probe files.

### `ruff-try400-error-instead-of-exception`

Port of Ruff's TRY400 (error-instead-of-exception): flags a call of `error` on a logger anywhere inside an except handler body, including nested functions, classes, lambdas and comprehensions, but not inside the type expression of a nested handler (which Ruff never visits). A logger is a `logging.getLogger(...)` or `logging.Logger(...)` call, the `logging` module (under any alias), `flask.current_app.logger`, or a non-imported name or attribute chain whose last name starts with log or LOG or ends with logger or logging (or their upper-case forms); `logging.error` imported by name also counts. A call whose `exc_info` keyword is `True` or a `sys.exc_info()` call is not reported; any other `exc_info` value is. The whole call is reported. Divergences: unparenthesized starred operands (`y = *logger.error('a'), 1`, `[*logger.error('b')]`) are missed because of a tree-sitter grammar quirk, and in a function nested in the handler, names are resolved lexically where Ruff resolves them in the handler's scope.

### `ruff-try401-verbose-log-message`

Port of Ruff's TRY401 (verbose-log-message): flags each load of a name bound by an `except ... as` clause (of the enclosing handler or an outer one) in the positional arguments of a logger `exception(...)` call inside a handler body (same logger recognition as TRY400, or `logging.exception` imported by name), including an unparenthesized generator argument, and nested in f-strings, containers, calls, subscripts, operators and comprehensions, but not under an attribute access (`e.args`, `foo(e).x`), in keyword arguments or `**` splats of the call, inside the type expression of a nested handler, or where the name has been rebound (assignment, loop, `with`, walrus, match capture, lambda or comprehension variable) or refers to a handler that has already ended. Calls in nested function bodies and lambdas are not reported, as in Ruff. Each name is reported. Divergences: a handler name declared `nonlocal` is missed, and so is a name in an unparenthesized generator argument that is the handler's last statement.

### `ruff-up001-useless-metaclass-type`

Port of Ruff's UP001 (useless-metaclass-type): an assignment statement `__metaclass__ = type` with a single target, the name `__metaclass__` (parentheses transparent, so `(__metaclass__) = type`; not a tuple target, not an annotated assignment, not a chained `a = __metaclass__ = type`), whose value is the name `type` (parentheses transparent; the name is compared, not resolved, as in Ruff), anywhere in the file. Reported on the assignment statement.

### `ruff-up003-type-of-primitive`

Port of Ruff's UP003 (type-of-primitive): a call `type(x)` whose callee (parentheses transparent) resolves to the builtin `type` (Ruff's `match_builtin_expr`: the unshadowed name, or `type` of the `builtins` module through the file's imports) and whose positional arguments are exactly one literal (parentheses transparent; keyword arguments allowed, as Ruff reads only the positional ones): a str literal (not an f- or t-string; implicit concatenations included), a bytes literal, an int, float or complex number literal, or `True`/`False` (not `None`, `...` or a negated number). One clause per primitive, so the message names the builtin as Ruff's does. Reported on the call.

### `ruff-up004-useless-object-inheritance`

Port of Ruff's UP004 (useless-object-inheritance): each positional base of a class definition (not a keyword such as `metaclass=`, not a starred base; parentheses transparent) that resolves to the builtin `object` (Ruff's `match_builtin_expr`: the unshadowed name, or `object` of the `builtins` module through the file's imports), one finding per such base, reported on the base expression inside any parentheses.

### `ruff-up005-deprecated-unittest-alias`

Port of Ruff's UP005 (deprecated-unittest-alias): the callee of a call (parentheses transparent) that is an attribute `self.<alias>` whose object is the name `self` (parentheses transparent; compared, not resolved) and whose attribute is one of the fifteen deprecated `unittest` aliases Ruff lists (`assertEquals`, `failUnless`, `assert_`, ...). Reported on the attribute; the message names the replacement, as Ruff's does.

### `ruff-up006-non-pep585-annotation`

Port of Ruff's UP006 (non-pep585-annotation): reports each reference to typing.List, Dict, Set, FrozenSet, Tuple, Type, Deque or DefaultDict (typing_extensions only for Type, Deque and DefaultDict), however imported or aliased and unshadowed. It fires wherever an expression can appear, including store targets and `case` value patterns, on Python 3.9 and later. On 3.7-3.8 it reports every reference in stub files, and in other files only references inside annotations, when an import of `annotations` from `__future__` comes before them. Inside strings it reads exactly the strings Ruff reads as type definitions, through the annotation grammar: annotations, typing generic slices, cast/TypeVar/NewType/TypeAliasType/TypeForm/NamedTuple/TypedDict arguments, TypeAlias and `type` values, and nested strings. It skips Literal and Annotated metadata, including metadata nested in another generic or written as a parenthesized tuple. The span is the reference, or the whole string for a complex string. A reference in a string nested in an implicitly concatenated string is reported twice, as Ruff does. Divergences: legacy TypedDict keyword fields, a class TypedDict's extra_items keyword and an aliased TypeAlias marker are not read as types, and a complex string with several references is reported once.

### `ruff-up008-super-call-with-parameters`

Port of Ruff's UP008 (super-call-with-parameters): reports the argument list of a call to builtin `super` (or `builtins.super`, through parentheses) with exactly two positional arguments. The call must be made directly in a function or lambda, not in a comprehension, class body, default or annotation. The second argument, through parentheses, must name the callable's first positional-or-keyword parameter (positional-only ones are excluded). The first must be `__class__` or name the enclosing classes outermost first, to any depth (A.B.C.D), with no further enclosing class; functions between classes are skipped, as Ruff's scopes skip them. It skips a class name the callable binds itself (assignment, parameter, import, def, nonlocal, or a walrus in a comprehension) and a `__class__` the callable binds. It skips `builtins.super` unless the callable loads `super` or `__class__` in its body, parameters or decorators (for a lambda, only in its body). It skips classes decorated with dataclasses.dataclass(slots=True). Keyword arguments do not prevent a report.

### `ruff-up009-utf8-encoding-declaration`

Port of Ruff's UP009 (utf8-encoding-declaration): matches a comment that is a UTF-8 coding declaration (`coding:` or `coding=` followed by exactly `utf8` or `utf-8` in lower case, the comparison Ruff makes) and reports the comment. The comment must be in the file's leading comment block (no code or docstring before it), must be on the first or second line, and must be the first coding declaration of that block. Its own first `coding[:=]name` must be the UTF-8 one. The regex is the complement automaton of Ruff's lazy first-occurrence match, so `# coding=latin1 coding=utf8` is not reported while `# cocoa coding: utf-8` is. It is not reported when the next comment, or the comment after a plain comment, declares another encoding. Trailing comments after code are ignored. No known divergence.

### `ruff-up010-unnecessary-future-import`

Port of Ruff's UP010 (unnecessary-future-import): reports a whole `from __future__ import ...` statement when at least one of its unaliased names is an unused binding (no read, no `__all__` entry) in a module or function scope. The names checked are nested_scopes, generators, with_statement, division, absolute_import, print_function, unicode_literals and generator_stop. A relative `from .__future__ import ...` is reported the same way, because Ruff binds it as a future import and ignores the level. Imports in a class body are skipped, as Ruff skips them, and aliased names (`as`) are ignored. Ruff's target-version is assumed to be 3.7 or later (its default), and isort's required-imports setting (empty by default) is not modelled. Divergence: if the file also contains `from __future__ import *` (a Python syntax error), the engine marks the binding analysis as incomplete and nothing is reported.

### `ruff-up011-lru-cache-without-parameters`

Port of Ruff's UP011 (lru-cache-without-parameters): matches a decorator of a function (sync or async, at any nesting) that calls `functools.lru_cache` with no arguments at all. The callee is resolved through imports and aliases, and a shadowed name does not count. Comments and backslash continuations inside the parentheses are allowed. The reported span runs from the end of the callee to the end of the decorator expression: the empty parentheses, plus any closing parentheses of a parenthesized decorator or callee, skipping whitespace, comments and backslash continuations in between. Class decorators are not checked, as in Ruff. Ruff's target-version is assumed to be 3.8 or later (its default).

### `ruff-up012-unnecessary-encode-utf8`

Port of Ruff's UP012 (unnecessary-encode-utf8): Reports a call `.encode(...)` on a plain or f-string literal (parenthesized, implicitly concatenated, any `u`/raw prefix, not a bytes or t-string), including a parenthesized callee such as `("a".encode)()`, whose arguments are empty or a single UTF-8 name (`utf-8`, `utf8`, `utf_8`, `u8`, `utf`, `cp65001`, case-insensitive, compared on the string's value so escapes and implicit concatenation count) given positionally or as `encoding=`. An ASCII-only plain literal is reported for the call itself (use a bytes literal); a non-ASCII plain literal or any f-string is reported only when a UTF-8 argument is present (drop it). Non-raw literal parts containing `\N`, `\u`, `\U` or an octal escape above `\377` are skipped, as are calls with `errors`, extra arguments, starred or double-starred arguments, a generator argument, or a non-UTF-8 encoding. Calls inside string annotations are reported too. The finding spans the whole call.

### `ruff-up013-convert-typed-dict-functional-to-class`

Port of Ruff's UP013 (convert-typed-dict-functional-to-class): Reports a statement `Name = TypedDict(...)` (a single plain name target, parentheses allowed; chained, tuple, list, attribute, subscript, augmented and annotated targets are not reported) where `TypedDict` resolves to typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed (import aliases, module attribute access and shadowing respected). The call must be: one positional argument (any expression, starred or a generator included) with no `**` keywords, where any keywords are the fields; or exactly two positional arguments whose second, parenthesized or not, is a dict literal whose keys are all plain string literals naming a non-keyword identifier that does not start with `__` (no `**` entries; an empty dict is fine), or a call to the name `dict` with no `**` argument (its positional arguments are ignored, as in Ruff); any other keywords, `**` included, may follow the fields argument. No positional arguments, three or more, or any other fields argument is skipped. The finding spans the whole assignment statement; the message names the variable and the TypedDict reference as written.

### `ruff-up014-convert-named-tuple-functional-to-class`

Port of Ruff's UP014 (convert-named-tuple-functional-to-class): Reports a statement `Name = NamedTuple(...)` (a single plain name target, parentheses allowed; chained, tuple, list, annotated and attribute targets are not reported) where `NamedTuple` resolves to typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed (import aliases, module attribute access and shadowing respected) and the call is one positional argument alone (any expression, starred or a generator included), one positional argument with keyword fields and no `**`, or exactly two positional arguments and no keywords where the second is a list literal (parenthesized or not; an empty list is allowed) of `("identifier", annotation)` two-tuples whose name is a plain string literal naming a non-keyword, non-dunder identifier and whose annotation is not starred. Mixed fields and keywords, `**kwargs`, a tuple fields argument, three-element tuples, dunder field names, starred list entries and non-literal fields are skipped. `collections.namedtuple` is not reported. The finding spans the whole assignment statement, including when it is not at the start of a line; the message names the variable and the NamedTuple reference as written.

### `ruff-up015-redundant-open-modes`

Port of Ruff's UP015 (redundant-open-modes): Reports the mode argument (`mode=` if present, otherwise the second positional argument when no starred argument precedes it) of a call to the builtin `open` or `aiofiles.open`, resolved through imports, parentheses and shadowing, when it is a plain string literal (implicit concatenations and escapes decoded) made only of valid open-mode flags and contains `t`, `U`, or a bare `r` (no `b` or `+`) that can be dropped. A mode that is wholly redundant (`r`, `rt`, `U`, `rU`) and a mode with removable flags among others (`wt`, `Ub`) are reported on the mode string by separate clauses with different advice. String annotations are parsed as Ruff does; f-string modes, repeated or conflicting flags are skipped. A positional mode after a backslash line continuation placed before its comma, which the engine's argument binding misses, is matched structurally.

### `ruff-up017-datetime-timezone-utc`

Port of Ruff's UP017 (datetime-timezone-utc): Reports an expression that resolves through imports to `datetime.timezone.utc` (`datetime.timezone.utc`, `dt.timezone.utc` with `import datetime as dt`, `timezone.utc` after `from datetime import timezone [as tz]`, parenthesized forms, and the same inside f-strings, string annotations and match-case value and class patterns) when the project targets Python 3.11 or later. Shadowed or rebound names and look-alikes (`datetime.timezone.min`, `foo.timezone.utc`) are not reported. Only the attribute chain is reported, not an enclosing call or attribute access; in a case pattern with a longer dotted value (`case dt.timezone.utc.x`) only the `dt.timezone.utc` prefix is reported, as in Ruff.

### `ruff-up018-native-literals`

Port of Ruff's UP018 (native-literals): Reports a call to the builtin `str`, `bytes`, `int`, `float`, `bool` or `complex` (resolved through builtins imports, parentheses and shadowing) that has no arguments, or exactly one argument that is a literal of the same type: a plain (non f-string, non t-string) string for `str`, a bytes literal for `bytes`, an integer for `int`, a float for `float`, an imaginary number for `complex`, a boolean for `bool`, with an optional leading `+` or `-` for the numeric types, any of them parenthesized, and the redundant keywords `str(object=...)` and `complex(real=...)`. Calls inside string annotations (including nested ones and `typing.cast` strings) are reported on the call inside the string, as Ruff does. Calls nested two f-strings deep are skipped as in Ruff. The whole call is reported.

### `ruff-up019-typing-text-str-alias`

Port of Ruff's UP019 (typing-text-str-alias): matches any name or attribute expression that resolves through imports to `typing.Text` or `typing_extensions.Text` (in any load context, including `case` class patterns), plus the same names inside every string Ruff reads as a type definition (annotations, type alias values, typing generic slices such as `Optional["Text"]` at runtime, the first `Annotated` element, and the type arguments of `cast`, `TypeVar`, `NewType`, `TypeAliasType`, `NamedTuple`, `TypedDict` and similar), read recursively and never inside `Literal[...]`. It reports the alias expression itself. Diverges from Ruff where the engine does not read a string as a type definition: a parenthesized typing callee such as `(cast)("Text", 1)` and legacy `TypedDict("T", a="Text")` keyword fields.

### `ruff-up020-open-alias`

Port of Ruff's UP020 (open-alias): matches a call whose callee resolves through imports to `io.open` (including `from io import open`, aliased imports and a parenthesized callee) and reports the whole call, also inside strings Ruff reads as type definitions (for example `x: "io.open('f')"`, reported at the call's span within the string). A locally rebound `io` or `open` is not reported.

### `ruff-up021-replace-universal-newlines`

Port of Ruff's UP021 (replace-universal-newlines): matches a call whose callee resolves to `subprocess.run` that passes a `universal_newlines=` keyword and reports that keyword name; calls inside strings Ruff reads as type definitions count too. Only the first such keyword is reported, as in Ruff. One clause advises renaming it to `text`; another, used when a `text=` keyword is also present (a `text` key in a `**` dict does not count), advises removing it. Calls using `**kwargs` expansion are treated like any other call, as Ruff does.

### `ruff-up022-replace-stdout-stderr`

Port of Ruff's UP022 (replace-stdout-stderr): reports a call of subprocess.run (resolved through imports, aliases, from-imports and parentheses, not shadowed) whose first `stdout` and first `stderr` keyword arguments are both subprocess.PIPE, over the whole call; this includes calls written inside string annotations, reported at their position inside the string. Two clauses give different advice depending on whether capture_output is already passed. `**kwargs` never counts. Divergence: a call that is the only element of a star expression in a list or set display (or a for-loop iterable) is parsed with a different span by the grammar and is not reported.

### `ruff-up023-deprecated-c-element-tree`

Port of Ruff's UP023 (deprecated-c-element-tree): reports an aliased `import xml.etree.cElementTree as X` (the alias node, also inside a multi-name import), any `from xml.etree.cElementTree import ...` (the whole statement, including star and parenthesized forms), and an aliased `from xml.etree import cElementTree as X` (the alias node). Unaliased `import xml.etree.cElementTree`, unaliased `from xml.etree import cElementTree` and relative imports are not reported, as in Ruff. Whitespace and backslash line continuations between the dotted parts are tolerated. Divergence: a Python 3.15 `lazy from xml.etree.cElementTree import ...` statement is reported from `from`, not from `lazy`, because the grammar does not parse the `lazy` keyword.

### `ruff-up025-unicode-kind-prefix`

Port of Ruff's UP025 (unicode-kind-prefix): every string literal part written with a `u` or `U` prefix (a plain literal or one part of an implicit concatenation, f-string parts aside, inside f-string replacement fields too), one finding per part, reported on the part. As in Ruff, a u-string that is a whole annotation (of a variable, parameter or return, optionally parenthesized or implicitly concatenated) is not reported in a `.pyi` stub or in a module with `from __future__ import annotations`, where Ruff defers it as a string type definition; in stubs the value of a `TypeAlias` assignment is skipped as well. Strings nested inside an annotation (`list[u"int"]`) are still reported. Known gap: in stubs Ruff also defers other type-definition arguments such as the first argument of `cast` and a `TypeVar` bound, which this port still reports.

### `ruff-up026-deprecated-mock-import`

Port of Ruff's UP026 (deprecated-mock-import): reports `import mock` and `import mock.mock` (aliased or not, at the alias), every absolute `from mock import ...` statement, and each `mock.mock` that is the object of a further attribute (`mock.mock.X`, also `(mock).mock.X` and `(mock.mock).X`), in code and inside every string Ruff reads as a type definition (annotations, cast/TypeVar/NewType/TypeAliasType arguments, generic slices, nested strings; not Literal values or Annotated metadata). As in Ruff, `mock` is matched by text once any `mock` or `mock.*` import has been visited in Ruff's order: earlier at module level or in the same function, at module level anywhere for function bodies, in an earlier function at the same level, in an enclosing function body for nested functions, and anywhere in the file for lambda bodies, string type definitions, type alias values and annotations under `from __future__ import annotations`. Divergence: stub files, where Ruff also defers annotations and class bases, are read like .py files, and an import in a function nested two or more levels deep counts for later code only by source order.

### `ruff-up031-printf-string-formatting`

Port of Ruff's UP031 (printf-string-formatting): reports `"..." % right` where the left side is a plain str literal (single, implicitly concatenated or parenthesized; not bytes, f-string or t-string). Each implicitly concatenated segment is parsed separately in order: a segment that does not parse silently ends the check; the first segment with a conversion that cannot be rewritten (`%d %i %u %c`, `*` width or precision, an empty `%()` key, `%r`/`%a`/`%%` with flags, width or precision, `%s` with a width, or an unnamed placeholder when the right side is a dict) is reported at the string alone, whatever the right side.
