Otherwise the expression is reported whole when the right side is a literal, name, attribute, subscript, call, tuple, or a dict with at least one item whose keys are distinct identifier (non-keyword) str literals and, when the dict spans lines, with a string key that starts its line; any other dict is reported at the string; other right sides (list, unary or binary operation, comprehension, conditional) are silent. Strings read as type annotations are checked the same way, with the string as the left operand of `%`. Divergence: escapes that spell a percent or conversion character are read from the decoded text, not the source.

### `ruff-up033-lru-cache-with-maxsize-none`

Port of Ruff's UP033 (lru-cache-with-maxsize-none): flags a decorator of a function definition (not a class) that is a call to `functools.lru_cache`, resolved through imports and aliases, with no positional arguments and exactly one keyword argument, `maxsize=None` (the `None` may be parenthesized, the decorator and the callee may be wrapped in parentheses). The reported range runs from the end of the callee to the end of the decorator, as in Ruff, including a closing parenthesis of a parenthesized decorator, comments inside a parenthesized callee and a backslash continuation before the argument list. Only active when the target Python version is 3.9 or later. `lru_cache(maxsize=None, typed=True)`, `lru_cache(None)`, `**kwargs`, `*args`, local redefinitions and class decorators are left alone.

### `ruff-up034-extraneous-parentheses`

Port of Ruff's UP034 (extraneous-parentheses): Ruff scans tokens; this document reproduces its result on the syntax tree. It reports a parenthesized expression or generator (the inner pair, whose range is reported) that is the only content of an enclosing pair of parentheses (a parenthesized expression, a call or class argument list without a trailing comma, a parenthesized pattern, or the single positional argument of a class pattern), ignoring comments and line continuations. The inner group is skipped when it holds a comma, a `yield` or nothing at its own level (tuples, `()`, `(yield x)`, lambdas with several parameters, comprehensions over tuple targets). It also reproduces Ruff's token-scan consumption: an opening parenthesis is not an outer candidate when it lies inside a group an earlier scan read, which for a group that stopped at a comma or `yield` is only the part before that token (a lambda's first parameter, a comprehension body and the target before its first comma), and a scan nested inside an earlier one does not define its own stopping point; a call whose callee is a successfully scanned group has its argument parenthesis consumed, so the group after that parenthesis becomes an outer candidate (for example `((f)(((x))))` reports `(x)`, and `((a)((b)))` and `(((1)), 2)` report nothing). Reported on `.py` and `.pyi` alike.

### `ruff-up035-deprecated-import`

Port of Ruff's UP035 (deprecated-import): flags `from MODULE import ...` (absolute, not `*`) when MODULE is collections, pipes, mypy_extensions, typing_extensions, typing, typing.io, typing.re or backports.strenum (spaces around the dots allowed) and at least one imported name (aliased or not) has moved, reported over the whole statement. As in Ruff there is one finding per destination module (collections.abc, collections, re, shlex, typing, types, warnings, enum), so a statement can produce several findings with the same range, and one finding per imported name that was moved and renamed (typing List, Dict, Set, Tuple, Type, FrozenSet, AbstractSet, Deque, DefaultDict, ContextManager, AsyncContextManager) from Python 3.9, repeated when the name is imported twice. Version-dependent name lists are split by the same thresholds as Ruff (3.8 to 3.13) with exclusive clauses so each destination reports once; `when.pythonVersion` selects them. Not modeled: isort required-imports settings and `lazy from` imports (not parsed).

### `ruff-up036-outdated-version-block`

Port of Ruff's UP036 (outdated-version-block): matches an `if` or `elif` test that is a single comparison whose left side is `sys.version_info` (through any import form or alias, with or without one subscript such as `[:2]` or `[0]`, or `.major`) and whose right side is a tuple of integer literals compared with `<`, `<=`, `>` or `>=`, or a bare 2 or 3 compared with any of `==`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`. Integer literals in any base (`0x3`, `0b11`, `1_0`) count; negative numbers, booleans, floats and complex literals do not. It reports the comparison when it is always true or always false for the minimum supported Python version, which is a document option (`python-minor-version`, default 10 for Ruff's default target of 3.10) instead of Ruff's `target-version`. A second clause reports an out-of-range component (above 255) at the tuple or integer, without any redundant parentheses around it, as Ruff's range does. Like Ruff, it stops reading an if/elif chain after a branch whose right side is another integer (or 2 or 3 with `is`/`in`), or a tuple holding a non-integer under an ordering operator. Parentheses around the test, the left side and the right side are transparent. Divergence: none known; probes verified against live Ruff at targets 3.8, 3.10 and 3.13 (option set to match).

### `ruff-up037-quoted-annotation`

Port of Ruff's UP037 (quoted-annotation): reports a string (or implicit concatenation) that Ruff reads as a type definition and that parses as an expression, when it sits in an annotation that is never evaluated at runtime: any annotation (parameter, return, annotated assignment) after a `from __future__ import annotations` (in any form, including parenthesized, aliased or with other features), any annotation in a `.pyi` stub or at target 3.14 and later, and an annotated assignment inside a function body otherwise (not in a class body nested in a function, and not at module level even under `if TYPE_CHECKING:`). Strings inside `Literal[...]`, `Annotated` metadata, bytes, f-strings and unparsable strings are skipped; parameter (non-variadic) and return annotations of `singledispatch`/`singledispatchmethod` implementations registered with a bare `@fun.register` are skipped (their annotations are read at runtime), and quoted types nested inside a quoted annotation are reported too, each at its own span. Keyword values of a functional `TypedDict` call follow Ruff: `extra_items` is always a type, `total` never, and any other keyword only before Python 3.13 and only when the call passes just the name or the name and `None` positionally; where the annotation grammar does not parse such a value, it is accepted by a charset check that also rejects two adjacent words and dangling brackets or separators. The `lint.future-annotations` preview behaviour is not modelled. Divergences: the three cases listed under the partial reason.

### `ruff-up039-unnecessary-class-parentheses`

Port of Ruff's UP039 (unnecessary-class-parentheses): a class definition whose base-class parentheses hold no positional, starred, keyword or `**` argument (only comments or line continuations, if anything), as in `class A():`. Reported on the parentheses (the argument list, from `(` to `)`).

### `ruff-up040-non-pep695-type-alias`

Port of Ruff's UP040 (non-pep695-type-alias): reports `NAME: TypeAlias = value` (TypeAlias from typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed, through any import form, annotation possibly parenthesized) with a plain name target, and `NAME = TypeAliasType("NAME", value[, type_params=(T, ...)])` with exactly those arguments, a single assignment target (chained assignments are skipped), a string that equals the target, and a tuple of names (parentheses around a name allowed) for `type_params`. The annotation form is skipped when a name loaded anywhere in the value resolves to a plain assignment of TypeVar/TypeVarTuple/ParamSpec whose first argument is a plain string literal (not an f-string or bytes) and that has a `default=` argument, or is a TypeVarTuple or ParamSpec with a `bound=` or a second positional argument (a `**` splat is not a constraint); the TypeAliasType form applies the same test only to the names in `type_params`. Only fires at target 3.12 and later. The preview-only handling of type variable defaults at 3.13 is not modelled. Divergence: the lambda/comprehension case in the partial reason.

### `ruff-up041-timeout-error-alias`

Port of Ruff's UP041 (timeout-error-alias), which runs at a target version of 3.10 or later: an alias of `TimeoutError` (a name or attribute resolving to `socket.timeout` and, from 3.11, `asyncio.TimeoutError`) used as an `except` clause's exception type, as the exception a `raise` raises (not its `from` cause), or as a called function, parentheses transparent; reported on the name or attribute. An `except` clause whose type is a tuple with such an element (parentheses transparent) is reported once, on the tuple.

### `ruff-up042-replace-str-enum`

Port of Ruff's UP042 (replace-str-enum): reports the name of a class (not the whole definition) whose argument-list bases include both the builtin `str` and `enum.Enum`, in any order and with other bases or keyword arguments (such as `metaclass=`) alongside; names resolve through imports and aliases (`import enum as e`, `from enum import Enum as E`, `builtins.str`) and a shadowed `str` or a different `Enum` is ignored. Parenthesized bases count; starred bases (`*bases`) and `IntEnum` or `Flag` do not. Runs only when the target Python version is 3.11 or later, in stubs too, as Ruff does. Same as Ruff, except that Ruff's fix is not offered and a class inside a function that declares `global str` (with no module-level `str` binding) is not reported.

### `ruff-up044-non-pep646-unpack`

Port of Ruff's UP044 (non-pep646-unpack): on Python 3.11 and later, reports `Unpack[X]` (from typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed, through any alias) when X is a single name, attribute or subscript (not a tuple, union, constant, call or boolean/comparison expression) and the subscript is either an element of another subscript's index (alone or as a direct element of the index tuple, parentheses transparent) or the annotation of a `*args` parameter; `**kwargs`, ordinary parameters, return annotations, assignments, calls and PEP 695 type-parameter bounds and defaults are left alone. The reported span is the whole `Unpack[...]` expression, inside string annotations too (but not a string that is itself the `*args` annotation, as in Ruff). Divergence: string annotations written as implicit concatenations or with escape sequences are not read.

### `ruff-up045-non-pep604-annotation-optional`

Port of Ruff's UP045 (non-pep604-annotation-optional): reports each `Optional[X]` subscript (typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed, through `import` aliases, from-imports and `(Optional)[X]`), anchored on the whole subscript. It follows Ruff's version gates. At 3.10 and later it reports everywhere, including runtime expressions. In stubs it reports at every version. At 3.7 to 3.9 it reports only after `from __future__ import annotations`, in annotations and string type definitions. A quoted argument (`Optional["A"]`) is reported only in a typing context: a TYPE_CHECKING block, a function-local variable annotation, a PEP 695 type alias value or type parameter bound or default, an annotation after the future import, any annotation in a stub, and any annotation from 3.14 (deferred annotations). Inside string type definitions (annotations, type alias values, `List["..."]` and other typing generic slices, `cast`, `TypeVar` bounds and constraints, `NewType`, `TypeAliasType`, functional `NamedTuple` and `TypedDict` fields, but not `Literal` or `Annotated` metadata), it always reports. It skips `Optional[NamedTuple]` and starred slices. Divergence: a file whose annotation contains a comparison (`x: Optional[str] is True`) is not evaluated, because the grammar rejects it.

### `ruff-up046-non-pep695-generic-class`

Port of Ruff's UP046 (non-pep695-generic-class), applied from target 3.12: reports the first positional `Generic[...]` base (typing, typing_extensions or _typeshed, through aliases and parentheses) of a class that has no type parameters and is not nested in a class or function, anchored on the subscript. When Generic is the last positional base, it reports only if a name in the slice does not resolve to a TypeVar, TypeVarTuple or ParamSpec assignment (`X = TypeVar("X", ...)` with a string first argument, or `TypeVar[...]`), or if every resolved variable is usable. A variable is not usable when it has a `default=`, when a TypeVarTuple or ParamSpec has a bound or constraints, when a name repeats (AnyStr in any spelling included), or when the slice holds no variables (`Generic["T"]`). `AnyStr` counts as a variable unless `str` or `bytes` is rebound at module level before the class. A Generic that is not last is always reported.

### `ruff-up047-non-pep695-generic-function`

Port of Ruff's UP047 (non-pep695-generic-function), applied from target 3.12. It reports a module-level function (not nested in a class or function, with no type parameters) when a parameter annotation (not the return annotation) references a TypeVar, TypeVarTuple or ParamSpec assignment, or `AnyStr` while `str` and `bytes` are not rebound at module level before it. It does not report when any referenced variable has `default=`, or when a referenced TypeVarTuple or ParamSpec has a bound or constraints. Unresolvable names are ignored. The span runs from the function name to the closing parenthesis of its parameter list. That parenthesis is found by a balanced match to four levels of nesting, skipping strings and comments, then by the first `)` followed by `:` or `->`. Divergence: at pythonVersion 3.14 and later, TypeVars are resolved with deferred-annotation semantics (the binding the module leaves), where Ruff uses the binding visible at the def.

### `ruff-up049-private-type-parameter`

Port of Ruff's UP049 (private-type-parameter): Reports the declaration of a PEP 695 type parameter (plain, bounded, constrained, defaulted, starred or double-starred) on a generic `class` or `def`/`async def` whose name starts with an underscore and does not end with one, so sunder, dunder and all-underscore names are skipped. The finding spans the parameter name only, not its uses, and underscored names inside bounds, constraints or defaults are not reported. Type parameters of `type` alias statements are not reported, matching Ruff. It is reported whether or not Ruff would offer a fix; the message tells the agent to remove all leading underscores and to pick another name when the bare name would shadow a variable or builtin or is not a valid identifier.

### `ruff-up050-useless-class-metaclass-type`

Port of Ruff's UP050 (useless-class-metaclass-type): a `metaclass=` keyword argument in a class definition's base-class list whose value (parentheses transparent) resolves to the builtin `type` (Ruff's `match_builtin_expr`: the unshadowed name `type`, or `type` of the `builtins` module through the file's imports). Reported on the keyword argument, from `metaclass` to the end of its value; every such keyword of a class is reported.

### `ruff-ytt101-sys-version-slice3`

Port of Ruff's YTT101 (sys-version-slice3): reports a reference to `sys.version` (found by qualified-name resolution through imports and aliases, so shadowed or rebound names are ignored) that is subscripted by exactly the slice `[:3]` (no lower bound, no step or an empty step as in `[:3:]`, integer literal 3 in any base, parentheses allowed, not a tuple index), at the range of `sys.version`, including inside string annotations and in any expression context (loads, stores, deletes). A one-element starred list or set such as `[*sys.version[:3]]`, which the Python grammar parses with the star inside the attribute, is matched separately and reported from after the star, as Ruff reports it.

### `ruff-ytt102-sys-version2`

Port of Ruff's YTT102 (sys-version2): reports a reference to `sys.version` (resolved through imports and aliases, so shadowed or rebound names are ignored) subscripted by exactly the integer literal 2 (any base, parentheses allowed, not a tuple index, not a float), at the range of `sys.version`, including inside string annotations and in any expression context (loads, stores, deletes). A one-element starred list or set such as `[*sys.version[2]]`, which the Python grammar parses with the star inside the attribute, is matched separately and reported from after the star, as Ruff reports it.

### `ruff-ytt103-sys-version-cmp-str3`

Port of Ruff's YTT103 (sys-version-cmp-str3): reports `sys.version` (resolved through imports and aliases, parentheses allowed) as the left operand of a single `<`, `<=`, `>` or `>=` comparison whose right operand is a plain string literal (also implicit concatenation, any non-f/t prefix including raw and unicode) whose decoded UTF-8 length is not exactly one byte, including the empty string and a single non-ASCII character; the one-byte case belongs to the sibling rule for single-character strings. Like Ruff, a raw CRLF line break inside a string counts as two bytes (a backslash-escaped one counts as none), so `"""<CRLF>"""` is reported. Chained comparisons, bytes, f-strings, t-strings, `==`, `in` and `is` are ignored, as in Ruff. Reported at the range of `sys.version`, also inside string annotations.

### `ruff-ytt201-sys-version-info0-eq3`

Port of Ruff's YTT201 (sys-version-info0-eq3): reports `sys.version_info[0]` (the sole index, integer literal 0 in any base, parentheses allowed, `sys.version_info` resolved through imports and aliases) as the left operand of a single `==` or `!=` comparison against the integer literal 3 (any base, parentheses allowed), at the range of the subscript, also inside string annotations and f-strings. Two clauses give different advice (`>=` for equality, `<` for inequality), matching Ruff's two message variants. Chained comparisons, reversed operands, signed or complex numbers, other numbers and other operators are ignored, as in Ruff.

### `ruff-ytt202-six-py3`

Port of Ruff's YTT202 (six-py3): reports every name or attribute expression that resolves through imports to `six.PY3` (`six.PY3`, `import six as s; s.PY3`, `from six import PY3 [as x]`), in any context, at the range of the expression, also inside string annotations; shadowed or rebound names, keyword-argument and parameter names, capture patterns and `six.moves` paths are ignored. Value patterns in `match` statements (`case six.PY3:`, also nested in sequence, mapping and class patterns, and as the head of a longer dotted name) are reported at the `six.PY3` part, as Ruff reports the attribute it visits there. A one-element starred list or set such as `[*six.PY3]`, which the Python grammar parses with the star inside the attribute, is matched separately and reported from after the star.

### `ruff-ytt203-sys-version-info1-cmp-int`

Port of Ruff's YTT203 (sys-version-info1-cmp-int): matches `sys.version_info[1]` (sys resolved through imports, aliases and from-imports; shadowing, class-scope and except-handler scoping and bare annotations handled as Ruff does) when it is the left operand of a single-operator comparison using <, <=, > or >= against an integer literal (parentheses allowed, any base), with the index the integer literal 1; it also checks string annotations, and reports just the `sys.version_info[1]` operand. Chained comparisons are skipped, as in Ruff. One engine-level divergence: inside a multi-line triple-quoted string annotation in a file with CRLF line endings, the finding spans the whole string rather than the inner node.

### `ruff-ytt204-sys-version-info-minor-cmp-int`

Port of Ruff's YTT204 (sys-version-info-minor-cmp-int): matches `sys.version_info.minor` (sys.version_info resolved through imports, aliases and from-imports, with Ruff's scoping) as the left operand of a single-operator <, <=, > or >= comparison against an integer literal, including inside string annotations, and reports just the left operand. Chained comparisons are skipped, as in Ruff. One engine-level divergence: inside a multi-line triple-quoted string annotation in a file with CRLF line endings, the finding spans the whole string rather than the inner node.

### `ruff-ytt301-sys-version0`

Port of Ruff's YTT301 (sys-version0): matches `sys.version[0]` in any context (load, store, del, annotations, string annotations) where `sys.version` resolves through imports (aliases and from-imports included, shadowing respected) and the index is the integer literal 0 in any base (parenthesised too, not a tuple); reports only the `sys.version` value, as Ruff does. One engine-level divergence: inside a multi-line triple-quoted string annotation in a file with CRLF line endings, the finding spans the whole string rather than the inner node.

### `ruff-ytt302-sys-version-cmp-str10`

Port of Ruff's YTT302 (sys-version-cmp-str10): matches `sys.version` (resolved through imports) as the left operand of a single-operator <, <=, > or >= comparison against a plain (non-bytes, non-f/t) string literal, implicit concatenation included, whose value is exactly one UTF-8 byte; reports just the left operand. Like Ruff, a raw CRLF line break inside a string counts as two bytes (a backslash-escaped one counts as none), so `"""<CRLF>"""` is not matched. Longer strings are Ruff's separate YTT103 and not matched here. One engine-level divergence: inside a multi-line triple-quoted string annotation in a file with CRLF line endings, the finding spans the whole string rather than the inner node.

### `ruff-ytt303-sys-version-slice1`

Port of Ruff's YTT303 (sys-version-slice1): matches `sys.version[:1]` in any context where `sys.version` resolves through imports and the slice has no lower bound, an upper bound of the integer literal 1 (any base, parenthesised too) and no step (`[:1:]` counts, `[:1:None]` and `[::1]` do not); reports only the `sys.version` value, as Ruff does. One engine-level divergence: inside a multi-line triple-quoted string annotation in a file with CRLF line endings, the finding spans the whole string rather than the inner node.

### `ruff-isc001-single-line-implicit-string-concatenation`

Port of Ruff's ISC001 (single-line-implicit-string-concatenation): reports each pair of adjacent string literals (plain, bytes, f-string or t-string, any prefix, single- or triple-quoted) separated only by spaces, tabs or form feeds, over the span from the first literal's start to the second's end, as Ruff does. A run of n literals gives up to n-1 overlapping pairs. One regex over the concatenated string's text walks literals, gaps and comments pair by pair, with one violation per match (each). One clause starts at the first literal and the other at the second, so together they reach every pair. A pair is not reported if a line break separates the two literals (backslash continuation or a newline inside brackets, with or without comments), or if one literal is a t-string and the other is not. An empty literal followed directly by the same quote is read as the start of a triple-quoted literal. Divergences: files that do not parse are not checked, and neither are the syntax-error regions of a file that partly parses. Ruff's lexer turns an unterminated string into a string token and pairs it with its neighbour; its ISC_syntax_error fixtures test exactly that. An f-string whose replacement fields nest braces four or more levels deep ends the walk of its run, so later pairs in that run are not reported.
